A Simple and Efficient One-Shot Signature Scheme
Andrew Huang, Vinod Vaikuntanathan

Abstract
One-shot signatures (OSS) are a powerful and uniquely quantum cryptographic primitive which allows anyone, given common reference string, to come up with a public verification key 𝗉𝗄 \mathsf{pk} and a secret signing state | 𝗌𝗄 ⟩ \ket{\mathsf{sk}} . With the secret signing state, one can produce the signature of any one message, but no more. In a recent breakthrough work, Shmueli and Zhandry (CRYPTO 2025) constructed one-shot signatures, either unconditionally in a classical oracle model or assuming post-quantum indistinguishability obfuscation and the hardness of Learning with Errors (LWE) in the plain model. In this work, we address the inefficiency of the Shmueli-Zhandry construction which signs messages bit-by-bit, resulting in signing keys of Θ ⁡ ( λ 4 ) \Theta(\lambda^{4}) qubits and signatures of size Θ ⁡ ( λ 3 ) \Theta(\lambda^{3}) bits for polynomially long messages, where λ \lambda is the security parameter. We construct a new, simple, direct, and efficient one-shot signature scheme which can sign messages of any polynomial length using signing keys of Θ ⁡ ( λ 2 ) \Theta(\lambda^{2}) qubits and signatures of size Θ ⁡ ( λ 2 ) \Theta(\lambda^{2}) bits. We achieve corresponding savings in runtimes, in both the oracle model and the plain model. In addition, unlike the Shmueli-Zhandry construction, our scheme achieves perfect correctness. Our scheme also achieves strong signature incompressibility, which implies a public-key quantum fire scheme with perfect correctness among other applications, correcting an error in a recent work of Çakan, Goyal and Shmueli (QCrypt 2025) and recovering their applications.



1
Introduction

One-shot signatures (OSS), first defined in the work of Amos, Georgiou, Kiayias and Zhandry
[
AGKZ20
]
, are a uniquely quantum form of digital signatures
[
GMR88
]
where, given some public parameters, anyone can sample a classical verification key together with a quantum signing key. The signing key allows anyone to produce the signature, a classical bit-string, of any message of their choice, but only one. In particular, the signing key self-destructs once a single signature is produced. Security requires that given the public parameter, no quantum polynomial-time algorithm can produce valid signatures for two different messages. (One could strengthen this definition to allow the adversary to produce a different signature of the same message; for more discussion, see Section
2
.)

One-shot signatures are very powerful: they immediately imply a number of exotic quantum cryptographic primitives, including quantum money, quantum lightning, non-collapsing hash functions, non-interactive and publicly verifiable proofs of quantumness and certified min-entropy, and more.

In a recent breakthrough work, Shmueli and Zhandry
[
SZ25
]
gave the first construction of one-shot signatures. In a classical oracle model, where all algorithms have oracle access to a (classical) function, their construction is unconditionally secure. In the plain model with no oracles, they show a construction assuming post-quantum secure indistinguishability obfuscation (pqIO), one-way functions (pqOWF), lossy functions (pqLF), and (essentially) a noisy trapdoor claw-free function (pqNTCF), the latter three of which can be instantiated assuming the post-quantum subexponential learning with errors (LWE) assumption.

Shmueli and Zhandry
[
SZ25
]
in fact construct a related primitive called a
non-collapsing hash function
, a notion that originates from the works of Ambainis, Rossmanis and Unruh
[
ARU14
,
Unr16
]
, and use generic reductions from
[
AGKZ20
,
DS22
]
to boost this to a one-shot signature scheme. Unfortunately, this path leads to a large polynomial factor blowup in the sizes of the signing state and the signature itself, and the runtimes of the associated algorithms. For example, as-is, the one-shot signature scheme from
[
SZ25
]
has signing keys with
\Theta(\lambda^{12})
qubits, public keys with
\Theta(\lambda^{12})
bits, and signatures with
\Theta(\lambda^{3})
bits, where
\lambda
is the security parameter
1
1

1

As is standard in cryptography, this means that we demand security against
2^{\lambda}
-time adversaries that attempt to succeed with better than
2^{-\lambda}
advantage.
. With an improved analysis of their non-collapsing hash function (see Appendix
G
) and opening up the
[
DS22
]
reduction, the signing and public keys can be reduced to
\Theta(\lambda^{4})
qubits and
\Theta(\lambda^{3})
bits, respectively.

Still, several inefficiencies remain. For one, the
[
SZ25
]
construction is seemingly inherently restricted to signing one bit at a time. Secondly, the signing algorithm seems to require a Marriott-Watrous-style
[
MW04
]
alternating projections algorithm.
2
2

2

The
[
DS22
]
reduction implements a more complicated quantum rewinding/quantum singular value transform (QSVT) circuit, but at its core, still involves an alternating projections algorithm.
The first of these issues causes a
\lambda
factor blowup in the signing key size and the public-key and signature size; the second causes a further
\lambda
factor blowup in the signing key size.

In this work, we construct a new one-shot signature scheme which is:

•

Efficient:
Our scheme signs polynomially many bits in one shot. In the classical oracle model, the signing keys have size
O(\lambda^{2})
qubits, and the public keys and signatures have size
O(\lambda^{2})
bits. Signing and verification also requires significantly fewer oracle queries. We achieve analogous savings in the plain model; for more details, see Table
1
.

•

Simple and Direct:
The algorithms are simply described and, for example, avoid complex Marriott-Watrous/QSVT-style circuits.

•

Perfectly Correct:
The OSS scheme of
[
SZ25
]
has a negligible correctness error, again seemingly inherently so, in the sense that the signing algorithm may fail to produce a valid signature. Our scheme, in contrast, enjoys perfect correctness.

•

Incompressible:
A simple modification of our scheme in the oracle model satisfies an incompressibility property, first defined in
[
ÇGS25
]
, which requires that it is impossible to telegraph a classical bit string of any polynomial length which communicates information about too many valid signatures. This in turn leads us to the construction of a quantum fire
[
BNZ25
]
and quantum key-fire
[
ÇGS25
]
scheme, both with the added advantage of perfect correctness.
In the process, we identify a gap in the work of
[
ÇGS25
]
who claimed that the
\lambda
-bit OSS scheme from
[
SZ25
]
satisfies incompressibility; indeed, we show that this scheme, a parallel repetition of a single-bit OSS scheme, cannot be incompressible. We fix this bug by showing that our multi-bit OSS scheme is indeed incompressible, recovering the claim of
[
ÇGS25
]
, albeit for our OSS scheme. (For more details, we refer the reader to Section
2.2
.)

Figure 1:
OSS parameters for signing polynomially long messages. By standard collision-resistant hashing, it suffices to sign
\lambda
-bit messages. The public-key size in the plain model
[
SZ25
]
construction consists of
O(\lambda)
repetitions of a base public key of
O(\lambda^{4})
bits, whereas our scheme has a single one.

Our results can be summarized by the following theorems:

Theorem 1.1

(Informal)
.

There exist one-shot signature schemes for polynomially long messages with perfect correctness and strong unforgeability with the following efficiency properties:

1.

In the classical oracle model, unconditionally: the scheme achieves signing key size
O(\lambda^{2})
qubits; public-key and signature size
O(\lambda^{2})
bits; key generation and verification algorithms make a constant number of oracle calls, and signing makes
O(\lambda)
oracle calls.

2.

In the plain model, assuming the existence of post-quantum secure indistinguishability obfuscation (pqIO) and the post-quantum subexponential learning with errors (LWE) assumption: the scheme achieves signing key size
O(\lambda^{2})
qubits; public-key size
O(\lambda^{4})
bits; and signature size
O(\lambda^{2})
bits.

We do not attempt to optimize the runtimes of the algorithms in the plain model construction as the limiting factor there is the use of an expensive indistinguishability obfuscation scheme. Still, we note that the signing and verification algorithms are conceptually simpler than those of
[
SZ25
]
.

Theorem 1.2

(Informal)
.

There exists an incompressible OSS scheme for
\lambda
-bit messages with perfect correctness, strong signature security and incompressibility. As a corollary of this and
[
ÇGS25
]
, there exists:

•

a public-key quantum fire (and key-fire) scheme with perfect verification correctness, perfect strong cloning correctness, and untelegraphability security;

•

a public-key quantum key-fire scheme for any classically-unlearnable functionality with perfect cloning correctness, perfect evaluation correctness, and key-untelegraphability security; and

•

a public-key encryption scheme with perfect correctness, perfectly clonable yet unbounded-leakage-resilient secret keys, and classical public keys and ciphertexts.

Moreover, assuming the existence of post-quantum one-way functions, all of these schemes can be made efficient (with computational security).

2
Technical Overview

We start by reviewing the one-shot signature (OSS) scheme of Shmueli and Zhandry
[
SZ25
]
(SZ, henceforth), taking care to point out the sources of inefficiency along the way. We then describe our ideas to improve the bandwidth and runtimes of the algorithms.

The SZ work first constructs a primitive called a
non-collapsing collision-resistant hash function

[
ARU14
,
Unr16
,
SZ25
]
and invokes the general results of
[
DS22
,
AGKZ20
]
to turn it into a one-shot signature scheme. A non-collapsing hash function is one where the public parameters
\mathsf{CRS}
define a function
H(\mathsf{CRS},\cdot)
. The novel and inherently quantum property of this hash function is that there should exist a QPT state sampler that, given
\mathsf{CRS}
, generates a state
\ket{\psi}
and an auxiliary state
\mathsf{aux}
; and a QPT distinguisher (that we will call the collapsing distinguisher) that distinguishes between the following two experiments. In the first experiment, the challenger measures
\ket{\psi}
and returns the result to the adversary; in the second, the challenger computes
H(\mathsf{CRS},\cdot)
on
\ket{\psi}
in superposition and measures only the result. In addition, the hash function is collision-resistant in the standard cryptographic sense against quantum polynomial-time adversaries.

To construct such a hash function, SZ uses a bijection (or permutation)
\Pi:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
where
n=\Theta(\lambda^{2})
, as well as a family of subspaces and cosets
\{(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})\}_{\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}
where
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
. With these in place, SZ defines

H(x):=\Pi(x)_{[1:r]}~,

namely the hash function simply outputs the first
r
bits of
\Pi(x)
. SZ also defines the following auxiliary functions, which are treated as oracles in the construction and the proof:

•

\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x})=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
where
\Pi(x)=\mathbf{y}||\mathbf{w}
: the function
\mathcal{P}
outputs the first
r
bits of
\Pi(x)
as-is, and outputs the last
n-r
bits encoded into the affine subspace indexed by
\mathbf{y}
.

•

\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u})=\begin{cases}\Pi^{-1}(\mathbf{y}||\mathbf{w})&\mbox{if }\exists\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}\text{ such that }\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{u}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

•

\mathcal{D}
is the function that checks if a given vector
\mathbf{v}
belongs to the dual of the subspace indexed by
\mathbf{y}
. That is,

\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}=0^{n-r}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Given oracle access to
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{D}
, the construction of the collapsing distinguisher is straightforward: the state sampler produces the uniform superposition state
\ket{\psi}=\sum_{x}\ket{x}
(no auxiliary information is necessary). In the first experiment, the adversary will have access to the basis state
\ket{x}
for some
x
, while in the second experiment, the adversary holds
\ket*{\psi_\vecy}:=\sum_{x:H(x)=\mathbf{y}}\ket{x}
for some
\mathbf{y}
. Distinguishing between the two states is straightforward: one can apply
\mathcal{P}
in the forward direction (and use
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
to uncompute
x
) to get either

\ket{\vecy,\vecz}\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{or}\hskip 14.45377pt\ket{\vecy}\otimes\sum_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}}\ket*{\matA_\vecy\vecw+ \vecb_\vecy}

where
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}
is just some string. Doing a Hadamard transform on the second register results in either
a full superposition over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
(with phases depending on
\mathbf{z}
) or a uniform superposition over the dual subspace of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
(again, with phases depending on
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
). Now, applying
\mathcal{D}
in superposition on
\mathbf{y}
together with the second register results in
0
in the first case with overwhelming probability, and
1
in the second case with probability
1
, distinguishing between the two cases.

Demonstrating collision-resistance of
H
is significantly more difficult: we do not go into detail about the proof and note only that the proof of collision resistance of our new scheme closely follows the proof strategy of
[
SZ25
]
. We now explain how
[
DS22
]
and
[
AGKZ20
]
produce a one-shot signature scheme from a non-collapsing and collision-resistant hash function.

The template of the OSS construction, essentially from
[
AGKZ20
]
, is to let the public key of the OSS scheme be an element
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r}
in the image of the hash function
H
. The signature of a bit
b
with respect to the public key
\mathbf{y}
is
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that
H(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{x}[1]=b
, i.e. a hash preimage of
\mathbf{y}
whose first bit is
b
. All one needs now to sign a bit
b
is to produce a hash preimage of
\mathbf{y}
that starts with the bit
b
; this is where one needs an equivocator for the hash function. The key innovation is a way to use the collapsing distinguisher, together with the pre-image state
\ket*{\psi_\vecy}
and the oracles, to build an equivocator; this is done using the reduction of Dall’Agnol and Spooner
[
DS22
]
.

While the
[
DS22
]
reduction from equivocating to distinguishing only considers non-collapsing commitment schemes, we outline how a similar reduction might work for non-collapsing and collision-resistant hash functions.
3
3

3

Technically speaking, the
[
SZ25
]
distinguisher is not actually enough for the
[
DS22
]
reduction to go through. There is an easy fix to this, as we explain in Section
2.3
.
The first step of the
[
DS22
]
reduction is to use the collapsing distinguisher for the hash function to construct an algorithm that breaks sum binding, that is, an adversary that can produce preimages of a hash that begin with either 0 or 1 on demand. The sum of the probabilities
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
that it succeeds in producing preimages that start with
0
and
1
respectively, should be non-negligibly larger than
1
. This may only succeed with a small, but non-negligible, probability. Therefore, the second step of
[
DS22
]
is an amplification step which uses the sum binding algorithm in parallel on many states
\ket*{\psi_{\vecy_i}}
.

The first step of
[
DS22
]
, the reduction from a collapsing distinguisher
D
(which we will think of as a projective measurement) to sum-binding algorithm
S
, works roughly as follows:
S
will measure the first bit of the preimage
x
. Upon being asked to open to bit
b
, if the measured bit is
b
, the algorithm is all set: it can measure all of
x
. Otherwise, it will try to use the projective measurement
(D,I-D)
before measuring
x
. The hope is that when
x_{1}\neq b
, the collapsing distinguisher
D
will allow
S
to project the partially measured state back to a subspace with non-trivial weight on preimages with
x_{1}=b
. This reduction is mildly lossy: if
D
distinguishes with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
, then
S
succeeds with probability roughly
\frac{1}{2}+O(\varepsilon^{2})
.

At this stage, one needs to amplify the sum-binding algorithm
S
into an equivocator
E
, which must succeed with overwhelming probability. A naïve attempt might be as follows: after measuring a hash value,
E
may try to equivocate by running an alternating projectors/Marriott-Watrous style rewinding algorithm using
S
. Unfortunately, for technical reasons that we will explain in more detail in Section
2.3
, this requires multiple copies of the state. Roughly speaking, with only one copy, we may get unlucky and require a super-polynomial amount of time to equivocate.

Dall’Agnol and Spooner
[
DS22
]
deal with this issue by using many preimage states
\ket*{\psi_{\vecy_i}}
and a QSVT/quantum rewinding circuit to produce an equivocator. This step introduces a fair bit of overhead; in particular, if
S
succeeds with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
, equivocating to a single bit with probability
1-2^{-\lambda}
requires roughly
\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}
preimage states, and the same number of hash functions. Overall, the
[
DS22
]
reduction will produce an equivocator with
\Theta(\lambda)
overhead in signing.
4
4

4

One could move this overhead into the key generation algorithm, but ultimately either the key generation algorithm or signing algorithm will incur
\Theta(\lambda)
overhead.

Finally, to produce a one-shot signature scheme for
\lambda
-bit messages, one needs to repeat this scheme in parallel, which introduces another
\lambda
overhead. This again seems unavoidable with the
[
SZ25
]
construction as we explain in Section
2.3
.

Thus, even with a collapsing disguisher that succeeds with probability a constant larger than
1/2
, the signature scheme that results from
[
SZ25
]
ends up with
\Theta(\lambda)\cdot\lambda\cdot\Theta(\lambda^{2})=\Theta(\lambda^{4})
qubit signing keys,
\lambda\cdot\Theta(\lambda^{2})=\Theta(\lambda^{3})
-bit signatures, and
\lambda\cdot\Theta(\lambda^{2})=\Theta(\lambda^{3})
-bit public keys. The runtime of the signing algorithm will likely also require close to
\Omega(\lambda^{4})
queries, where an
\Omega(\lambda^{2})
overhead comes from the QSVT circuit, to the underlying programs/oracles
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{D}
, while the key generation and verification algorithms will require
\Theta(\lambda)
queries.

2.1
Our Construction

As noted earlier,
[
SZ25
]
technically only provides a distinguisher for the complete measurement of the preimage state, which is not enough to apply the
[
DS22
]
reduction. Fortunately, in Appendix
G
, we observe that their distinguisher has a constant advantage on a known index: the first bit. Using this fact allows us to break sum binding with probability
\frac{1}{2}+c
for some constant
c>0
, thus allowing the
[
DS22
]
reduction to go through (see Section
2.3
for more details). However, the final
\Theta(\lambda)
overhead of the
[
DS22
]
reduction seems unavoidable without significant modification. Additionally, there is no clear way to boost the
[
SZ25
]
scheme to one which has perfect correctness, since the collapsing distinguisher already begins with imperfect advantage.

Our construction avoids the
[
DS22
]
and
[
AGKZ20
]
reductions altogether by constructing a one-shot signature scheme in one go. Our main idea is simple: instead of signing a bit
b
by looking for it in the hash preimage, we will look for
b
in the
image
. That is to say, to sign a bit
b
with respect to a public key
\mathbf{y}
, we will produce a vector
\vec{\sigma}
such that
\vec{\sigma}[1]=b
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x})=(\mathbf{y},\vec{\sigma})
for some
\mathbf{x}
.

To see that this new scheme has an equivocator, we rely on intuition from
[
AGKZ20
]
: we can easily implement a reflection oracle across valid coset vectors
\vec{\sigma}
such that
\vec{\sigma}[1]=b
, while the dual oracle/program
\mathcal{D}
(essentially) provides a reflection oracle across the state

\ket*{\psi_{\vecy}}:=\sum_{\mathbf{x}:H(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{y}}\ket*{\matA_\vecy\cdot\vecw_{\vecx} + \vecb_\vecy}~.

where
\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{x}}
is the last
n-r
bits of
\Pi(\mathbf{x})
. Note that
\ket*{\psi_\vecy}
is the uniform superposition over the affine subspace
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
. This naturally leads us to attempt to implement a Grover search algorithm to sign a bit. Because we have switched to the setting where the bit to be signed is in the image of
\mathcal{P}
rather than the pre-image, we know that with overwhelming probability, the coset
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
contains vectors that begin with
b
, in which case precisely half of them do (unless the first row of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
is the zero vector, a case we will deal with shortly) and so Grover search will succeed. Producing a signature directly is now simple: given
\ket*{\psi_\vecy}
, we can use Grover search using
\mathcal{D}
to transform it into

\ket*{\psi_{\vecy, b}}:=\sum_{x:\mathcal{P}(x)_{[1:r+1]}=\mathbf{y}||b}\ket*{\matA_\vecy\cdot\vecw_x + \vecb_\vecy}~.

Once we have done so, we can simply measure
\ket*{\psi_{\vecy, b}}
to get a coset vector
\vec{\sigma}
such that
\vec{\sigma}[1]=b
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x})=(\mathbf{y},\vec{\sigma})
for some
\mathbf{x}
. Verification simply consists of applying
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
to a supposed coset vector
\vec{\sigma}
and checking this condition.

By construction, any two message-signature pairs which are valid with respect to the same public key/hash
\mathbf{y}
(after an invocation of
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
) give us
\mathbf{x}_{0}\neq\mathbf{x}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x}_{0})_{[1:r]}=\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x}_{1})_{[1:r]}
– but this is exactly a collision in the hash function of
[
SZ25
]
! Thus, in this simple setting, we get collision resistance essentially for free. As a corollary, this also allows us to easily achieve the stronger notion of security for our one-shot signature scheme, where it is not only hard to produce a signature of a different message, but also to produce a new signature of the same message.

There are still two undesirable properties in this basic construction: imperfect correctness and short messages. First, observe that our equivocation/signing algorithm will only work if the coset
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
has vectors that start with the bit we want to sign, which does not happen with probability
1
. Our next idea, therefore, is to force this to be the case. Specifically, note that the bad event happens when all the column vectors of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
begin with 0. With this in mind, we will instead sample
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
by first sampling a random column vector that has a
1
in the first coordinate, followed by
n-r-1
linearly independent vectors with 0’s in the first coordinate, thereby guaranteeing that our cosets are “good”.

The second undesirable property of this basic scheme is that it only allows us to sign single-bit messages, which is the same issue that we encountered with
[
SZ25
]
. Consider for a moment the most natural extension to allow us to sign
\lambda
-bit messages: simply equivocate to the first
\lambda
bits of the coset register. Of course, this will break our signing algorithm, because we only have a dual oracle for
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
! However, there is a crucial difference between our signature scheme and that of
[
SZ25
]
: unlike in the
[
SZ25
]
construction, where the set of preimages whose first
\lambda
bits have been fixed are essentially a random collection of points, our images are very structured: indeed, the coset
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
, after fixing the first
i
bits to arbitrary values, is
still a coset
. Thus, we can modify
\mathcal{D}
to provide a series of oracles for the dual of the
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
cosets restricted to the first
i
bits being fixed; note that the duals do not depend on the particular fixing of the bits. Once we have done so, the signing algorithm can once again use
\mathcal{D}
to essentially sign bit-by-bit
before
measuring an image, allowing us to compress
\lambda
signatures into a single signature.

With some work, we show that even with this set of oracles/programs, our scheme remains secure. To do so, we prove several lemmas about random subspaces (see Appendix
D
) and modify the security proof of
[
SZ25
]
as necessary (see Appendices
E
and
F
for more details). This gives us a signature scheme with
\Theta(\lambda^{2})
qubit signing keys (which consists of just one preimage state),
\Theta(\lambda^{2})
-bit public keys and signatures for
\lambda
-bit messages, and a simple, iterative signing algorithm that makes one call to
\mathcal{D}
per bit of the message being signed. Verification is also more efficient, as we can now verify a signature for a
\lambda
-bit message with a single call to
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
.

To sign arbitrary polynomial-length messages, we use a random oracle to compress the message to
\lambda
bits before using our scheme to sign; this hash-and-sign transformation maintains the stronger security notion we want as well as perfect correctness.

Our construction in the plain model looks largely the same as our oracle model construction, where we replace our permutation with a pseudorandom permutation (PRP) and random functions with pseudorandom functions (PRFs) before obfuscating all our programs instead of providing oracle access, similar to
[
SZ25
]
. One difference between the oracle and plain model constructions is that the public key becomes larger due to padding; this is also a feature/bug of the
[
SZ25
]
construction. We note that in our construction, the public key used to verify a
\lambda
-bit signature is the same size as the public keys used to verify a single bit in
[
SZ25
]
, which we estimate to be around
\Theta(\lambda^{4})
bits. In the plain model, we can instantiate the random oracle with any arbitrary-domain collision-resistant hash function to extend the scheme to polynomial length messages.

2.2
Application to Quantum Fire

Our new OSS construction also allows us to construct public-key quantum fire, a notion first formalized in
[
BNZ25
]
. The recent result of
[
ÇGS25
]
constructed such a scheme (along with other applications of quantum fire) with respect to a classical oracle using the OSS scheme of
[
SZ25
]
. However, their proof relies on the existence of a one-shot signature scheme with strong incompressibility security, which does
not
hold for the
[
SZ25
]
construction.
5
5

5

It is potentially possible that the
one-bit

[
SZ25
]
construction satisfies a weaker version of incompressibility where the oracles the adversary gets are quantum rather than classical, but we leave the question of whether this is the case to future work.

Let us briefly recall the strong incompressibility guarantee: we say that a one-shot signature scheme
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Ver})
relative to a tuple of classical oracles
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Gen}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}})
respectively, has strong signature incompressibility if for all query-bounded quantum adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, the following is true with overwhelming probability: given oracle access to
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Gen}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}})
, for any polynomial-sized string
L
produced by
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, there is a list
\mathfrak{L}_{L}
of size
|\mathfrak{L}_{L}|\leq\mathsf{poly}(|L|,\lambda)
such that no query-bounded quantum adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
with access to only
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}
can produce valid signatures with respect to verification keys outside of
\mathfrak{L}_{L}
(with noticeable probability).

We observe that the
[
SZ25
]
construction is
not incompresssible
for at least two reasons:

1.

First, the signing algorithm uses both the
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
oracles, and given these two oracles,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
can ignore the string
L
it receives and simply run the key generation algorithm before measuring a random valid key-message-signature tuple. Each verification key appears with equal (but exponentially small) probability, so for any fixed list
\mathfrak{L}_{L}
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
will almost always produce signatures with respect to verification keys which are outside of
\mathfrak{L}_{L}
.
[
ÇGS25
]
incorrectly claim that the
[
SZ25
]
signing algorithm does not require access to
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
.

2.

Second, the
[
SZ25
]
construction for
\lambda
-bit messages (which is what is needed for
[
ÇGS25
]
) is simply a parallel repetition of the one-bit OSS scheme. However, schemes based on parallel repetition are generically
highly compressible
: the adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
can generate two valid key-message-signature tuples
(\mathsf{pk}_{i,0},0,\sigma_{i,0}),(\mathsf{pk}_{i,1},1,\sigma_{i,1})
for each bit and store this as the string
L
. The adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
can then output a valid signature with respect to an exponential number of verification keys, by mixing and matching the signatures for each bit.

Nevertheless, we show that a simple modification of our many-bit one-shot signature scheme
does
have strong signature incompressibility and thereby recover the results of
[
ÇGS25
]
. In fact, since this modified one-shot signature scheme has perfect correctness, the corresponding public-key quantum fire scheme (and all other related schemes) will also have perfect correctness. In Section
5
, we elaborate on how to tweak our scheme from Section
4
to produce an OSS scheme with signature incompressibility.

2.3
On the Use of the
[
DS22
]
Reduction in
[
SZ25
]

As noted earlier,
[
SZ25
]
provides a collapsing distinguisher between the case of measuring the hash value alone versus measuring the entire preimage. On the other hand, the
[
DS22
]
reduction is only able to construct equivocal hashes/commitments from non-collapsing
bit
commitments/hash functions. Thus, applying their reduction would require a proof that there is a distinguisher with good advantage between measuring the hash value alone versus measuring the hash value together with
some specific bit
of the preimage. It turns out that this is not hard to show; we include a proof that the
[
SZ25
]
distinguisher itself has this property in Appendix
G
.

We alluded to the possibility of using a simple Mariott-Watrous style algorithm for “amplifying” the success probability of a sum-binding adversary and turn it into a equivocal adversary. We argue that this is unlikely to work, at least without significant further analysis of the specifics of the
[
SZ25
]
scheme. Recall that to analyze the runtime of the alternating projections algorithm between projections
\Pi_{A}
and
\Pi_{B}
, we need to consider the Jordan decomposition between
\Pi_{A}
and
\Pi_{B}
. Specifically, Jordan’s lemma states that there is an orthogonal decomposition of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
upon which
\Pi_{A}
and
\Pi_{B}
act into one-dimensional or two-dimensional subspaces
\mathcal{S}_{j}
, such that
\mathcal{S}_{j}
is invariant under both
\Pi_{A}
and
\Pi_{B}
. This decomposition also has the property that on two-dimensional subspaces
\mathcal{S}_{j}
,
\Pi_{A}
and
\Pi_{B}
are rank-one projectors onto the states
\ket*{v_j}
and
\ket*{w_j}
, respectively, which defines the values
p_{j}=\bra*{w_j}\Pi_{A}\ket*{w_j}
, the cosine of the angle between
v_{j}
and
w_{j}
. The runtime of an average execution of the Mariott-Watrous algorithm starting with the state
\ket{\psi}=\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}\ket*{w_j}
can be equivalently described as first sampling
j
with probability
|\alpha_{j}|^{2}
and then flipping a
p_{j}
-biased coin repeatedly until a head appears; thus, the expected runtime is
\sum_{j}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}/{p_{j}}
. However, the only clear guarantee about the state
\ket{\psi}
is that the
average
probability
\sum_{j}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\cdot p_{j}
is good, i.e. some small constant. It might be the case that one of the
p_{j}
’s is very small (potentially exponentially so), in which case the average runtime may become superpolynomial or exponential in the unlucky scenario where the
j
sampled corresponds to this bad angle
p_{j}
. This motivates the need to consider multiple copies of the preimage state, as
[
DS22
]
does, which introduces overhead into the key generation and/or signing algorithms.

While it is potentially possible that a more detailed analysis of the Jordan decomposition could show that one could avoid using a rewinding/QSVT circuit, such an equivocator would still not be able to sign more than
O(\log\lambda)
bits at a time, since after
\omega(\log\lambda)
bits of the preimage state have been measured, the state does not admit any subspace/coset structure, and the advantage of the
[
SZ25
]
distinguisher vanishes completely.

2.4
Open Problems

We end this section with two open problems. First, the elephant in the room: can we improve the size of all parameters in the oracle model to
\Theta(\lambda)
bits? For the reader familiar with the
[
SZ25
]
proof, we offer a brief explanation why this is likely to require completely new techniques: in the oracle model, the bottleneck currently lies in analyzing the dual-free case, which requires collision resistance despite a coset structure.
[
SZ25
]
sidesteps this issue by using the parallel repetition of a 2-to-1 function and taking advantage of the fact that any pair of preimages is a coset for the
2
-to-
1
and noting that repetition preserves coset structure; this causes a
\lambda
factor blowup. A more direct analysis could yield better parameters: in particular, the recent frameworks for compressed permutation oracles
[
MMW25
,
Car25
]
may prove useful in deriving better query lower bounds. This could eliminate the
\lambda
factor blowup and result in (optimal)
\Theta(\lambda)
-bit public keys, signatures, and signing states.

Secondly, one can ask if the parameters of the plain model construction can be improved. Here, the blowup due to the parallel repetition of a 2-to-1 function shows up as well, and it is less clear how to deal with this issue. Additionally, in the
[
SZ25
]
proof, the base 2-to-1 function needs more properties (essentially, the function needs to be a simplified version of a NTCF), which forces a blowup in the public-key size. We suspect that entirely new techniques will be needed to significantly improve the parameters of our construction (and that of
[
SZ25
]
) in the plain model.

3
Preliminaries

Notations.

We will let
\mathsf{PPT}
denote classical probabilistic polynomial-time algorithms, and
\mathsf{QPT}
quantum polynomial-time algorithms.

For a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times m}
, let
\mathbf{A}^{[i:j]}
denote the
i
th through
j
th columns of
\mathbf{A}
, and let
\mathbf{A}^{[k]}
refer to the
k
th column of
\mathbf{A}
. For a matrix
\mathbf{A}
as above, we will let
\ColSpan(\mathbf{A})
denote the subspace generated by the columns of
\mathbf{A}
. Throughout this work, we will slightly abuse notation and refer to spans (denoted by
\spn\{\cdot\}
) of sets of vectors as well as sets of vectors and subspaces. For example,
\spn\{\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}),\mathbf{e}_{1}\}
refers to the set of all vectors which can be written as the linear combination of the unit vector
\mathbf{e}_{1}
and the column vectors of
\mathbf{A}
.

3.1
One-Shot Signatures

Definition 1

(One-Shot Signatures, adapted from
[
AGKZ20
,
SZ25
]
)
.

A one-shot signature (OSS) scheme is a tuple of algorithms
(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Ver})
together with message-space
(\mathcal{M}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
with the following interface:

•

\mathsf{CRS}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
: A
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithm that, given the security parameter
\lambda
, samples a classical common reference string
\mathsf{CRS}
.

•

(\mathsf{pk},\ket*{\sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(\mathsf{CRS})
: A
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm that takes the classical
\mathsf{CRS}
and samples a classical public key
\mathsf{pk}
and a quantum secret key
\ket*{\sk}
.

•

\sigma\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{CRS},\ket*{\sk}\!,m)
: A
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm that, given
\mathsf{CRS}
, the quantum key
\ket*{\sk}
and any message
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
, produces a classical signature
\sigma
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{CRS},\mathsf{pk},m,\sigma)\in\{0,1\}
: A classical deterministic polynomial-time algorithm which verifies a message
m
and signature
\sigma
relative to a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and a common reference string
\mathsf{CRS}
.

The algorithms satisfy the following correctness and security properties:

•

Perfect Correctness:
For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
,

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{CRS},\mathsf{pk},m,\sigma_{m})=1\end{array}\;:\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{CRS}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(\mathsf{CRS})\\
\sigma_{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{CRS},\ket*{\sk},m)\end{array}\right]=1.

•

Strong Security:
For any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathcal{A}
and for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}(m_{0},\sigma_{0})\neq(m_{1},\sigma_{1})\hskip 5.0pt\land\\
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{CRS},\mathsf{pk},m_{0},\sigma_{0})=1\hskip 5.0pt\land\\
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{CRS},\mathsf{pk},m_{1},\sigma_{1})=1\end{array}\;:\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{CRS}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{pk},m_{0},m_{1},\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathsf{CRS})\end{array}\right]=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Remark 3.1
.

Both the correctness and the security properties in this definition are stronger than
[
SZ25
]
.

Our perfect correctness property guarantees that for any message, the signing algorithm produces a valid signature with probability
1
(over the CRS and the internal randomness of
\mathsf{Gen}
and
\mathsf{Sign}
). One could consider a weaker notion of correctness where, for every message
m
, for most
\mathsf{CRS}
, the (strict) quantum polynomial-time signing algorithm produces a correct signature with prob
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
. The construction of
[
SZ25
]
only achieves this weaker form of correctness, and seemingly inherently so.

Additionally, one may consider a weaker security notion, where an adversary may be able to produce multiple valid signatures for the same message, but not for different messages. Both our construction and the construction of
[
SZ25
]
(the latter, along with our proof in Appendix
G
) achieve the strong security notion. We note, however, that a generically non-collapsing and binding commitment scheme would only give rise to a one-shot signature scheme with the weaker form of security via the
[
DS22
,
AGKZ20
]
reduction.

4
Our Construction in the Oracle Model

We begin by describing our one-shot signature scheme that allows us to sign
\lambda
-bit messages in the oracle model.

4.1
The Construction

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter and
\ell
denote the length of the messages we sign. We use the following parameters:

s:=16\cdot\lambda,r:=s\cdot(\lambda-1),\ell=\ell(\lambda):=\lambda,\hskip 7.22743pt\mbox{and}\hskip 7.22743ptn:=r+\ell+\frac{3}{2}\cdot s~.

Let
\Pi:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
be a random permutation and let
F:\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}^{n\cdot(n-r+1)}
be a random function. For each
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, let
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times\ell}
be a random matrix,
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times(n-r-\ell)}
a random matrix with full column-rank, and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be a uniformly random vector, all generated by the output randomness of
F(\mathbf{y})
. Define

\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)}~.

Then, let
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}
be functions (treated as oracles by all algorithms) defined as follows:

•

\mathcal{P}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to(\{0,1\}^{r}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})
is defined to be

\mathcal{P}(x)=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})\text{ where }\Pi(x)=\mathbf{y}||\mathbf{w}~.

•

\mathcal{P}^{-1}:(\{0,1\}^{r}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})\to\{0,1\}^{n}\cup\{\bot\}
is defined to be

\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u})=\begin{cases}\Pi^{-1}(\mathbf{y}||\mathbf{w})&\mbox{if }\exists\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}\text{ such that }\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{u}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

•

\mathcal{D}:([\ell+1]\times\{0,1\}^{r}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})\to\{0,1\}
is defined to be

\mathcal{D}(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}\text{ and }j\in[\ell+1]\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

The above distribution of oracles is denoted
\mathcal{O}_{n,r,\ell}
. We define our hash function
H(\mathbf{x})
to be the first
r
bits of
\Pi(\mathbf{x})
, so

H(\mathbf{x})=\Pi(\mathbf{x})_{[1:r]}

We will take the message space
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
to be the set of all strings
m\in\{0,1\}^{\ell(\lambda)}
. (In Appendix
C
, we show how to sign messages of arbitrary length, so we consider for now only messages with one fixed length for a given security parameter.)

Our OSS Scheme in the Classical Oracle Model

\mathsf{Gen}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1}}
:

Prepare the state
\ket{+}^{\otimes n}=\frac{1}{{2^{n/2}}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\ket{\vecx}
and apply
\mathcal{P}
.
Measure
H
to get a string
\mathsf{pk}:=\mathbf{y}
and the state

\propto\sum_{\mathbf{x}:H(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{y}}\ket{\vecx}\ket*{\matA_\vecy\cdot\vecw_\vecx+ \vecb_\vecy}~.

where
\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{x}}=\Pi(\mathbf{x})_{[r+1:n]}
.
Apply
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y},\cdot)
to uncompute
\mathbf{x}
, resulting in the state

\ket{\sk}\propto\sum_{\mathbf{x}:H(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{y}}\ket*{\matA_\vecy\cdot\vecw_{\vecx} + \vecb_\vecy}~.

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\sk})
.

\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{P}^{-1}}(\mathsf{pk},m,\vec{\sigma})
:

Output 1 if
\vec{\sigma}_{[1:\ell]}=m
and
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathsf{pk},\vec{\sigma})\neq\bot
; else, output 0.

\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\sk},m)
:

Initialize
\ket*{\psi_0}:=\ket*{\sk}
and parse
\mathsf{pk}=\mathbf{y}
.

For
\mathsf{iter}=1
to
\ell
:

•

Define

O_{1}(\mathsf{iter},m,\mathbf{y}):=(i-1)\sum_{\vec{\sigma}[1:\mathsf{iter}]=m[1:\mathsf{iter}]}\ket*{\vec\sigma}\bra*{\vec\sigma}+I

to be the first Grover reflection oracle that, on input
\vec{\sigma}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
, adds an imaginary phase
i
to every
\vec{\sigma}
such that
\vec{\sigma}[1:\mathsf{iter}]=m[1:\mathsf{iter}]
.

•

Define

O^{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(\mathsf{iter},m,\mathbf{y}):=H^{\otimes n}((i-1)\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{iter},\mathbf{y},\cdot)+I)H^{\otimes n}

to be the second Grover reflection oracle that, on input
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
, adds an imaginary phase
i
to every
\mathbf{v}
in the dual of the submatrix
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\mathsf{iter}:n-r]}
generated by all but the first
\mathsf{iter}-1
columns of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
.

•

Compute

\ket*{\psi_{\iter}}:=O^{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(\mathsf{iter},m,\mathbf{y})O_{1}(\mathsf{iter},m,\mathbf{y})\ket*{\psi_{\iter-1}}~.

Given
\ket*{\psi_\ell}
, measure in the standard basis to get a string
\vec{\sigma}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Output
\vec{\sigma}
.

Figure 2:
Our OSS scheme in the classical oracle model using oracles
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{D}
. For ease of exposition, we choose to give the signing algorithm the public key
\mathsf{pk}
in addition to the state
\ket{\sk}
; this is without loss of generality.

Our OSS Scheme.

With this setup, we are ready to construct our one-shot signature scheme; the scheme is described in Figure
1
. Since we are working in the oracle model, we omit
\mathsf{Setup}
and let
\mathcal{O}_{n,r,\ell}
play the role of the CRS.

The key generation and verification algorithms are self-explanatory, but the signing algorithm, which follows the general framework of
[
AGKZ20
]
, requires a bit of elaboration. For simplicity, consider the process of signing a single bit, say
1
. We start with the signing state
\ket{\sk}
which is a superposition of all vectors in the affine subspace
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
. Indeed,

\ket{\sk}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{\sk_0}+\ket{\sk_1})

where
\ket{\sk_b}
is the set of all vectors in the affine subspace
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
whose first coordinate is the bit
b
. Note that the support of
\ket{\sk_b}
is the set of all valid signature of the bit
b
. Applying the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{1}
from Figure
2
with
m=1
to
\ket{\sk}
results in the state
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{\sk_0}+i\ket{\sk_1})
.

Denote the result of the Hadamard transform applied to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{\sk_0}+\ket{\sk_1})
as
\ket*{\overline{\sk_0}}
, and that applied to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{\sk_0}-\ket{\sk_1})
as
\ket*{\overline{\sk_1}}
. The key observation is that the unitary
(i-1)\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{iter},\mathbf{y},\cdot)+I
transforms
\ket*{\overline{\sk_0}}
to
i\ket*{\overline{\sk_0}}
and leaves
\ket*{\overline{\sk_1}}
unchanged.

Now, applying the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{2}
results in the following sequence of transformations:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{\sk_0}+i\ket{\sk_1})\xrightarrow{H^{\otimes n}}\frac{1}{2}(\ket*{\overline{\sk_0}}

\displaystyle+\ket*{\overline{\sk_1}})+\frac{i}{2}(\ket*{\overline{\sk_0}}-\ket*{\overline{\sk_1}})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{(i-1)\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{iter},\mathbf{y},\cdot)+I}\frac{i(1+i)}{2}\ket*{\overline{\sk_0}}+\frac{(1-i)}{2}\ket*{\overline{\sk_1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(i-1)}{2}(\ket*{\overline{\sk_0}}-\ket*{\overline{\sk_1}})\xrightarrow{H^{\otimes n}}\frac{(i-1)}{\sqrt{2}}\ket{\sk_1}

This final state is simply the superposition over all valid signatures of the bit
1
, so measuring it in the standard basis does the job.

Remark 4.1
.

Each iteration of the Grover search will add a global phase to the signing token; this does not affect the correctness of the algorithm as this phase simply accumulates over iterations and the final step of the algorithm is to measure the token state, thereby erasing the phase.
For our applications, we will not need to implement a controlled version of the signing algorithm, for which a global phase could make a difference
[
Zha25a
]
. However, if that were to be desired in a future application, one can easily eliminate the global phase either by (a) extending our scheme to only sign messages whose lengths are the nearest multiple of 8; this will erase the global phase as
((i-1)/\sqrt{2})^{8}=1
, or (b) applying another phase gate, similar to
\mathcal{O}_{1}
, at the end of each iteration.

Remark 4.2
.

We note that in our oracle (and plain model) construction, the signing algorithm only needs to call
\mathcal{D}
with indices
j
where
1\leq j\leq\ell
, even though we also allow access to
j=\ell+1
. This is purely for notational convenience in the security proof and not necessary for correctness.

4.2
Perfect Correctness

In this section, we show that our signing algorithm always produces a valid signature for any message.

Lemma 4.3
.

For all security parameters
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and messages
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
,

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{P}^{-1}}(\mathsf{pk},m,\sigma_{m})=1\end{array}\;:\;\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1}},\\
\sigma_{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{D}}(\ket{\sk},m)\end{array}\right]=1.

where the probability is over the experiments and the choice of the oracles
\mathcal{O}_{n,r,\ell}
.

Proof.

First, observe that for any
\mathbf{y}
, the coset

W_{\mathbf{y}}:=\{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\}_{\mathbf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n-r}}

contains vectors which start with every prefix in
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
by construction. For
m\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
0\leq j\leq\ell
, define the sets

W_{\mathbf{y},m,j}:=\{w\in\{0,1\}^{n}:w_{[1:j]}=m_{[1:j]}\land\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y},w)\neq\bot\}

to be the set of all valid signatures of
m
w.r.t. the public key
\mathbf{y}
.
We will now fix
m
.

Since
W_{\mathbf{y}}
contains vectors that have all prefixes, this also means that it is split equally among all prefixes, i.e.
|W_{\mathbf{y},m,j}|=2^{n-r-j}
for all
m\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},0\leq j\leq\ell
. Indeed, this follows from the structure of the first
\ell
column vectors of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
for all
\mathbf{y}
.

For
1\leq\mathsf{iter}\leq\ell
, the idea is to run a phase-matched variant of Grover search
[
BHMT00
]
to transform

\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter-1}}:=\sum_{w\in W_{\mathbf{y},m,\mathsf{iter}-1}}\ket{w}\longrightarrow\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter}}:=\sum_{w\in W_{\mathbf{y},m,\mathsf{iter}}}\ket{w}

perhaps up to some global phase, so that we can eventually sign
m
.

In order to run Grover search, we need two reflection oracles: the first is an oracle which reflects marked items, which we have in
O_{1}(\mathsf{iter},m,\mathbf{y})
. The second is
U(\mathsf{iter},m,\mathbf{y}):=(i-1)\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter}}\bra*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter}}+I
; unfortunately, we do not have access to such an oracle. However, as in
[
AGKZ20
]
, we do have access to
\mathcal{D}
, which we claim suffices.

Observe that since the Grover search will operate on the subspace spanned by
\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter}}
and
\ket*{W_{\vecy, m+\vece_{\iter}, \iter}}
, it suffices to merely implement an oracle which applies a phase of
i
to
\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter-1}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter}}+\ket*{W_{\vecy, m+\vece_{\iter}, \iter}})
and acts as the identity on
\ket*{-_{\vecy, m, \iter-1}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter}}-\ket*{W_{\vecy, m+\vece_{\iter}, \iter}})
. It is not hard to see that
O_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{iter},m,\mathbf{y})
is precisely such a reflection oracle. Let us first consider what happens to
\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter-1}}
:

1.

We begin with the state
\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter-1}}=\sum_{w\in W_{\mathbf{y},m,\mathsf{iter}-1}}\ket*{w}_{\mathsf{W}}
.

2.

Applying
H^{\otimes n}
gives the state
\sum_{d\in\left(A^{[\mathsf{iter}:n-r]}_{\mathbf{y}}\right)^{\perp}}\ket{d}_{\mathsf{W}}
.

3.

Applying
((i-1)\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{iter},\mathbf{y},\cdot)+I)
adds a phase of
i
to the state. Applying
H^{\otimes n}
again results in the state
i\sum_{w\in W_{\mathbf{y},m,\mathsf{iter}-1}}\ket*{w}_{\mathsf{W}}=i\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \iter-1}}
.

On the other hand, we can consider what happens to
\ket*{-_{\vecy, m, \iter-1}}
:

1.

We begin with the state

\ket*{-_{\vecy, m, \iter-1}}=\sum_{w\in W_{\mathbf{y},m,\mathsf{iter}}}\ket*{w}_{\mathsf{W}}-\sum_{w\in W_{\mathbf{y},m+\mathbf{e}_{\mathsf{iter}},\mathsf{iter}}}\ket*{w}_{\mathsf{W}}.

2.

Applying
H^{\otimes n}
gives the state

\sum_{d\in\left(A^{[\mathsf{iter}+1:n-r]}_{\mathbf{y}}\right)^{\perp}\setminus\left(A^{[\mathsf{iter}:n-r]}_{\mathbf{y}}\right)^{\perp}}(-1)^{f(d,m)}\ket{d}_{\mathsf{W}},

where
f
is some Boolean function.

3.

Now, applying
((i-1)\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{iter},\mathbf{y},\cdot)+I)
acts as the identity. Thus, applying
H^{\otimes n}
again results in the state

\sum_{w\in W_{\mathbf{y},m,\mathsf{iter}}}\ket*{w}_{\mathsf{W}}-\sum_{w\in W_{\mathbf{y},m+\mathbf{e}_{\mathsf{iter}},\mathsf{iter}}}\ket*{w}_{\mathsf{W}}=\ket*{-_{\vecy, m, \iter-1}}.

Since
|W_{\mathbf{y},m,\mathsf{iter}}|=2^{n-r-\mathsf{iter}}
is exactly half of the size of
W_{\mathbf{y},m,\mathsf{iter}-1}
, we will succeed in rotating our state with certainty in just one iteration (up to some global phase) by the correctness of Grover search. Iterating for
1\leq\mathsf{iter}\leq\ell
results in the state
\ket*{W_{\vecy, m, \ell}}=\sum_{w\in W_{\mathbf{y},m,\ell}}\ket{w}
. All that remains is to measure this state, which gives some string
\sigma_{m}
such that
(\sigma_{m})_{[1:\ell]}=m
and
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y},\sigma_{m})\neq\bot
; this is precisely the condition for the signature verification to succeed.
∎

4.3
Strong Security

We now show that our new one-shot signature scheme is strongly secure. We use the same general proof strategy as
[
SZ25
]
, modifying components as needed. As such, we defer most proofs to Appendix
E
.

We begin by considering what happens when we “bloat” the dual oracle. Let
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n,r,\ell,s}
denote the following distribution over oracles
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. The oracles
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
are defined identically to the oracles in
\mathcal{O}_{n,r,\ell}
. However, our new dual oracle
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
will be a relaxed version of the original dual oracle
\mathcal{D}
:

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+1+s:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}=0^{n-r-s-j+1}\text{ and }j\in[\ell+1],\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The following lemma states that any adversary
\mathcal{A}
that finds a collision given access to
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{D}
, is also likely to find a collision
6
6

6

Technically, the lemma states that
\mathcal{A}
produces something which is stronger than a collision.
given access to
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 4.4

(The Bloating Lemma)
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
. Suppose there is an oracle-aided
q
-query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
such that

\Pr[(\mathbf{y}_{0}=\mathbf{y}_{1})\land(\mathbf{x}_{0}\neq\mathbf{x}_{1}):\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})&\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{n,r,\ell}\\
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})&\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}}\\
(\mathbf{y}_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})&\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x}_{b})\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon.

Define
t:=n-r-s
. If
q^{3}\cdot n^{3}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell})=o(1)
and
\frac{q^{7}\cdot n^{10}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}=o(1)
, then

\Pr[\begin{array}[]{l}{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{y}_{0}=\mathbf{y}_{1}:=\mathbf{y})\hskip 5.0pt\land}\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})\notin\ColSpan}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[1:\ell]}}&{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}}\end{bmatrix}\right)\end{array}:\begin{aligned} {\scriptstyle(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n,r,\ell,s}}\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}}\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{y}_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x}_{b})}\end{aligned}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}.

We now show that given an adversary which succeeds with a bloated dual oracle, one can actually entirely simulate the dual with only access to the primal oracles.

Lemma 4.5

(The “Dual is Useless” Lemma)
.

Suppose that there is an oracle aided
q
-query algorithm
\mathcal{A}
such that

\Pr[\begin{array}[]{l}{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{y}_{0}=\mathbf{y}_{1}:=\mathbf{y})\hskip 5.0pt\land}\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})\notin\ColSpan}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[1:\ell]}}&{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}}\end{bmatrix}\right)\end{array}:\begin{aligned} {\scriptstyle(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n,r,\ell,s}}\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}}\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{y}_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x}_{b})}\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon.

Then there is an oracle aided
q
-query algorithm
\mathcal{B}
such that

\Pr[(\overline{\mathbf{y}}_{0}=\overline{\mathbf{y}}_{1})\land(\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{0}\neq\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{1}):\begin{aligned} (\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1},\overline{\mathcal{D}})&\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{r+s,r,0}\\
(\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{1})&\leftarrow\mathcal{B}^{\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}}\\
(\overline{\mathbf{y}}_{b},\overline{\mathbf{u}}_{b})&\leftarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{b})\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon.

The proofs of Lemmas
4.4
and Lemma
4.5
closely follow the proofs of Lemmas 30 and 31 in
[
SZ25
]
, so we include their proofs for completeness but defer them to Appendix
E
.

Let us now recall the definition of a coset partition function.

Definition 2

(Coset Partition Functions,
[
SZ25
]
)
.

For
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k\leq n
we say a function
Q:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{m}
is a
(n,m,k)
-coset partition function if, for each
y
in the image of
Q
, the pre-image set
Q^{-1}(y)
has size
2^{k}
and is a coset of a linear space of dimension
k
. We allow different preimage sets to be cosets of different linear spaces.

Having reduced our collision resistance to the dual-free case, we can use the reduction in
[
SZ25
]
from the dual-free case to finding collisions in coset-partition functions:

Theorem 4.6

(Adapted from Theorem 34,
[
SZ25
]
)
.

Suppose there is an oracle aided
q
-query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
such that

\Pr[(\mathbf{y}_{0}=\mathbf{y}_{1})\land(\mathbf{x}_{0}\neq\mathbf{x}_{1}):\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})&\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{r+s,r,0}\\
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})&\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1}}\\
(\mathbf{y}_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})&\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x}_{b})\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon.

Then there is an oracle aided
q
-query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{B}
that given any
(r+s,r,s)
-coset partition function
Q
, satisfies

\Pr[(Q(\mathbf{w}_{0})=Q(\mathbf{w}_{1}))\land(\mathbf{w}_{0}\neq\mathbf{w}_{1}):(\mathbf{w}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{1})\leftarrow\mathcal{B}^{Q}]\geq\varepsilon.

Combining the three results gives us the following corollary:

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
. Suppose there is an oracle-aided
q
-query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
such that

\Pr[(\mathbf{y}_{0}=\mathbf{y}_{1})\land(\mathbf{x}_{0}\neq\mathbf{x}_{1}):\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})&\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{n,r,\ell}\\
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})&\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}}\\
(\mathbf{y}_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})&\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x}_{b})\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon.

Define
t:=n-r-s
and suppose
q^{3}\cdot n^{3}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell})=o(1)
and
\frac{q^{7}\cdot n^{10}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}}{\sqrt{2^{t-\ell}}}=o(1)
. Then there is an oracle aided
q
-query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{B}
that given any
(r+s,r,s)
-coset partition function
Q
, satisfies

\Pr[(Q(\mathbf{w}_{0})=Q(\mathbf{w}_{1}))\land(\mathbf{w}_{0}\neq\mathbf{w}_{1}):(\mathbf{w}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{1})\leftarrow\mathcal{B}^{Q}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}.

Finally, we use the collision-resistance of coset partition functions as shown in
[
SZ25
]
.

Theorem 4.8

(Adapted from Theorem 35,
[
SZ25
]
)
.

For any
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s\mid r
, there exists a distribution over
(r+s,r,s)
-coset partition functions
Q
, such that any algorithm making
q
queries to
Q
can only find collisions in
Q
with probability at most
O(\frac{s^{3}\cdot q^{3}}{2^{(r+s)/s}})
.

Combining all these results allows us to conclude that our one-shot signature scheme is collision-resistant:

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an oracle-aided
q
-query quantum algorithm. Then,

\Pr[(\mathbf{x}_{0}\neq\mathbf{x}_{1})\land(H(\mathbf{x}_{0})=H(\mathbf{x}_{1})):\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})&\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{n,r,\ell}\\
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})&\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}}\end{aligned}]=O\left(\frac{\lambda^{3}\cdot q^{3}\cdot n^{3}}{2^{\lambda}}\right).

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
\mathcal{A}
which makes
q
queries which outputs a collision in
H
with probability
\varepsilon=\omega\left(\frac{\lambda^{3}\cdot q^{3}\cdot n^{3}}{2^{\lambda}}\right)
. By the choice of our parameters in the OSS Construction (Figure
2
), we have that for
t:=n-r-s
,

\displaystyle q^{3}\cdot n^{3}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell})

\displaystyle=q^{3}\cdot n^{3}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot(2^{\ell-s/2}+2^{-s/2})

\displaystyle\leq q^{3}\cdot n^{3}\cdot\frac{2^{2\lambda}}{\lambda^{6}\cdot q^{6}\cdot n^{6}}\cdot(2^{\ell-s/2}+2^{-s/2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{6}\cdot q^{3}\cdot n^{3}}\cdot(2^{-5\lambda}+2^{-6\lambda})=o(1),

and

\frac{q^{7}\cdot n^{10}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}=\frac{q^{7}\cdot n^{10}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}}{2^{s/4}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{12}\cdot q^{5}\cdot n^{2}}=o(1).

Thus, the conditions of Corollary
4.7
are fulfilled, so there is a
q
-query algorithm
\mathcal{B}
that given oracle access to any
(r+s,r,s)
-coset partition function
Q
(and thus over any distribution of such functions), finds a collision in
Q
with probability
\frac{\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}
. Since
s\mid r
, we can consider the distribution over
(r+s,r,s)
-coset partition functions generated by Theorem
4.8
, which implies that

\frac{\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}=O\left(\frac{s^{3}\cdot q^{3}}{2^{(r+s)/s}}\right)=O\left(\frac{\lambda^{3}\cdot q^{3}}{2^{\lambda}}\right),

and so

\varepsilon=O\left(\frac{\lambda^{3}\cdot q^{3}\cdot n^{2}}{2^{\lambda}}\right),

contradicting
\varepsilon=\omega(\frac{\lambda^{3}\cdot q^{3}\cdot n^{3}}{2^{\lambda}})
.
∎

We can now see that our scheme is strongly secure:

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an oracle aided
q
-query quantum algorithm. Then,

\Pr[\begin{array}[]{l}(m_{0},\sigma_{0})\neq(m_{1},\sigma_{1})\hskip 3.0pt\land\\
\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{P}^{-1}}(\mathsf{pk},m_{0},\sigma_{0})=1\hskip 3.0pt\land\\
\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{P}^{-1}}(\mathsf{pk},m_{1},\sigma_{1})=1\end{array}:\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})&\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{n,r,\ell}\\
(\mathsf{pk},m_{0},\sigma_{0},m_{1},\sigma_{1})&\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}}\end{aligned}]=O\left(\frac{\lambda^{3}\cdot q^{3}\cdot n^{3}}{2^{\lambda}}\right).

Proof.

Fix any adversary
\mathcal{A}
that outputs
(\mathsf{pk},m_{0},\sigma_{0},m_{1},\sigma_{1})
such that
(m_{0},\sigma_{0})\neq(m_{1},\sigma_{1})
and

\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{P}^{-1}}(\mathsf{pk},m_{0},\sigma_{0})=\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{P}^{-1}}(\mathsf{pk},m_{1},\sigma_{1})=1.

If
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{1}
, then we must have
(\sigma_{i})_{[1:\ell]}=m_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
and thus
m_{0}=m_{1}
, which is a contradiction. Thus, it must be the case that
\sigma_{0}\neq\sigma_{1}
.

This means that
\mathcal{A}
produces two signatures
\sigma_{0}\neq\sigma_{1}
and a public key
\mathsf{pk}
such that
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathsf{pk},\sigma_{i})\neq\bot
. This means that there exists a
(q+2)
-query algorithm
\mathcal{B}
which produces
x_{0}\neq x_{1}
such that
H(x_{0})=H(x_{1})
which first runs
\mathcal{A}
and then queries
x_{0}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathsf{pk},\sigma_{0})
and
x_{1}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathsf{pk},\sigma_{1})
with the same success probability as
\mathcal{A}
. As
\mathcal{B}
succeeds with probability at most
O\left(\frac{\lambda^{3}\cdot(q+2)^{3}\cdot n^{3}}{2^{\lambda}}\right)=O\left(\frac{\lambda^{3}\cdot q^{3}\cdot n^{3}}{2^{\lambda}}\right)
by Theorem
4.9
,
\mathcal{A}
is only able to produce two valid message-signature pairs with respect to the same verification key with probability
O\left(\frac{\lambda^{3}\cdot q^{3}\cdot n^{3}}{2^{\lambda}}\right)
as desired.
∎

5
Public-Key Quantum Fire and Other Applications

To build a public-key quantum fire scheme, we first need a one-shot signature scheme with strong incompressibility security, as defined below:

Definition 3

(Strong Incompressibility Security,
[
ÇGS25
]
)
.

Consider a one-shot signature scheme
\mathsf{OSS}=(\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver})
relative to a tuple of classical oracles
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Gen}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})
where each algorithm of
\mathsf{OSS}
uses the related oracle only.

\mathsf{OSS}
is said to satisfy average-case strong (polynomial) incompressibility security if for any query-bounded adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with polynomial size classical output, there is a polynomial
s
such that

\Pr[E:\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Gen}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})&\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
L&\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Gen}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}\end{aligned}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),

where
E
is the event defined as

\displaystyle\exists\mathfrak{L}_{L}\in(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)})^{s(\lambda)}:\forall\textrm{\ query-bounded uniform adversaries }\mathcal{A}_{2},

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{pk}\notin\mathfrak{L}_{L}\ \land\\
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}(\mathsf{pk},m,\mathsf{sig})=1\end{array}\;:\;\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathsf{pk},m,\mathsf{sig})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}(L)\end{array}\right]=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

We are now ready to describe the incompressible OSS scheme. It is nearly identical to the construction in Figure
2
, but modified so that the coset vector
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
is never a valid signature w.r.t. the public key
\mathbf{y}
. This will help with the incompressibility proof, where we will prove a stronger statement for adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{2}
that have
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
.

Define parameters
s:=16\cdot(\lambda+1)
,
r:=s\cdot\lambda
,
\ell=\ell(\lambda):=\lambda+1
, and
n:=r+\ell+\frac{3}{2}\cdot s
.
Let the random permutation
\Pi
and the random function
F
be as before. For each
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, let
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times\ell}
be a random matrix,
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times(n-r-\ell)}
a random matrix with full column-rank. Slightly differently from before, we will choose
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
to be a uniformly random vector whose
\ell
’th coordinate is 1. All these are generated by the output randomness of
F(\mathbf{y})
. Define as before

\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)}~.

Then, let
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
,
\mathcal{D}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{0}
be the following oracles:

•

\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}
are the same as in Figure
2
.

•

\mathcal{D}_{0}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u})=\begin{cases}1&\exists\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}\text{ such that }\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{u}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

We will refer to the distribution of oracles
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{0})
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{n,r,\ell}
. We define our incompressible OSS scheme for signing
\lambda
-bit messages in Figure
3
.

Incompressible OSS Scheme in the Classical Oracle Model

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

Sample
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
,
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{D}_{0}
as above. Set
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}=\mathcal{D}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}=\mathcal{D},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Gen}}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{0})
. Output
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Gen}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}})
.

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Gen}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Gen}}}
:

Same as in Figure
2
.

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}}(\mathsf{pk},m,\sigma)
:

Output 1 if
\sigma_{[1:\ell]}=m||0
and
\mathcal{D}_{0}(\mathsf{pk},\sigma)=1
; else, output 0.

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}}(\ket{\sk}\!,m)
:

Run
\mathsf{OSS}.\widetilde{\mathsf{Sign}}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}}(\ket{\sk}\!,m||0)
, where
\mathsf{OSS}.\widetilde{\mathsf{Sign}}
is the signing algorithm in Figure
2
.

Figure 3:
Incompressible OSS scheme in the classical oracle model using oracles
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
,
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}_{0}
.

Lemma 5.1

(Correctness and Security)
.

The OSS scheme in Figure
3
has perfect correctness and strong signature security.

We defer the proof of this lemma to Appendix
E
since it is nearly identical to the correctness and security proofs of the construction in Figure
2
.
Before we continue, we introduce a result that we will use in our incompressibility proof.

Definition 4

(
[
BBBV97
]
)
.

For an oracle algorithm
A
making
q
queries to an oracle
\mathcal{O}
with input domain
D
, let
\sum_{x}\alpha^{(i)}_{x}\ket{x}
denote the
i
th oracle query, and let
M_{x}(i)=|\alpha^{(i)}_{x}|^{2}
be the query mass of
x
in the
i
’th query. For a subset
V\subseteq[q]\times D
, let
M_{V}=\sum_{(i,x)\in V}M_{x}(i)
be the total query mass of points in
V
.

Theorem 5.2

(
[
BBBV97
]
)
.

Let
A
be an algorithm which makes
q
queries to an oracle
\mathcal{O}
with input domain
D
and let
V\subseteq[q]\times D
be a set of time-input pairs to
\mathcal{O}
. If we modify
\mathcal{O}
into an oracle
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
which is identical to
\mathcal{O}
except possibly on inputs in
V
, then the final quantum states of
A
given access to
\mathcal{O}
or
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
have trace distance at most
O\left(\sqrt{q\cdot M_{V}}\right)
.

Lemma 5.3

(Incompressibility)
.

The OSS scheme in Figure
3
has strong incompressibility security.

Proof.

The proof follows the general outline of
[
ÇGS25
]
closely; some parts may be cited verbatim. We point out the key differences which address the issues with the proof of
[
ÇGS25
]
.

First, note that in the construction of
[
SZ25
]
, the signing algorithm makes critical use of all three oracles
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{D}
. But if
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has access to both
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, then even without
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
or
L
, it can trivially produce signatures for many keys outside of any polynomial-sized list of keys
\mathfrak{L}_{L}
. This is since it can always run the key generation algorithm and produce key-message-signature tuples
(\mathsf{pk},m,\sigma)
such that with high probability,
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{pk},m,\sigma)=1
and
\mathsf{pk}\notin\mathfrak{L}_{L}
. In particular, this means that the
[
SZ25
]
OSS construction does
not
have signature incompressibility. On the other hand, our construction only requires
\mathcal{D}
to sign and
\mathcal{D}_{0}
to verify, which means it is plausible that our scheme has signature incompressibility (and we will show that this is indeed the case).

We will actually prove a stronger result where
\mathcal{A}_{1}
directly gets the values
\Pi
,
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})_{\mathbf{y}}
,
(\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})_{\mathbf{y}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
directly gets
\Pi
,
(\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})_{\mathbf{y}}
, and oracle access to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
. Additionally, instead of the original game, where we ask
\mathcal{A}_{2}
to output a valid key-message-signature tuple, we will ask
\mathcal{A}_{2}
to output a pair
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
such that
\mathbf{v}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
. We explain why this is at least as easy for
\mathcal{A}_{2}
here as in the original security game.

Observe that in order for
\mathsf{sig}
to be a valid signature for
m
with respect to some key
\mathsf{pk}=\mathbf{y}
, it must be the case that
\mathsf{sig}-\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})
and that
\mathsf{sig}_{\ell}=0
. However, since the
\ell
’th coordinate of
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
is always nonzero (by construction),
\mathsf{sig}-\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
is also always nonzero. Thus, given
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
, any signature
\mathsf{sig}
automatically gives rise to a nonzero vector
\mathsf{sig}-\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
in the column span of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
. This is the second difference between our construction and the
[
SZ25
]
scheme; in the
[
SZ25
]
construction,
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
(or more accurately,
\mathbf{y}
and the preimage of
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
) is always a valid signature for some message, so
\mathcal{A}_{2}
can once again trivially produce valid key-message-signature tuples (this time for
any
key) given
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
. On the other hand, in our construction,
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
is never a valid signature for any message since it is (by design) nonzero on the
\ell
’th coordinate.

We now observe that for each
\mathbf{y}
, there are

C_{1}=2^{(n-\ell)\ell}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n-r-\ell-1}(2^{n-\ell}-2^{i})

equally likely matrices for
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
.

Now fix any vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
. We first consider how many matrices of the form
\mathbf{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{C}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)}
, where
\mathbf{C}
is a full-rank matrix, contain
\mathbf{v}
in their column span. We can equivalently consider the probability that a random matrix of this form contains
\mathbf{v}
in its column span and derive the total number of matrices accordingly.

Suppose
\mathbf{v}_{[1:\ell]}=0^{\ell}
; in this case, whether a matrix contains
\mathbf{v}
in its column span or not is independent of
\mathbf{B}
. Thus, this probability is simply the probability that a random full-rank matrix in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times(n-r-\ell)}
contain a nonzero vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-\ell}
in its column span, which is simply
\frac{2^{n-r-\ell}-1}{2^{n-\ell}-1}
and so there are

C_{2}=\frac{2^{n-r-\ell}-1}{2^{n-\ell}-1}\cdot C_{1}=2^{(n-\ell)\ell}\cdot(2^{n-r-\ell}-1)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n-r-\ell-1}(2^{n-\ell}-2^{i})

such matrices.

Now suppose instead that
\mathbf{v}_{[1:\ell]}\neq 0^{\ell}
; in this case,
\mathbf{v}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{A})
if and only if
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{v}_{[1:\ell]}+\mathbf{v}_{[\ell+1:n]}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{C})
. Since
\mathbf{v}_{[1:\ell]}
is nonzero, for a random matrix
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{v}_{[1:\ell]}+\mathbf{v}_{[\ell+1:n]}
is a uniformly random vector in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-\ell}
. Thus, the probability that
\mathbf{v}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{A})
is

\frac{1}{2^{n-\ell}}\cdot 1+\frac{2^{n-\ell}-1}{2^{n-\ell}}\cdot\frac{2^{n-r-\ell}-1}{2^{n-\ell}-1}=\frac{2^{n-r-\ell}}{2^{n-\ell}},

and so there are

C_{3}=\frac{2^{n-r-\ell}}{2^{n-\ell}}\cdot C_{1}=2^{(n-\ell)(\ell-1)}\cdot 2^{n-r-\ell}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n-r-\ell-1}(2^{n-\ell}-2^{i})>C_{2}

such matrices.

The second thing we consider is how many matrices of the form
\mathbf{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{C}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)}
, where
\mathbf{C}
is a full-rank matrix, have the property that
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
for some
j\in[\ell+1]
. It is clear that if this condition holds for any
j
, then it must be the case that
\mathbf{v}_{[\ell+1:n]}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}=0^{n-r-\ell}
. In addition, for any
j
, if
\mathbf{v}_{[\ell+1:n]}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
, then either
\mathbf{v}_{[j:n]}=0^{n-j+1}
, in which case it is always the case that
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
, or
\mathbf{v}_{[\ell+1:n]}\neq 0^{n-\ell}
. We will ignore (for now) the case where
\mathbf{v}_{[j:n]}=0^{n-j+1}
since this reveals no information about
\mathbf{A}
. We upper bound the fraction of matrices
\mathbf{C}
such that
\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}=0^{n-r-\ell}
for some nonzero
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-\ell}
, and thus the fraction of matrices
\mathbf{A}
such that
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
assuming
\mathbf{v}_{[j:n]}\neq 0^{n-j+1}
.

Clearly the dual of
\mathbf{C}
has dimension
(n-\ell)-(n-r-\ell)=r
; thus, the fraction of matrices
\mathbf{C}
such that
\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}=0^{n-r-\ell}
is at most

\frac{2^{r}-1}{2^{n-\ell}-1}\leq\frac{2^{r}}{2^{n-\ell}}=2^{-(n-\ell)+r}.

The number of matrices
\mathbf{A}
is thus at most

C_{4}=2^{-(n-\ell)+r}\cdot C_{1}=2^{-3s/2}\cdot C_{1}>C_{3}.

For any string
L^{*}
from the output support of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, let
S_{L^{*}}
denote the set consisting of all vectors
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
such that
H_{\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\mid L=L^{*})\leq\log C_{4}+\lambda
. We will argue that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
cannot succeed with non-negligible probability even when given
\{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\}_{\mathbf{y}\in S_{L^{*}}}
.

We begin by considering the special case where
\mathcal{A}_{2}
does not make any oracle queries. We claim that the probability that
\mathcal{A}_{2}(L^{*})
can output either a)
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
such that
\mathbf{v}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})\setminus\{0\}
or b)
(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
such that
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{[j:n]}\neq 0^{n-j+1}
for some
j\in[\ell+1]
where
\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}
is negligible.

Suppose this is not the case; then there is some non-negligible
\varepsilon
such that either
\mathcal{A}_{2}
can output
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
with probability
\varepsilon
or it can output
(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
with probability
\varepsilon
. Then, whenever
\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs a valid tuple
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
or
(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
for
\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}
, there are at most
\max\{C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}\}=C_{4}
options for
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
and so
\mathcal{A}_{2}
can guess
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
with probability at least
\frac{\varepsilon}{C_{4}}
. However, this contradicts the fact that
H_{\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\mid L=L^{*})\leq\log C_{4}+\lambda
; thus, it must be the case that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
succeeds only with negligible probability.

We now bound the size of
S_{L^{*}}
in this special case. Observe that

\displaystyle\Pr[H_{\infty}((\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})_{\mathbf{y}}\mid L=L^{*})\geq 2^{r}\cdot\log C_{1}-|L|-\lambda]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr[H_{\infty}((\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})_{\mathbf{y}}\mid L=L^{*})\geq\sum_{\mathbf{y}}H_{\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})-|L|-\lambda]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\Pr[H_{\infty}((\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})_{\mathbf{y}}\mid L=L^{*})\geq H_{\infty}((\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})_{\mathbf{y}}|L)-\lambda]\geq 1-2^{-\lambda}.

On the other hand, for any string
L^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle H_{\infty}((\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})_{\mathbf{y}}\mid L=L^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{y}}H_{\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\mid L=L^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in S_{L^{*}}}H_{\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\mid L=L^{*})+\sum_{\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}}H_{\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\mid L=L^{*})

\displaystyle\leq|S_{L^{*}}|\cdot\left(\log C_{4}+\lambda\right)+(2^{r}-|S_{L^{*}}|)\cdot\log C_{1}.

Thus, with probability
1-2^{-\lambda}
,

\displaystyle|S_{L^{*}}|\cdot\left(\log C_{4}+\lambda\right)+(2^{r}-|S_{L^{*}}|)\cdot\log C_{1}
