\displaystyle\geq 2^{r}\cdot\log C_{1}-|L|-\lambda

\displaystyle\implies|L|+\lambda\geq|S_{L^{*}}|\cdot\left(\log\frac{C_{1}}{C_{4}}-\lambda\right)

\displaystyle\geq|S_{L^{*}}|\cdot\left(3s/2-\lambda\right)\geq|S_{L^{*}}|,

and so
\Pr[|S_{L^{*}}|\leq|L|+\lambda]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
for all
\lambda
, which completes the proof for the special case.

We now move to the general setting where
\mathcal{A}_{2}(L^{*})
can make any polynomial
q(\lambda)
number of queries. Let
\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}
be the success probability of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. We show how to remove the first query of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
before iterating this process to remove all queries of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Here, we clarify an ambiguity in the proof of
[
ÇGS25
]
, which does not concretely address queries to
\mathcal{D}
; this modification is not too hard with additional calculations, but requires some care.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}_{2}
’s first query is to
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. For just the first query, consider running
\mathcal{A}_{2}
with the oracle
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}
which is a modification of
\mathcal{D}_{0}
as follows: on all inputs
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
where
\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}
, output 0 if
\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
and 1 otherwise; on inputs
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
where
\mathbf{y}\in S_{L^{*}}
, behave identically to
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Clearly, it is the case that
\mathcal{D}_{0}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})=1
, and so the two oracles differ precisely on input pairs
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
such that
\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}
,
\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{0}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=1
. The total query mass on inputs on which
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}
differ is at most

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}\\
\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\land\mathcal{D}_{0}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y})=1\end{subarray}}|\alpha_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}}|^{2}.

We claim that this query mass is negligible. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that this query mass was non-negligible; consider the adversary
\mathcal{B}
which runs
\mathcal{A}_{2}
up until it makes its first query, measures the query that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
would have made to
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and gets the measurement outcome
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
before returning
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
. By assumption, with non-negligible probability,
\mathcal{B}
outputs a pair
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
such that
\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}
,
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\neq 0
, but
\mathcal{D}_{0}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=1
and so
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}})\setminus\{0\}
. But this violates the security we proved for the special case since
\mathcal{B}
makes no queries! Thus, it must be the case that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}\\
\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\land\mathcal{D}_{0}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y})=1\end{subarray}}|\alpha_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}}|^{2}=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

By Theorem
5.2
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
still succeeds with the reprogrammed oracle
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}
for its first query (and
\mathcal{D}_{0}
or
\mathcal{D}
for the other queries) with probability

\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}-\sqrt{q\cdot\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)}\geq\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}-\frac{1}{2p(\lambda)q(\lambda)}.

But now, the query that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
makes to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}
is completely unnecessary: given
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
for all
\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
for
\mathbf{y}\in S_{L^{*}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
can
itself
simulate access to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}
! We can therefore remove/simulate
\mathcal{A}_{2}
’s first query altogether and end up with an adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{2}
which makes
q-1
queries and still succeeds with probability at least
\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}-\frac{1}{2p(\lambda)q(\lambda)}
.

We now consider the case where
\mathcal{A}_{2}
’s first query is to
\mathcal{D}
. For just the first query, consider running
\mathcal{A}_{2}
with the oracle
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
which is a modification of
\mathcal{D}
as follows: on inputs
(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
where
\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}
, output 0 if
\mathbf{v}_{[j:n]}\neq 0^{n-j+1}
and 1 otherwise; if
\mathbf{y}\in S_{L^{*}}
, then behave identically to
\mathcal{D}
. As argued earlier, if
\mathbf{v}_{[j:n]}=0^{n-j+1}
, then we always have that
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
and thus
\mathcal{D}(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=1
. We conclude that
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
differ precisely on inputs
(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
such that
\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}
,
\mathbf{v}_{[j:n]}\neq 0^{n-j+1}
, and
\mathcal{D}(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=1
. The total query mass on inputs on which
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
differ is thus at most

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}\\
i,\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{v}_{[j:n]}\neq 0^{n-j+1}\\
\mathcal{D}(j,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y})=1\end{subarray}}|\alpha_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}}|^{2}.

As with the previous case, we claim that this query mass is negligible. Suppose otherwise; consider the adversary
\mathcal{B}
which runs
\mathcal{A}_{2}
up until it makes its first query, measures the query that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
would have made to
\mathcal{D}
and gets the measurement outcome
(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
, which it returns. By assumption, with non-negligible probability,
\mathcal{B}
outputs
(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
such that
\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}
,
\mathbf{v}_{[j:n]}\neq 0^{n-j+1}
, but
\mathcal{D}(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=1
and so
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
. But this violates the security we proved for the special case since
\mathcal{B}
makes no queries. Thus, it must be the case that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\notin S_{L^{*}}\\
j,\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{v}_{[j:n]}\neq 0^{n-j+1}\\
\mathcal{D}(j,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y})=1\end{subarray}}|\alpha_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}}|^{2}=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

By Theorem
5.2
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
still succeeds with the reprogrammed oracle
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
for its first query (and
\mathcal{D}
or
\mathcal{D}_{0}
for the other queries) with probability

\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}-\sqrt{q\cdot\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)}\geq\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}-\frac{1}{2p(\lambda)q(\lambda)}.

But now, the query that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
makes to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is completely unnecessary: given
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
for all
\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
for
\mathbf{y}\in S_{L^{*}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
can once again simulate access to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. We can therefore remove/simulate
\mathcal{A}_{2}
’s first query altogether and end up with an adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{2}
which makes
q-1
queries and still succeeds with probability at least
\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}-\frac{1}{2p(\lambda)q(\lambda)}
.

Iterating this procedure for each query that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
makes, we end up with an adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{*}
which makes no queries and still succeeds with probability at least

\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}-q(\lambda)\cdot\frac{1}{2p(\lambda)q(\lambda)}\geq\frac{1}{2p(\lambda)},

which is non-negligible. But this violates the security of the special case, and so
\mathcal{A}_{2}
cannot succeed with non-negligible probability even when given
\{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\}_{\mathbf{y}\in S_{L^{*}}}
. We conclude that without
\{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\}_{\mathbf{y}\in S_{L^{*}}}
,
\{\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\}_{\mathbf{y}}
, and
\Pi
, and given only
L^{*}
, no (polynomially) query-bounded algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{2}
can succeed with non-negligible probability in producing a valid signature for a verification key outside of
S_{L^{*}}
. Since we argued earlier that
|S_{L^{*}}|\leq|L|+\lambda
with all but negligible probability, the proof is complete.
∎

With this, we can appeal to the result of
[
ÇGS25
]
:

Theorem 5.4

(Theorems 10 and 11,
[
ÇGS25
]
)
.

Suppose there exists a one-shot signature scheme for
\lambda
-bit messages with perfect correctness, (weak) signature security, and strong incompressibility security. Then there exists a quantum key-fire scheme for signing with perfect signing correctness, perfect strong cloning correctness, and key-untelegraphability security. Moreover, assuming the existence of post-quantum one-way functions, the key-fire scheme can be made efficient (with computational security) if the one-shot signature scheme is efficient.

Corollary 5.5
.

Relative to a classical oracle, there exists a public-key quantum key-fire scheme for signing with perfect signing correctness, perfect strong cloning correctness, and key-untelegraphability security. Moreover, assuming the existence of post-quantum one-way functions, the key-fire scheme can be made efficient (with computational security).

Proof.

The corollary follows from combining Lemmas
5.1
and
5.3
with Theorem
5.4
. The efficient version follows from observing that one can efficiently simulate the OSS scheme by replacing the permutation
\Pi
and random function
F
with a post-quantum PRP and PRF, which exist assuming post-quantum one-way functions exist
[
Zha21a
,
Zha25b
]
.
∎

Corollary 5.6

(Combining Corollary
5.5
and
[
ÇGS25
]
)
.

Relative to a classical oracle, there exists:

•

a public-key quantum fire scheme with perfect verification correctness, perfect strong cloning correctness, and untelegraphability security,

•

a public-key quantum key-fire scheme with untelegraphability security for any classically-unlearnable functionality with perfect cloning correctness, perfect evaluation correctness, and key-untelegraphability security,

•

a public-key encryption scheme with perfect correctness and perfectly clonable yet unbounded-leakage-resilient secret keys (and classical public keys and ciphertexts).

Moreover, assuming the existence of post-quantum one-way functions, all of these schemes can be made efficient (with computational security).

Appendix A
Cryptographic Definitions and Lemmas

Definition 5

(Puncturable PRFs)
.

A puncturable pseudorandom function (P-PRF) is a triple of efficient algorithms
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{Punc},\mathsf{Eval})
with an associated output-length function
m(\lambda)
with the following interface:

•

\mathsf{F}:\{0,1\}^{\lambda}\times\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{m(\lambda)}
is a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm.

•

\mathsf{Punc}(k,S)
is a
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithm which takes as input a key
k\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and a set of points
S\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
. It outputs a punctured key
k\{S\}
.

•

\mathsf{Eval}(k\{S\},x)
is a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm.

The algorithms satisfy the following correctness and security properties:

•

Correctness:
For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
S\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
,
k\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
,
x\notin S
, and
k\{S\}
in the support of
\mathsf{Punc}(k,S)
, we have that

\mathsf{Eval}(k\{S\},x)=\mathsf{F}(k,x)~.

•

(Post-Quantum) Security:
A puncturable PRF is
(f,\epsilon)
-secure for some functions
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon:\mathbb{N}\to[0,1]
if for every
f(\lambda)
-time quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, the following experiment with
\mathcal{A}
outputs
1
with probability at most
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon(\lambda)
:

–

\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda})
produces a set
S\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
.

–

The experiment chooses a random
k\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and computes
k\{S\}\leftarrow\mathsf{Punc}(k,S)
. For each
x\in S
, it also sets
y_{x}^{0}:=\mathsf{F}(k,x)
and samples
y_{x}^{1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{m(\lambda)}
uniformly at random. Then, it chooses a random bit
b
. It finally gives
k\{S\}
and
\{(x,y_{x}^{b})\}_{x\in S}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

–

\mathcal{A}
outputs a guess
b^{\prime}
for
b
. The experiment outputs
1
if and only if
b^{\prime}=b
.

Concretely, a sub-exponentially secure P-PRF scheme would be one for which there exists a positive real constant
c>0
such that the scheme is
(2^{\lambda^{c}},\frac{1}{2^{\lambda^{c}}})
-secure.

Definition 6

(Indistinguishability Obfuscation (iO))
.

An indistinguishability obfuscator for Boolean circuits is a probabilistic polynomial-time algorithm
\mathsf{iO}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
with the following properties:

•

Correctness:
For all
\lambda,s\in\mathbb{N}
, Boolean circuits
C
of size at most
s
, and all inputs
x
,

\Pr[\mathsf{O}_{C}(x)=C(x):\mathsf{O}_{C}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{s},C)]=1.

•

(Post-Quantum) Security:
An iO scheme is
(f,\epsilon)
-secure for some functions
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon:\mathbb{N}\to[0,1]
if for every
f(\lambda)
-time quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, and every two classical circuits
C_{0},C_{1}
with the same functionality and size at most
s
,

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{O}_{b})=b\end{array}\;:\;\begin{array}[]{l}b\leftarrow\{0,1\}\\
\mathsf{O}_{b}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{s},C_{b})\end{array}\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon(\lambda)

Concretely, a sub-exponentially secure iO scheme would be one for which there exists a positive real constant
c>0
such that the scheme is
(2^{\lambda^{c}},\frac{1}{2^{\lambda^{c}}})
-secure. When the upper bound on the size of the circuits is clear from the context, we will omit the size parameter
1^{s}
from the input to the obfuscator.

We will use the following lemma from
[
CLTV15
]
:

Lemma A.1

(Adapted from Theorem 2,
[
CLTV15
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a finite set and let
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
be two distribution samplers that take as input an index
x
and some random coins
r
, and output a sample.
Suppose that given the samplers
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the distributions
\mathcal{D}_{0}(x;\cdot)
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}(x;\cdot)
are
(f_{D},\delta_{D})
-indistinguishable.

Let
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{Punc},\mathsf{Eval})
and
(\mathsf{F}^{\prime},\mathsf{Punc}^{\prime},\mathsf{Eval}^{\prime})
be
(f_{\mathsf{F}},\delta_{\mathsf{F}})
-secure puncturable PRFs and let
\mathsf{iO}
be a
(f_{\mathsf{iO}},\delta_{\mathsf{iO}})
-secure iO scheme.
Let

E_{0,k_{0}}(x):=\mathcal{D}_{0}(x;\mathsf{F}(k_{0},x))\hskip 7.22743pt\mbox{and}\hskip 7.22743ptE_{1,k_{1}}(x):=\mathcal{D}_{1}(x;\mathsf{F}^{\prime}(k_{1},x))~,

and let

\widetilde{E}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{s},E_{0,k_{0}})\hskip 7.22743pt\mbox{and}\hskip 7.22743pt\widetilde{E}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{s},E_{1,k_{1}})

be the obfuscated programs for a sufficiently large polynomial
s
and uniformly random keys
k_{0}
and
k_{1}
. Then,
\widetilde{E}_{0}
and
\widetilde{E}_{1}
are
(f,\delta)
-indistinguishable, where

f=\min(f_{\mathsf{F}},f_{\mathsf{iO}},f_{D})\hskip 7.22743pt\mbox{and}\hskip 7.22743pt\epsilon=O(|\mathcal{X}|\cdot(\delta_{\mathsf{F}}+\delta_{\mathsf{iO}}+\delta_{D}))~.

Strictly speaking,
[
CLTV15
,
SZ25
]
only shows such a lemma for one P-PRF and two different keys, but the proof easily extends to the setting of two P-PRFs which use separate keys.

Definition 7

(Lossy Functions)
.

A lossy function (LF) family consists of classical algorithms
(\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F})
with the following interface:

•

\mathsf{pk}\leftarrow\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda},b,1^{\ell})
: a PPT algorithm that takes as input the security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, a bit
b
and a lossiness parameter
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
with
\ell\leq\lambda
. The algorithm outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}
.

•

y\leftarrow\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pk},x)
: a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm that takes as input the security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, the public key
\mathsf{pk}
and an input
x\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and outputs a string
y\in\{0,1\}^{m}
for some
m\geq\lambda
.

The algorithms satisfy the following guarantees:

•

Statistical Correctness for Injective Mode:
There exists a negligible function
\mathsf{negl}
such that for every
\lambda,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}|\mathsf{Img}(\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk},\cdot))|=2^{\lambda}\end{array}\;:\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{pk}\leftarrow\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda},0,1^{\ell})\end{array}\right]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

•

Statistical Correctness for Lossy Mode:
There exists a negligible function
\mathsf{negl}
such that for every
\lambda,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}|\mathsf{Img}(\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk},\cdot))|\leq 2^{\ell}\end{array}\;:\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{pk}\leftarrow\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda},1,1^{\ell})\end{array}\right]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

•

(Post-Quantum) Security:
An LF family is
(f,\epsilon)
-secure for some functions
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon:\mathbb{N}\to[0,1]
if for every
f(\lambda)
-time quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
,

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pk}_{b})=b\end{array}\;:\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{pk}_{b}\leftarrow\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda},b,1^{\ell})\end{array}\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon(\lambda)

Concretely, a sub-exponentially secure LF scheme would be for which there exists a positive real constant
c>0
such that the scheme is
(2^{\lambda^{c}},\frac{1}{2^{\lambda^{c}}})
-secure.

Definition 8

(Permutable PRPs,
[
SZ25
]
)
.

Let
G=\{G_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be a collection where each
G_{N}
is a set of permutations over
[N]
. An output-permutable PRP (OP-PRP) for
G
is a tuple of algorithms
(\Pi,\Pi^{-1},\mathsf{Permute},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Eval}^{-1})
with the following interface:

•

Efficient Permutations:
For any key
k\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and any desired “block size”
N
,
\Pi(k,\cdot)
is an efficiently computable permutation on
[N]
with
\Pi^{-1}(k,\cdot)
being its efficiently computable inverse. The same is true of
\mathsf{Eval}
and
\mathsf{Eval}^{-1}
.

•

Output Permuting:

\mathsf{Permute}(k,\Gamma,c)
is a deterministic polynomial-time procedure which takes as input a key
k\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
, the circuit description of a permutation
\Gamma\in G_{N}
, and a bit
c
. It outputs a permuted key
k^{\Gamma,c}
.

The algorithms satisfy the following guarantees:

•

Output Permuted Correctness:
For all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
,
c\in\{0,1\}
,
\Gamma\in G_{N}
, and
x,z\in[N]
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Eval}(k^{\Gamma,c},x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Pi(k,x)&\text{if $c=0$}\\
\Gamma(\Pi(k,x))&\text{if $c=1$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Eval}^{-1}(k^{\Gamma,c},z)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Pi^{-1}(k,z)&\text{if $c=0$}\\
\Pi^{-1}(k,\Gamma^{-1}(z))&\text{if $c=1$}\end{cases}.

with probability
1
. We call
k^{\Gamma,c}\leftarrow\mathsf{Permute}(k,\Gamma,c)
a permuted key.

•

(Post-Quantum) Security:

An OP-PRP family is
(f,\epsilon)
-secure for some functions
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon:\mathbb{N}\to[0,1]
if every
f(\lambda)
-time quantum interactive algorithm
\mathcal{A}
outputs
1
with probability at most
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon(\lambda)
in the following experiment:

–

\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda})
produces a block size
N
(in binary) and the description of a permutation
\Gamma\in G_{N}
.

–

The experiment chooses a random
k\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and a random bit
c\in\{0,1\}
. It gives
k^{\Gamma,c}\leftarrow\mathsf{Permute}(k,\Gamma,c)
to
\mathcal{A}
.

–

\mathcal{A}
produces a guess
c^{\prime}
for
c
. The experiment outputs
1
if
c^{\prime}=c
.

Concretely, a sub-exponentially secure OP-PRP scheme would be one such that there exists a positive real constant
c>0
such that the scheme is
(2^{\lambda^{c}},\frac{1}{2^{\lambda^{c}}})
-secure.

Remark A.2

(From
[
SZ25
]
)
.

We will often overload notation, and use the same symbol for both
\Pi
and
\mathsf{Eval}
(and corresponding symbols for
\Pi^{-1}
and
\mathsf{Eval}^{-1}
), when clear from context whether we are using a permuted or normal key. In this case, an OP-PRP would be just a triple
(\Pi,\Pi^{-1},\mathsf{Permute})
.

We say that a PRP is an input permutable (IP-)PRP if we apply
\Gamma
to the inputs rather than the outputs of
\Pi
(and accordingly apply
\Gamma^{-1}
to the output, and not the input, of
\Pi^{-1}
). A PRP which is simultaneously an IP-PRP and OP-PRP is called a permutable PRP (P-PRP).

Definition 9

(Decomposable Permutations,
[
SZ25
]
)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Gamma
a permutation on
[N]
, and let
T,s:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
. We say that
\Gamma
is
(T(N),s(N))
-decomposable if there exists a sequence of permutations
\Gamma_{0},\ldots,\Gamma_{T(N)}
such that:

1.

\Gamma_{0}
is the identity,
and
\Gamma_{T(N)}=\Gamma
.

2.

Each
\Gamma_{i},\Gamma_{i}^{-1}
has circuit size at most
s(N)
.

3.

For each
i
, either (a)
\Gamma_{i}=\Gamma_{i-1}
or (b) there exists a
z_{i}\in[N]
such that
\Gamma_{i}=\Gamma_{i-1}\circ(z_{i}\kern 5.0ptz_{i}+1)
. Here
(z_{i}\kern 5.0ptz_{i}+1)
is the neighbor-swap permutation for
z_{i}
, which swaps between
z_{i}
and
z_{i}+1\pmod{N}
, and acts as the identity on all other elements in
[N]
.

In the uniform setting, we will additionally ask that there is a uniform polynomial-time (quantum) algorithm which, given the description of
\Gamma
and
i
, constructs both
z_{i}
(if it exists) and the circuits for
\Gamma_{i},\Gamma_{i}^{-1}
.

We refer the reader to
[
SZ25
]
for examples of decomposable permutations; we will only need to consider two such classes of efficiently decomposable permutations.

Lemma A.3

(
[
SZ25
]
)
.

The following classes of permutations are decomposable:

1.

Affine permutations
\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}
, where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times n}
is invertible and
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
, are
(O(2^{n}\times n^{2}),\mathsf{poly}(n))
-decomposable.

2.

If
N=N_{0}\cdot N_{1}
, and for every
v\in[N_{0}]
,
\Gamma_{v}
is a
(T,s)
-decomposable permutation on domain
[N_{1}]
, the controlled permutation
\Gamma
with domain
[N]:=[N_{0}]\times[N_{1}]
where
\Gamma
applies
\Gamma_{v}
to
[N_{1}]
conditioned on the element in
[N_{0}]
being
v
is
(T\cdot N_{0},s+\mathsf{poly}\log(N))
-decomposable.

Theorem A.4

(Theorem 39,
[
SZ25
]
)
.

Let
T
be any exponential function and
p
any polynomial. Assuming the existence of sub-exponentially-secure one-way functions and sub-exponentially-secure iO, there exists an P-PRP for the class of
(T,p)
-decomposable permutations. Moreover, the P-PRP is itself
(T,p)
-decomposable.

We also use the following lemma from
[
SZ25
]
which allows us to use OP-PRP’s in conjunction with indistinguishability obfuscation:

Lemma A.5

(Lemma 40,
[
SZ25
]
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a permutation, let
(\Pi,\Pi^{-1},\mathsf{Permute})
be an
(f_{\mathsf{P}},\delta_{\mathsf{P}})
-secure OP-PRP for a class of permutations which includes
\Gamma
, and let
\mathsf{iO}
be a
(f_{\mathsf{iO}},\delta_{\mathsf{iO}})
-secure iO scheme. Then for a sufficiently large polynomial
s
,

\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{s},P^{\Pi(k,\cdot),\Pi^{-1}(k,\cdot)})\approx_{\min(f_{\mathsf{P}},f_{\mathsf{iO}}),O(\delta_{\mathsf{P}}+\delta_{\mathsf{iO}})}\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{s},P^{\Gamma(\Pi(k,\cdot)),\Pi^{-1}(k,\Gamma^{-1}(\cdot))}),

where
k\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
is uniformly random.

Appendix B
Our Construction in the Plain Model

Like in
[
SZ25
]
, to instantiate Construction
4
, we replace the random permutation and functions with pseudorandom versions, and provide an obfuscation of the programs.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the statistical security parameter. We use the following parameters:

s:=16\cdot\lambda,r:=s\cdot(\lambda-1),\ell=\ell(\lambda):=\lambda,\hskip 7.22743pt\mbox{and}\hskip 7.22743ptn:=r+\ell+\frac{3}{2}\cdot s~.

Let
d:=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
be the expansion parameter,
\kappa:=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)\geq d
be the cryptographic security parameter, and
p:=\mathsf{poly}(\kappa)=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
be the size parameter, for some sufficiently large polynomials.

Let
\mathsf{iO}
be an iO scheme,
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{Punc},\mathsf{Eval})
a puncturable PRF, and
(\Pi,\Pi^{-1},\mathsf{Permute})
a permutable PRP for the class of all
(2^{\mathsf{poly}(\kappa)},\mathsf{poly}(\kappa))
-decomposable permutations.

Incompressible OSS Scheme in the Plain Model

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

Sample
k_{\mathsf{in}},k_{\mathsf{out}},k_{\mathsf{lin}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
.
\Pi(k_{\mathsf{in}},\cdot)
is a permutation with domain
\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\Pi(k_{\mathsf{out}},\cdot)
is a permutation with domain
\{0,1\}^{d}
, and
\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}},\cdot)
is a PRF with inputs in
\{0,1\}^{d}
that outputs some polynomial number of bits. Let
H(\cdot)
denote the first
r
output bits of
\Pi(k_{\mathsf{in}},\cdot)
and
J(\cdot)
denote the remaining
n-r
bits. For each
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
, let
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times\ell}
be a matrix,
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times(n-r-\ell)}
a matrix with full column-rank, and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be a vector, all generated pseudorandomly by the output of
\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}},\mathbf{y})
. Define
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)}
.

As the common reference string output
\mathsf{CRS}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})
where
\mathcal{P}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},P)
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},P^{-1})
, and
\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},D)
such that

•

P(x)=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot J(x)+\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
where
\mathbf{y}\leftarrow\Pi^{-1}(k_{\mathsf{out}},H(x)||0^{d-r})
,

•

P^{-1}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u})=\begin{cases}\Pi^{-1}(k_{\mathsf{in}},\mathbf{w}||\mathbf{z})&\exists\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z}:(\Pi(k_{\mathsf{out}},\mathbf{y})=\mathbf{w}||0^{d-r})\\
&\land\hskip 3.61371pt(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{u})\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

•

D(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}\text{ and }j\in[\ell+1]\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Let
\mathsf{Hash}(\mathsf{CRS},x)
refer to the first
d
bits of
\mathcal{P}(x)
. We will take the message space
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
to be the set of all strings
m\in\{0,1\}^{\ell(\lambda)}
.

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Gen}(\mathsf{CRS})
:

Prepare the state
\ket{+}^{\otimes n}=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\ket{x}
and apply
\mathcal{P}
. Measure
\mathsf{Hash}
to get a string
\mathsf{pk}:=\mathbf{y}
and state
\sum_{x:\mathcal{P}(x)_{[1:d]}=\mathbf{y}}\ket{x}\ket*{\matA_\vecy\cdot\vecw_{\vecx} + \vecb_\vecy}
, where
\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{x}}
is
J(\mathbf{x})
interpreted as a vector in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}
. Apply
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y},\cdot)
to uncompute
x
, resulting in the state
\ket{\sk}:=(\mathbf{y},\sum_{x:\mathcal{P}(x)_{[1:d]}=\mathbf{y}}\ket*{\matA_\vecy\cdot\vecw_{\vecx} + \vecb_\vecy})
. Output
(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\sk})
.

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{CRS},\ket{\sk}\!,m)
:

Identical to the signing algorithm of Section
4
.

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{CRS},\mathsf{pk},m,\sigma)
:

Output 1 if
\sigma_{[1:\ell]}=m
and
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathsf{pk},\sigma)\neq\bot
; else, output 0.

Figure 4:
Incompressible OSS scheme in the plain model.

B.1
Perfect Correctness

Lemma B.1
.

For all security parameters
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and messages
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
,

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{CRS}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(\mathsf{CRS})\end{subarray}}\left[\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{CRS},\mathsf{pk},m,\sigma_{m})=1:\sigma_{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{CRS},\ket{\sk},m)\right]=1.

The perfect correctness of the signing algorithm follows from an identical argument to Section
4
.

B.2
Strong Security

We now show that our one-shot signature scheme is strongly secure. We use the same proof strategy as the plain model proof in
[
SZ25
]
, modifying components as needed. As such, we defer most proofs to Appendix
F
.

We begin by considering what happens when we “bloat” the dual oracle. Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda},n,r,\ell,s)
denote the following modified setup protocol. It samples a distribution over
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}
.
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
are defined identically; however, our new dual program
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}:=\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},D^{\prime})
will be a relaxed version of the original dual program
\mathcal{D}:=\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},D)
:

D^{\prime}(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+1+s:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}=0^{n-r-s-j+1}\text{ and }j\in[\ell+1],\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Observe that
\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda},n,r,\ell,0)
is precisely the distribution of programs in Construction
4
. The following lemma states that any adversary
\mathcal{A}
that finds a collision given access to
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{D}
, is also likely to find a collision
7
7

7

Technically, the lemma states that
\mathcal{A}
produces something which is stronger than a collision.
given access to
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\lambda,\kappa,n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
,
\kappa\geq n-r-s-\ell
, and
n=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
. Suppose there is a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
with complexity
T_{\mathcal{A}}
such that

\Pr[(y_{0}=y_{1})\land(x_{0}\neq x_{1}):\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})&\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda},n,r,\ell,0)\\
(x_{0},x_{1})&\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})\\
(y_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})&\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(x_{b})\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon,

for some non-negligible function
\varepsilon:=\varepsilon(\lambda)
.

Suppose that the primitives used in Construction
4
are
(f(\kappa),\frac{1}{f(\kappa)})
-secure for some sub-exponential
f(\kappa):=2^{\kappa^{\delta}}
for some constant real number
\delta>0
. Suppose there exists a constant
c>0
such that for
w:=\kappa^{c\cdot\delta}
and
t:=(n-r-s)
, all of the following are true:

1.

\frac{(2^{r}+T_{\mathcal{A}})\cdot n^{2}/\varepsilon}{f(\kappa)}\leq o(1)
,

2.

2^{w}\cdot\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\cdot(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell})\leq o(1)
, and

3.

\frac{2^{w}\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n,\kappa)\cdot T_{\mathcal{A}}/\varepsilon}{f(n-r-s-\ell)}\leq o(1)
.

Then,

\Pr[\begin{array}[]{l}(y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y})\hskip 5.0pt\land\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})\notin\ColSpan}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[1:\ell]}}&{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}}\end{bmatrix}\right)\end{array}:\begin{aligned} {\scriptstyle(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda},n,r,\ell,s)}\\
{\scriptstyle(x_{0},x_{1})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}\\
{\scriptstyle(y_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(x_{b})}\end{aligned}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{512n^{2}}.

We now show that given an adversary which succeeds with a bloated dual program, one can actually entirely simulate the dual with only access to the primal program obfuscations.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\lambda,\kappa,n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
,
\kappa\geq n-r-s-\ell
, and
n=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
. Suppose there is a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
with complexity
T_{\mathcal{A}}
such that

\Pr[\begin{array}[]{l}(y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y})\hskip 5.0pt\land\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})\notin\ColSpan}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[1:\ell]}}&{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}}\end{bmatrix}\right)\end{array}:\begin{aligned} {\scriptstyle(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda},n,r,\ell,s)}\\
{\scriptstyle(x_{0},x_{1})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}\\
{\scriptstyle(y_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(x_{b})}\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon.

Suppose that the primitives used in Construction
4
are
(f(\kappa),\frac{1}{f(\kappa)})
-secure for some sub-exponential
f(\kappa):=2^{\kappa^{\delta}}
for some constant real number
\delta>0
,
\frac{2^{r}/\varepsilon}{f(\kappa)}\leq o(1)
, and
\frac{T_{\mathcal{A}}+\mathsf{poly}(\kappa)}{f(\kappa)}\leq o(1)
. Then there is a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{B}
running in time
T_{\mathcal{A}}+\mathsf{poly}(\kappa)
such that

\Pr[(\overline{y}_{0}=\overline{y}_{1})\land(\overline{x}_{0}\neq\overline{x}_{1}):\begin{aligned} (\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1},\overline{\mathcal{D}})&\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda},r+s,r,0,0)\\
(\overline{x}_{0},\overline{x}_{1})&\leftarrow\mathcal{B}^{\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}}\\
(\overline{y}_{b},\overline{\mathbf{u}}_{b})&\leftarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{x}_{b})\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon/2.

The proofs of Lemmas
B.2
and
B.3
closely follow the proofs of Lemmas 54 and 55 in
[
SZ25
]
, so we defer their proofs to Appendix
F
.

Having reduced to the dual-free case, we can now directly use the collision resistance argument of
[
SZ25
]
.

Theorem B.4

(Adapted from Theorem 56,
[
SZ25
]
)
.

Let
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s\mid r
. Suppose the post-quantum sub-exponential hardness of LWE and that
d
is a sufficiently large polynomial in
\lambda
.

Then, for every
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, the probability that
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1})
outputs a collision in
\mathsf{Hash}
, where
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1})
is sampled from
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda},r+s,r,0,0)
, is
\mathsf{negl}((r+s)/s)
.

By combining Lemma
B.2
, Lemma
B.3
, and Theorem
B.4
, we see that our hash function is collision-resistant.

Corollary B.5
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and set all other parameters accordingly to Construction
4
. Then, for every
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathcal{A}
,

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{CRS}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(x_{0},x_{1})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathsf{CRS})\end{subarray}}\left[(x_{0}\neq x_{1})\land(\mathsf{Hash}(\mathsf{CRS},x_{0})=\mathsf{Hash}(\mathsf{CRS},x_{1}))\right]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

We can now conclude that our one-shot signature scheme is strongly secure, from an identical argument as in the proof of Corollary
4.10
.

Corollary B.6
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and set all other parameters accordingly to Construction
4
. Then, for every
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathcal{A}
,

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{CRS}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{pk},m_{0},\sigma_{0},m_{1},\sigma_{1})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathsf{CRS})\end{subarray}}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}(m_{0},\sigma_{0})\neq(m_{1},\sigma_{1})\hskip 5.0pt\land\\
\forall b\in\{0,1\},\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{CRS},\mathsf{pk},m_{b},\sigma_{b})=1\end{array}\right]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Appendix C
Signing Polynomial-Length Messages

With the ability to sign
\lambda
-sized messages with signatures of size
O(\lambda^{2})
and a single signing key, we show how to extend this to messages of arbitrary polynomial length without blowing up the signature size. The idea is simple (in both the oracle and plain models): we will use the classic
hash-and-sign
approach, either with a random oracle or with a collision-resistant hash function.

C.1
Oracle Model Construction

In this section, we describe how to extend Construction
2
to sign arbitrary polynomial length messages using a random oracle.

Construction 5

: Oracle Model Hash-and-Sign OSS

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter, and let
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Ver})
be any oracle-based one-shot signature scheme for messages of length
\lambda
which uses the oracle distribution
\mathcal{O}\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
. Let
\mathcal{H}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
be a random oracle drawn from the distribution
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
. We omit
\mathsf{Setup}
and let a random sample from
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
play the role of the CRS. Our other algorithms are as follows:

•

\widetilde{\mathsf{Gen}}^{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{O}}
: Output
(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}^{\mathcal{O}}
.

•

\widetilde{\mathsf{Sign}}^{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{O}}(\ket{\sk},m)
: Apply
\mathcal{H}(m)
to get a message
m^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
. Output
\sigma\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{O}}(\ket{\sk},m^{\prime})
.

•

\widetilde{\mathsf{Ver}}^{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{pk},m,\sigma)
: Apply
\mathcal{H}(m)
to get a message
m^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
. Output
b\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{pk},m^{\prime},\sigma)
.

Correctness follows directly from the equivocality of the base OSS scheme. We now show that the hash-and-sign scheme is still strongly secure.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Ver})
be any one-shot signature scheme which uses the oracle distribution
\{\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
with collision-resistance
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{OSS}}(q,\lambda)
against
q
-query quantum algorithms for a security parameter
\lambda
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an oracle aided
q
-query quantum algorithm. Then, for all
\lambda
,

\Pr[\begin{array}[]{l}(m_{0},\sigma_{0})\neq(m_{1},\sigma_{1})\hskip 5.0pt\land\\
\widetilde{\mathsf{Ver}}^{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{pk},m_{0},\sigma_{0})=1\ \land\\
\widetilde{\mathsf{Ver}}^{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{pk},m_{1},\sigma_{1})=1\end{array}:\begin{aligned} \mathcal{H}\leftarrow\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},\mathcal{O}&\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}\\
(\mathsf{pk},m_{b},\sigma_{b})&\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{O}}\\
\mbox{for $b\in$}&\mbox{$\{0,1\}$}\end{aligned}]\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{OSS}}(q,\lambda)+O\left(\frac{q^{3}}{2^{\lambda}}\right),

where
\mathcal{H}\leftarrow\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
is a random oracle.

Proof.

The lemma follows from a straightforward hybrid argument. Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
produces a collision
(\mathsf{pk},m_{0},\sigma_{0},m_{1},\sigma_{1})
. If
\mathcal{H}(m_{0})=\mathcal{H}(m_{1})
and
m_{0}\neq m_{1}
, this produces a collision in
\mathcal{H}
. Otherwise, either
\mathcal{H}(m_{0})\neq\mathcal{H}(m_{1})
or
m_{0}=m_{1}
and thus
\sigma_{0}\neq\sigma_{1}
; in either case,
\mathcal{A}
produces two valid message-signature pairs
(\mathcal{H}(m_{0}),\sigma_{0})\neq(\mathcal{H}(m_{0}),\sigma_{1})
for the underlying one-shot signature scheme. Since
\mathcal{A}
makes
q
total queries, it makes at most
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{H}
each, and so it can produce two such message-signature pairs with probability at most
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{OSS}}(q,\lambda)
by assumption and a collision in
\mathcal{H}
with probability at most
O\left(\frac{q^{3}}{2^{\lambda}}\right)
by standard collision lower bounds
[
Zha15
]
.
∎

Instantiating this hash-and-sign construction with the base one-shot signature scheme from Section
4
gives a strongly secure scheme for signing arbitrary polynomial-length messages:

Corollary C.2
.

Let
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Ver})
be the one-shot signature scheme from Section
4
, and define the one-shot signature scheme
(\widetilde{\mathsf{Gen}},\widetilde{\mathsf{Sign}},\widetilde{\mathsf{Ver}})
accordingly. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an oracle aided
q
-query quantum algorithm. Then,

{\scriptstyle\Pr[\begin{array}[]{l}{\scriptstyle(m_{0},\sigma_{0})\neq(m_{1},\sigma_{1})\hskip 5.0pt\land}\\
{\scriptstyle\forall b\in\{0,1\},\widetilde{\mathsf{Ver}}^{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{pk},m_{b},\sigma_{b})=1}\end{array}:\begin{aligned} {\scriptstyle\mathcal{H}\leftarrow\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{n,r,\ell}}\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathsf{pk},m_{0},\sigma_{0},m_{1},\sigma_{1})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}}}\end{aligned}]\leq O\left(\frac{\lambda^{3}\cdot q^{3}\cdot n^{3}}{2^{\lambda}}\right),}

where
\mathcal{H}\leftarrow\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
is a random oracle.

Perfect correctness of
(\widetilde{\mathsf{Gen}},\widetilde{\mathsf{Sign}},\widetilde{\mathsf{Ver}})
follows immediately from the perfect correctness of
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Ver})
.

C.2
Plain Model Construction

In this section, we describe how to extend Construction
4
to sign arbitrary polynomial length messages by instantiating the random oracle with an arbitrary domain collision-resistant hash function.

Construction 6

: Plain Model Hash-and-Sign OSS

Let
\lambda
be the security parameter. Let
(\mathsf{Setup}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Gen}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Sign}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Ver}_{\lambda})
be any one-shot signature scheme which signs messages of length
\ell(\lambda)
, and let
\{H_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell(\lambda)}
be any family of collision-resistant hash functions. Our hash-and-sign based one-shot signature scheme is as follows:

•

\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda})
: Sample
\mathsf{CRS}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and hash function
h\leftarrow H_{\lambda}
. Output
\mathsf{CRS}^{\prime}=(h,\mathsf{CRS})
.

•

\widetilde{\mathsf{Gen}}(\mathsf{CRS}^{\prime}=(h,\mathsf{CRS}))
: Output
(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(\mathsf{CRS})
.

•

\widetilde{\mathsf{Sign}}(\mathsf{CRS}^{\prime}=(h,\mathsf{CRS}),\ket{\sk},m)
: Compute
m^{\prime}:=h(m)\in\{0,1\}^{\ell(\lambda)}
. Output
\sigma\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathsf{CRS},\ket{\sk},m^{\prime})
.

•

\widetilde{\mathsf{Ver}}(\mathsf{CRS}^{\prime}=(h,\mathsf{CRS}),\mathsf{pk},m,\sigma)
: Compute
m^{\prime}:=h(m)\in\{0,1\}^{\ell(\lambda)}
. Output
b\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{CRS},\mathsf{pk},m^{\prime},\sigma)
.

The proof of the following lemma is identical to the proof of Lemma
C.1
and follows from a straightforward hybrid argument.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
(\mathsf{Setup}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Gen}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Sign}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Ver}_{\lambda})
and
\{H_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell(\lambda)}
be any one-shot signature scheme which signs messages of length
\ell(\lambda)
and any family of hash functions with collision resistance
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{OSS}}(\lambda)
and
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{CRHF}}(\lambda)
, respectively, against
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms for a security parameter
\lambda
. Then, for all
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms
\mathcal{A}
,

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{CRS}^{\prime}\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{pk},m_{0},m_{1},\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathsf{CRS}^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\bigg[\begin{array}[]{l}{\scriptstyle(m_{0},\sigma_{0})\neq(m_{1},\sigma_{1})\hskip 5.0pt\land}\\
{\scriptstyle\forall b\in\{0,1\},\widetilde{\mathsf{Ver}}(\mathsf{CRS}^{\prime},\mathsf{pk},m_{b},\sigma_{b})=1}\end{array}\bigg]\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{OSS}}(\lambda)+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{CRHF}}(\lambda).

Instantiating the one-shot signature scheme with the scheme in Section
B
and any LWE-based collision-resistant hash function gives the following corollary:

Corollary C.4
.

Assuming (1) subexponentially-secure indistinguishability obfuscation, (2) subexponentially-secure one-way functions, and (3) (polynomially-secure) LWE with a subexponential noise-modulus ratio, there exists a secure OSS scheme with perfect correctness and
O(\lambda^{2})
-bit signatures and
O(\lambda^{2})
-qubit signing keys for
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
-bit messages.

Appendix D
Subspace Hiding Lemmas

We begin by stating two lemmas on equivalent ways to sample superspaces which will be used throughout this work.

Lemma D.1
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
, and let
S_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\ell+1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be subspaces where
S_{j}
has dimension
r+j-1
. Fix any full column-rank matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)}
such that
S_{j}^{\perp}:=\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]})
.

Then, the following distributions are statistically equivalent:

1.

Sample a random vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\setminus S_{\ell+1}
and output
(T_{1,1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1,1})
, where
T_{j,1}:=S_{j}\cup(S_{j}+\mathbf{v})
for all
j
.

2.

Sample a random vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\setminus S_{\ell+1}
. Compute
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}
. Output
(T_{1,2},\ldots,T_{\ell+1,2})
, where
T_{j,2}:=\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\mid(\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1})\lor(\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=\mathbf{y}_{[j:n-r]})\}
for all
j
.

3.

Sample a random matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times\ell}
and compute
\mathbf{C}:=\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}
. Sample a random vector
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r-\ell}\setminus\{0\}
. Output
(T_{1,3},\ldots,T_{\ell+1,3})
, where
T_{j,3}:=\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\mid(\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1})\lor(\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{\ell-j+1}||\mathbf{z})\}
for all
j
.

Proof.

We first show that Distributions
1
and
2
are equivalent distributions. By hypothesis, we have that

\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\mid\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}\}=\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\mid\mathbf{w}\in\ColSpan\left(\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}\right)^{\perp}\}=S_{j}.

Fix any vector
\mathbf{v}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\setminus S_{\ell+1}
. Consider any index
j
and any vector
\mathbf{x}\in S_{j}+\mathbf{v}
. By definition,
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{s}_{j}+\mathbf{v}
for some vector
\mathbf{s}_{j}\in S_{j}
. Thus,

\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=(\mathbf{s}_{j}+\mathbf{v})^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=\mathbf{s}_{j}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}+\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=\mathbf{y}_{[j:n-r]},

so
\mathbf{x}\in\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\mid\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=\mathbf{y}_{[j:n-r]}\}
. In other words,
T_{j,1}\subseteq T_{j,2}
for all
j
. But observe that since
\mathbf{A}
has full column-rank,
T_{j,2}
is a
(r+j)
-dimensional subspace, while
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\setminus S_{\ell+1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\setminus S_{j}
, so
T_{j,1}
is also a
(r+j)
-dimensional subspace. This means that for a given
\mathbf{v}
,
(T_{1,1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1,1})=(T_{1,2},\ldots,T_{\ell+1,2})
, so Distributions
1
and
2
are equivalent distributions (over random
\mathbf{v}
).

We now show that Distributions
2
and
3
are equivalent distributions. First, observe that for
any
matrix
\mathbf{B}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\left(\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{[j:n-r]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\left(\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbf{A}^{[1:\ell]}+\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}\cdot\mathbf{B})&\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{[j:n-r]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbf{A}^{[j:\ell]}+\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{[j:\ell]})&\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}+\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{[j:\ell]})&0^{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}.

Thus, if
\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}+\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{[j:\ell]})&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=0^{n-r-j+1}+\begin{bmatrix}(0^{n-r-\ell}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{[j:\ell]})&0^{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}=0^{n-r-j+1}.

But the matrix
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}
is its own inverse, so we also have that
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{C}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}
and thus

\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}+\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{[j:\ell]})&0^{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}.

Therefore, if
\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
, it must also be the case that
\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
.

Since
\mathbf{A}
is full column-rank and
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}
is full-rank for all
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{C}
is always full column-rank. Thus, for all
\mathbf{v}
,
T_{j,2}
and
T_{j,3}
are
(r+j)
-dimensional subspaces. It therefore suffices to show the following distributions are equivalent:

•

Sample a random vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\setminus S_{\ell+1}
. Compute
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}
. Output
(U_{1},\ldots,U_{\ell+1})
, where
U_{j}:=\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\mid\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=\mathbf{y}_{[j:n-r]}\}
for all
j
.

•

Sample a random matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times\ell}
and compute
\mathbf{C}:=\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}
. Sample a random vector
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r-\ell}\setminus\{0\}
. Output
(U^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,U^{\prime}_{\ell+1})
, where
U^{\prime}_{j}:=\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\mid\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{\ell-j+1}||\mathbf{z}\}
for all
j
.

Note that since
\mathbf{A}
has full column-rank, the multiplication-by-
\mathbf{A}
map
L:\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}
, defined as taking
\mathbf{v}\mapsto\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{A}
, is
2^{r}
-to-1. Since
L(S_{\ell+1})=\spn\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\}
, we see that
L(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\setminus S_{\ell+1})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}\setminus\spn\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\}
. In other words, in the first distribution,
\mathbf{y}
is a uniformly random vector in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}
whose last
n-r-\ell
coordinates are not all zero.

We now consider the second distribution: for a fixed matrix
\mathbf{B}
and index
j
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0^{\ell-j+1}&\mathbf{z}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\exists\mathbf{s}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{j-1}:\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{s}_{j}&0^{\ell-j+1}&\mathbf{z}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\exists\mathbf{s}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{j-1}:\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{s}_{j}&0^{\ell-j+1}&\mathbf{z}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\exists\mathbf{s}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{j-1}:\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{s}_{j}&0^{\ell-j+1}&\mathbf{z}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\exists\mathbf{s}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{j-1}:\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{s}_{j}&0^{\ell-j+1}\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{B})&\mathbf{z}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathbf{w}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{B})&\mathbf{z}\end{bmatrix}\right)_{[j:n-r]}.

For any fixed nonzero vector
\mathbf{z}
, over the randomness of
\mathbf{B}
, each column of
\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{B}
is a i.i.d. random bit, so the joint distribution of
\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{B})&\mathbf{z}\end{bmatrix}
for a random nonzero
\mathbf{z}
and random matrix
\mathbf{B}
is precisely a uniformly random vector in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}
whose last
n-r-\ell
coordinates are not all zero. Thus,
(U_{1},\ldots,U_{\ell+1})\equiv(U^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,U^{\prime}_{\ell+1})
which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.2
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
. Fix any matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times\ell}
and any full column-rank matrix
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times(n-r-\ell)}
; this defines
\mathbf{A}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{C}\end{bmatrix}
and
S_{j}:=\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]})^{\perp}
.

Then, the following distributions over subspaces are statistically equivalent:

1.

Sample
s
random linearly independent vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{s}
such that

\spn\{\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{s}\}\cap S_{\ell+1}=\{0\}.

Compute
T_{j}:=\spn\{S_{j},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{s}\}
for all
j
. Output
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
.

2.

Sample a random full-rank matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times(n-r-\ell)}
and random matrix
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times\ell}
. Compute
\overline{\mathbf{A}}:=\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}&\mathbf{M}\end{bmatrix}
and

T_{j}:=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[j:\ell]}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{\perp}.

Output
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
.

Proof.

We first prove the lemma for the case of
s=1
. This means that Distribution
1
consist of sampling any vector
\mathbf{v}\notin S_{\ell+1}
and setting
T_{j}=\spn\{S_{j},\mathbf{v}\}
. We claim that for fixed
S_{j}
, there are exactly
2^{n-r}-2^{\ell}
possible sets of subspaces
T_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq T_{\ell+1}
. This follows from two facts:

•

For any two vectors
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\notin S_{\ell+1}
such that
\spn\{S_{1},\mathbf{v}\}=\spn\{S_{1},\mathbf{w}\}
, we have
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}\in S_{1}\subseteq S_{j}
for all
j
and so
\spn\{S_{j},\mathbf{v}\}=\spn\{S_{j},\mathbf{w}\}
. Thus, determining
T_{1}
fixes all other subspaces
T_{2},\ldots,T_{\ell+1}
. It is also not hard to see that among all vectors
\mathbf{v}\notin S_{\ell+1}
consistent with a superspace
T_{j+1}
, there are exactly two possible superspaces
T_{j}:=\spn\{S_{j},\mathbf{v}\}
.

•

T_{1}
is always a dimension
(r+1)
superspace of
S_{1}
such that
T_{1}\subsetneq S_{\ell+1}
. There are at most
2^{n-r}-2^{\ell}
such superspaces. On the other hand, there are
2^{n}-2^{r+\ell}
vectors
\mathbf{v}\notin S_{\ell+1}
, and at most
|S_{1}|=2^{r}
will result in the same
T_{1}
, so there are at least
\frac{2^{n}-2^{r+\ell}}{2^{r}}=2^{n-r}-2^{\ell}
such superspaces.

Moreover, these two facts imply that Distribution
1
is the uniform distribution over all possible sets of subspaces
T_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq T_{\ell+1}
such that
T_{j}=\spn\{S_{j},\mathbf{v}\}
for some vector
\mathbf{v}\notin S_{\ell+1}
.

We now take a look at Distribution
2
. We claim that for any set of subspaces
T_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq T_{\ell+1}
produced in Distribution
2
, there exists some vector
\mathbf{v}\notin S_{\ell+1}
such that
T_{j}=S_{j}\cup(S_{j}+\mathbf{v})
. Note that this is equivalent to the condition that there is some vector
\mathbf{v}\in\bigcap_{j=1}^{\ell+1}T_{j}\setminus S_{j}
.

Fix any full-rank matrix
\mathbf{M}
and matrix
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
. We have that

\overline{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}&\mathbf{M}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{C}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}&\mathbf{M}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{M}^{\prime}&\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{M}\end{bmatrix},

and
T_{j}=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[j:\ell]}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[\ell+2:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{\perp}
. This means that
\mathbf{v}\in T_{j}
if and only if
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[j:\ell]}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[\ell+2:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}=0^{n-r-j}
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\ColSpan\left(\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[j:n-r]}\right)

\displaystyle=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{0}\\
(\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{M}^{\prime})^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{M}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{C}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\ColSpan\left(\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}\right),

since
\mathbf{M}
is a full-rank matrix. Thus,
\mathbf{v}\in S_{j}
if and only if
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
. Therefore, an equivalent condition to
\mathbf{v}\in\bigcap_{j=1}^{\ell+1}T_{j}\setminus S_{j}
is if there exists a vector
\mathbf{v}
such that
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\overline{\mathbf{A}}=0^{\ell}||1||0^{n-r-\ell-1}
. But since
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{M}
are both full column-rank matrices, so must
\overline{\mathbf{A}}
, and so the multiplication-by-
\overline{\mathbf{A}}
is surjective and thus such a vector
\mathbf{v}
must exist. In particular, this establishes that the support of Distribution
2
is a subset of the support of Distribution
1
.

We move to considering the dual spaces
T_{\ell+1}^{\perp}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq T_{1}^{\perp}
. Note that

T_{\ell+1}^{\perp}=\ColSpan\left(\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[\ell+2:n-r]}\right)=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{M}^{[2:n-r-\ell]}\end{bmatrix}\right),

while

S_{\ell+1}^{\perp}=\ColSpan\left(\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}\right)=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}\end{bmatrix}\right),

and since
\mathbf{M}^{[2:n-r-\ell]}
is a random full column-rank matrix (as
\mathbf{M}
is random and full-rank),
T_{\ell+1}^{\perp}
is a uniformly random
(n-r-\ell-1)
-dimension subspace of
S_{\ell+1}^{\perp}
; note that there are
2^{n-r-\ell}-1
such subspaces.

Now let us fix any
\mathbf{M}
, which thereby fixes the last
n-r-\ell
columns of
\overline{\mathbf{A}}
and
T_{\ell+1}^{\perp}
. We claim that over the randomness of
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
,
(T_{1}^{\perp},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{\perp})
take on one of
2^{\ell}
possible sets of subspaces with equal probability. To see why this is the case, it suffices to show that for any index
j\in[\ell]
, after fixing the
(j+1)
’th through
\ell
’th column of
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
(which fixes
T_{j+1}^{\perp},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{\perp}
), picking the
j
’th column
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j}
of
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
at random results in one of two possible
T_{j}^{\perp}
’s with equal probability.

How is
T_{j}^{\perp}
defined with respect to
T_{j+1}^{\perp}
? Recall that

\displaystyle T_{j}^{\perp}

\displaystyle:=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[j:\ell]}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[\ell+2:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)=T_{j+1}^{\perp}\cup\left(T_{j+1}^{\perp}+\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[j]}\right)

\displaystyle=T_{j+1}^{\perp}\cup\left(T_{j+1}^{\perp}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}^{[j]}\\
\mathbf{B}^{[j]}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j}\end{bmatrix}\right),

where there are
2^{n-r-\ell}
possible vectors
\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j}
. For any two vectors
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j,1},\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j,2}
, the subspaces
T_{j,1}^{\perp}
and
T_{j,2}^{\perp}
are equal if and only if

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}\cdot(\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j,1}-\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j,2})\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\in T_{j+1}^{\perp}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}\cdot(\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j,1}-\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j,2})\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\in T_{\ell+1}^{\perp}=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{M}^{[2:n-r-\ell]}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j,1}-\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j,2}

\displaystyle\in\ColSpan\left(\mathbf{M}^{[2:n-r-\ell]}\right),

since columns
j+1
through
\ell
of
\overline{\mathbf{A}}
have a 1 somewhere in the first
\ell
coordinates and
\mathbf{C}
has full column-rank. Since
\mathbf{M}
has full rank and thus
\ColSpan(\mathbf{M}^{[2:n-r-\ell]})
has dimension
n-r-\ell-1
, this partitions the vectors
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r-\ell}
evenly into exactly two cosets. Thus,
T_{j}^{\perp}
takes on one of two possible values over the randomness of the
j
th column of
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
.

Thus,
(T_{1}^{\perp},\ldots,T_{\ell+1}^{\perp})
take on one of
2^{\ell}\cdot(2^{n-r-\ell}-1)=2^{n-r}-2^{\ell}
possible sets of subspaces with equal probability. Consequently, the output distribution
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
in Distribution
2
is also the uniform distribution over a support of size
2^{n-r}-2^{\ell}
. But we argued earlier that the support of Distribution
2
is a subset of the support of Distribution
1
, which has size
2^{n-r}-2^{\ell}
. Thus Distribution
2
is also the uniform distribution over the same
2^{n-r}-2^{\ell}
possible subspaces, which concludes the proof for the case of
s=1
.

For
s>1
, we assume the lemma is true for all smaller
s
. Consider the following recursive process:

1.

First, sample a random matrix
\mathbf{D}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times\ell}
and a random full-rank matrix
\mathbf{E}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times(n-r-\ell)}
. Compute the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{1}:=\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{1}&\mathbf{E}_{1}\end{bmatrix}
and subspaces
S^{\prime}_{j}:=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{1}^{[\ell+2:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{\perp}
.

2.

Continue by sampling a new random matrix
\mathbf{D}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell-1)\times\ell}
and random full-rank matrix
\mathbf{E}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell-1)\times(n-r-\ell-1)}
. Compute the matrix

\mathbf{A}_{2}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{[1:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{1}^{[\ell+2:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{2}&\mathbf{E}_{2}\end{bmatrix}

and subspaces

T_{j}:=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{2}^{[\ell+s:n-r-1]}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{\perp}.

Output
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
.

After step 1, we compute the matrix

\mathbf{A}_{1}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{1}&\mathbf{E}_{1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{C}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{1}&\mathbf{E}_{1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{1}&\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1}\end{bmatrix}.

Thus,
S^{\prime}_{j}=\ColSpan\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{[j:(n-1)-r]}\right)^{\perp}
for

\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{1}^{[\ell+2:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{1}&(\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1})^{[2:n-r-\ell]}\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{E}_{1}
have full column-rank, so does
\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1}
and therefore
\left(\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1}\right)^{[2:n-r-\ell]}
. By our inductive hypothesis, as
r+(s-1)+\ell\leq n-1
, we have that the distribution of
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
can be equivalently sampled as follows:

1.

Sample a random vector
\mathbf{v}_{1}
such that
\mathbf{v}_{1}\notin S_{\ell+1}
. Compute
S^{\prime}_{j}:=\spn\{S_{j},\mathbf{v}_{1}\}
for all
j
.

2.

Sample
s-1
random linearly independent vectors
\mathbf{v}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{s}
such that
\spn\{\mathbf{v}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{s}\}\cap S^{\prime}_{\ell+1}=\{0\}
. Compute
T_{j}:=\spn\{S^{\prime}_{j},\mathbf{v}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{s}\}
for all
j
. Output
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
.

It is easy to see that this is statistically identical to sampling
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{s}
simultaneously, as in Distribution
1
. What remains is to show that the distribution produced by the recursive process is also statistically equivalent to Distribution
2
.

Let us compute the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{[1:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{1}^{[\ell+2:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{2}&\mathbf{E}_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{1}&(\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1})^{[2:n-r-\ell]}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{2}&\mathbf{E}_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{1}+(\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1})^{[2:n-r-\ell]}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{2}&(\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1})^{[2:n-r-\ell]}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

Observe that
\mathbf{A}_{2}
is closely related to the following matrix:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{1}&\mathbf{E}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{2}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{E}_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{1}&\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{2}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{E}_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{1}+(\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1})^{[2:n-r-\ell]}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{2}&(\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1})^{[1]}&(\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{1})^{[2:n-r-\ell]}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

Thus,
\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{2}^{[j:\ell]}&\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{2}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{2}^{[\ell+s:n-r-1]}\end{bmatrix}
. So we can equivalently sample
\mathbf{D}_{1},\mathbf{D}_{2},\mathbf{E}_{1},\mathbf{E}_{2}
together and compute
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{2}
accordingly before defining
T_{j}:=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{2}^{[j:\ell]}&\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{2}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{\perp}
.

If we now compute the following matrix:

\mathbf{F}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{1}&\mathbf{E}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{2}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{E}_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{D}_{1}+\mathbf{E}_{1}^{[2:n-r-\ell]}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{2}&\mathbf{E}_{1}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{E}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix},

we observe that (1)
\mathbf{E}_{2}
is full-rank and
\mathbf{E}_{1}
is a random full-rank matrix and (2) for each fixed
\mathbf{E}_{1}
and
\mathbf{D}_{2}
,
\mathbf{D}_{1}
is a random matrix. Thus,
\mathbf{F}
has the same distribution as
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}&\mathbf{M}\end{bmatrix}
in Distribution
2
, and since
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{2}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{F}
and
T_{j}:=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{2}^{[j:\ell]}&\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{2}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{\perp}
, this distribution is equivalent to Distribution
2
.
∎

We will also use variations of the information-theoretical and cryptographic subspace-hiding lemmas of
[
SZ25
]
, stating proofs for completeness; many of these proofs will closely track the proof outlines of
[
SZ25
]
.

Lemma D.3

(Adapted from
[
SZ25
]
)
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
, and let
S_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\ell+1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be subspaces where
S_{j}
has dimension
r+j-1
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{s}
be the distribution over subspaces
T_{j}
defined as follows: pick
s
random linearly independent vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that
\spn\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}\cap S_{\ell+1}=\{0\}
and let
T_{j}:=\spn\{S_{j},v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}
for all
j
. For any subspace
S^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
let
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}}
be the oracle that checks membership in
S^{\prime}
(outputs 1 if and only if the input is inside
S^{\prime}
).

Then, for every oracle-aided quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
making at most
q
quantum queries, we have the following indistinguishability over oracle distributions:

\{\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{S_{\ell+1}}\}\approx_{O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right)}\{\mathcal{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}:(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}\}.

Proof.

We prove the claim by a hybrid argument, increasing the dimension of the random super-spaces
T_{j}
one at a time.

First, note that since
S_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\ell+1}
, there is some full-column-rank matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)}
such that
\mathcal{O}_{S_{j}}
can be described as accepting
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
if and only if
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
; fix any such matrix
\mathbf{A}
.

We now sample a random matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times\ell}
and compute
\mathbf{C}:=\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}
. We switch to the oracles
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{S_{j}}
which accept
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
if and only if
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
. As shown in the proof of Lemma
D.1
,
(\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{S_{\ell+1}})
and
(\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}_{\ell+1}})
are functionally identical for all
\mathbf{B}
, so this change is indistinguishable. We can also indistinguishably switch to the oracles
\mathcal{O}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{j}}
which accept
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
if and only if
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}=0^{n-r-\ell}
and
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:\ell]}=0^{\ell-j+1}
.

Next, we additionally sample a uniformly random vector
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r-\ell}\setminus\{0\}
. Consider the oracles
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}_{j}}
which accept
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
if and only if (1)
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}=0^{n-r-\ell}
or
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}=\mathbf{z}
and (2)
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:\ell]}=0^{\ell-j+1}
. By a simulation argument, it is easy to see that we are essentially giving
\ell+1
copies of an oracle which in one case accepts
\mathbf{x}
if
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}=0^{n-r-\ell}
and in the other accepts if
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}=0^{n-r-\ell}
or
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}=\mathbf{z}
. By the randomness of
\mathbf{z}
and a standard quantum lower bound, we have that

\{\mathcal{O}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{\ell+1}}\}\approx_{O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-\ell}}}\right)}\{\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}\}.

Finally, we move from
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}_{j}}
to the functionally equivalent oracles
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{S^{\prime}_{j}}
which accept
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
if and only if
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
or
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{\ell-j+1}||\mathbf{z}
; this change is indistinguishable.

By Lemma
D.1
,
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{S^{\prime}_{j}}
are precisely membership oracles for the distribution of superspaces produced by adding a random vector outside
S_{\ell+1}
to all
S_{j}
’s, thereby proving the claim for
s=1
. For arbitrary
s
, iterating the argument
s
times in total gives a distinguishing advantage of

\sum_{j=1}^{s}O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-\ell-(j-1)}}}\right)=O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right),

as desired.
∎

A straightforward combination of a simulation argument and a hybrid argument gives the following corollary of Lemma
D.3
.

Corollary D.4
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
, and let
S_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\ell+1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
as in Lemma
D.3
. Define the distribution
\mathcal{S}_{s}
and membership oracles
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}}
for subspaces
S^{\prime}
similarly.

Then, for every oracle-aided quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
making at most
q
quantum queries, we have the following indistinguishability over oracle distributions:

\displaystyle\{(\mathcal{O}_{T_{1,1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{T_{1,\ell+1}}),\ldots,(\mathcal{O}_{T_{m,1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{T_{m,\ell+1}}):\forall i\in[m],(T_{i,1},\ldots,T_{i,\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}\}

\displaystyle\approx_{O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot m\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right)}\{(\mathcal{O}^{1}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{1}_{T_{\ell+1}}),\ldots,(\mathcal{O}^{m}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{m}_{T_{\ell+1}}):(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}\}

.

Using Corollary
D.4
, we can now show the following subspace anti-concentration result.

Lemma D.5
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
, and let
S_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\ell+1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
as in Lemma
D.3
. Define the distribution
\mathcal{S}_{s}
and membership oracles
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}}
for subspaces
S^{\prime}
similarly.

Assume there is an oracle-aided quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
making at most
q
quantum queries and outputting a vector
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
at the end of its execution, such that

\Pr[\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}}\in(T_{1}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}):(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}]\geq\varepsilon.

Denote
t:=n-r-s,m:=\frac{n(t+1)}{\varepsilon}
and assume (1)
\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\cdot\left(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell}\right)\leq o(1)
and (2)
\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot m^{2}\cdot s}{2^{t/2}}\leq o(1)
. Then,

\Pr[\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}}\in(S_{1}^{\perp}\setminus T_{1}^{\perp}):(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{16n\cdot(t+1)}.

Proof.

Our proof follows
[
SZ25
]
closely, but with a few modifications. We first construct a reduction
\mathcal{B}
that uses
\mathcal{A}
as a subroutine;
\mathcal{B}
has access to
m:=n(t+1)/\varepsilon
samples of oracles
\left(\mathcal{O}^{(1,1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(1,\ell+1)}\right)
,
\ldots
,
\left(\mathcal{O}^{(m,1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(m,\ell+1)}\right)
, for
t:=n-r-s
.
\mathcal{B}
executes
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}^{(i,1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(i,\ell+1)}}
for every
i\in[m]
and obtains
m
vectors
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\}
, before taking only the vectors
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{j}\}
that are inside
S_{1}^{\perp}
. Finally, it computes
D:=\dim(\mathsf{span}\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{j}\})
; note that
\mathcal{B}
only makes
q\cdot m
queries.

Consider the following oracle distribution
\mathcal{D}_{1}
: sample
(T_{i,1},\ldots,T_{i,\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}
i.i.d.
m
times and give
\mathcal{B}
access to all of the associated membership oracles
(\mathcal{O}_{T_{i,1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{T_{i,\ell+1}})
. Our goal is to show that
D\geq t+1
with high probability over
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.

For every
i\in[m]
we define the probability
p_{i}
as follows. For indices
i\in[n/\varepsilon]
in the first bucket,
p_{i}
is the probability that given access to
\mathcal{O}_{T_{i,1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{T_{i,\ell+1}}
, the output of
\mathcal{A}
is a vector
u_{i}\in T_{i,1}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}\subseteq S_{1}^{\perp}
, in which case we call the
i
’th execution
successful
. We denote by
T_{(1,1)},\ldots,T_{(1,\ell+1)}
the first set of subspaces in the first bucket where a successful execution happens (and set
\left(T_{(1,1)},\ldots,T_{(1,\ell+1)}\right):=(\bot,\ldots,\bot)
if no execution in the bucket is successful). For any
i
inside buckets
2\leq b\leq t+1
, let
p_{i}
be the probability that (1)
u_{i}\in T_{i,1}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
and (2) the intersection between
T_{i,1}^{\perp}
and each of
T_{(1,1)}^{\perp},\ldots,T_{(b-1,1)}^{\perp}
contains only
\{0\}
. Similarly to the first bucket, we denote by
T_{(b,1)},\ldots,T_{(b,\ell+1)}
the first set of subspaces in bucket
b
with a successful execution. Observe that a successful execution in all buckets immediately implies that
\mathcal{B}
finds at least
t+1
linearly independent vectors which are in
S_{1}^{\perp}
and thus that
D\geq t+1
.

Consider the probability
p^{\prime}
defined as follows. Let
T_{1,1},\ldots,T_{t,1}
be any
t
subspaces, each of dimension
r+s
, and hence their duals
T_{1,1}^{\perp},\ldots,T_{t,1}^{\perp}
have dimension
t
.
p^{\prime}_{T_{1,1},\ldots,T_{t,1}}
is the probability that (1) when sampling
T_{1}^{\perp}
, the intersection of
T_{1}^{\perp}
with each of the
t
dual subspaces
T_{1,1}^{\perp},\ldots,T_{t,1}^{\perp}
is only the zero vector and (2) the output of
\mathcal{A}
is inside
T_{1}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
.
p^{\prime}
is defined to be the minimal probability taken over all possible choices of
t
subspaces
T_{1,1},\ldots,T_{t,1}
. It is easy to see that
p^{\prime}\leq p_{i}
for all
i
and thus it suffices to give a lower bound on
p^{\prime}
.

We first show that the probability for a nontrivial intersection is small. Fix any index
\mathsf{idx}
and consider the probability that
T_{1}^{\perp}
intersects with
T_{\mathsf{idx},1}^{\perp}
. How is
T_{1}^{\perp}
(and in particular
T_{1}
) sampled? A random set of linearly independent vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}
is chosen such that
\spn\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}\cap S_{\ell+1}=\{0\}
, and
T_{1}
is computed as
\spn\{S_{1},v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}
. Of course, one alternative way to think of this process is to first pick random linearly independent vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}
such that
\spn\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}\cap S_{1}=\{0\}
, compute
T_{1}:=\spn\{S_{1},v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}
, and resample if
\spn\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}\cap S_{\ell+1}\neq\{0\}
. In other words, if we let
A
denote the event that a random dimension
(r+s)
-superspace
T^{\prime}_{1}
of
S_{1}
can be written as
T^{\prime}_{1}=\spn\{S_{1},v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{s}\}
for
v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that
\spn\{v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{s}\}\cap S_{\ell+1}=\{0\}
, and
B
the event that a random superspace
T^{\prime}_{1}
of
S_{1}
satisfies
(T^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp}\cap T_{\mathsf{idx},1}^{\perp}=\{0\}
, then we want a lower bound on
\Pr[B\mid A]
.

We begin by bounding
\Pr[\neg A]
. We can think of the process of picking a random superspace of
S_{1}
as a
s
-step process of picking vectors
\mathbf{v}_{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
for
z=1
to
s
, where each time, we select
\mathbf{v}_{z}
such that
\mathbf{v}_{z}\notin\spn\{S_{1},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{z-1}\}
. We observe that the probability that
A
does not occur is the sum over
z\in[s]
of the following event: the
z
’th step is the first step such that
A
becomes false. This is at most the sum over
z\in[s]
of the probability that in the
z
’th step, (1)
\mathbf{v}_{z}\in\spn\{S_{\ell+1},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{z-1}\}
, conditioned on (2)
\mathbf{v}_{k}\notin\spn\{S_{\ell+1},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\}
for all
k<z
and (3)
\mathbf{v}_{z}\notin\spn\{S_{1},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{z-1}\}
. Observe that given condition (2), there are only
2^{n}-2^{r+z-1}
vectors which also satisfy condition (3). On the other hand, given condition (2), only
2^{r+\ell+z-1}-2^{r+z-1}
vectors also satisfy conditions (1) and (3). Thus, the probability for a given
z
is at most
