\frac{2^{r+\ell+z-1}-2^{r+z-1}}{2^{n}-2^{r+z-1}}=\frac{2^{\ell}-1}{2^{n-r-z+1}-1}<\frac{2^{\ell}}{2^{n-r-z+1}}=2^{-(n-\ell-r-z+1)}.

We therefore conclude that
\Pr[\neg A]
is at most

\sum_{z=1}^{s}2^{-(n-\ell-r-z+1)}<2^{-(n-\ell-r-s)}=2^{-t+\ell}.

Now, let us bound
\Pr[\neg B]
. Recall that if
T^{\prime}_{1}
is a random superspace of
S_{1}
, then
(T^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp}
is a random dimension
t
subspace of
S_{1}^{\perp}
, which has dimension
n-r
. On the other hand,
T_{\mathsf{idx},1}^{\perp}
is a fixed dimension
t
subspace of
S_{1}^{\perp}
. We want to determine the probability that
(T^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp}\cap T_{\mathsf{idx},1}^{\perp}=\{0\}
. Like before, we can now think of the process of sampling a basis for
(T^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp}
as a
t
-step process, where in the
z
’th step, a vector
\mathbf{v}_{z}\in S_{1}^{\perp}
is chosen such that
\mathbf{v}_{z}\notin\spn\{\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{z-1}\}
. We again bound the sum over
z\in[t]
of the following event: conditioned on (1)
\mathbf{v}_{k}\notin\spn\{T_{\mathsf{idx},1}^{\perp},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\}
for all
k<z
and (2)
\mathbf{v}_{z}\notin\spn\{\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{z-1}\}
, (3)
\mathbf{v}_{z}\in\spn\{T_{\mathsf{idx},1}^{\perp},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{z-1}\}
. Observe that condition (1) obviously implies that
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{z-1}
are linearly independent and thus given condition (1), there are precisely
2^{n-r}-2^{z-1}
vectors in
S_{1}^{\perp}
which satisfy condition (2). On the other hand, given condition (1), there are only
2^{t+z-1}-2^{z-1}
vectors which satisfy both conditions (2) and (3). Thus, the probability for a given
z
is at most

\frac{2^{t+z-1}-2^{z-1}}{2^{n-r}-2^{z-1}}=\frac{2^{t}-1}{2^{n-r-z+1}-1}<\frac{2^{t}}{2^{n-r-z+1}}=2^{-(n-r-t-z+1)}.

We therefore conclude that
\Pr[\neg B]
is at most

\sum_{z=1}^{t}2^{-(n-r-t-z+1)}<2^{t-s}.

Thus, we have that

\Pr[B\mid A]\geq\Pr[B]-\Pr[\neg A]=1-\Pr[\neg B]-\Pr[\neg A]\geq 1-2^{t-s}-2^{-t+\ell}.

By a union bound, this means the probability that there exists
\mathsf{idx}
such that
T_{1}^{\perp}
has a nontrivial intersection with
T_{\mathsf{idx},1}^{\perp}
is at most
t(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell})
.

If we now let
C
denote the event that
\mathcal{A}
outputs a vector in
T_{1}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
and
D
the event that
T_{1}^{\perp}\cap T_{\mathsf{idx},1}^{\perp}=\{0\}
for all
\mathsf{idx}\in[t]
, we have that

\Pr[C\mid D]\geq\Pr[C]-\Pr[\neg D]\geq\varepsilon-t(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell}).

As
\frac{t}{\varepsilon}(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell})\leq o(1)
by assumption,

\displaystyle p^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Pr[C\land D]=\Pr[C\mid D]\cdot\Pr[D]

\displaystyle\geq\left(\varepsilon-t\left(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell}\right)\right)\cdot\left(1-t\left(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon/4.

Since every bucket has
n/\varepsilon
indices, each of which succeeds with probability at least
\varepsilon/4
, the overall success probability in a bucket is at least
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
. By a union bound,
D\geq t+1
occurs with probability at least
1-(t+1)e^{-\Omega(n)}=1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
.

We now consider a different oracle distribution
\mathcal{D}_{2}
: sample
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}
once, then allow an
m
-oracle access to it. Note that
\mathcal{B}
is a
(q\cdot m)
-query algorithm and thus by Corollary
D.4
, we have that

\mathcal{D}_{1}\approx_{O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot m^{2}\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right)}\mathcal{D}_{2}.

Thus, when we execute
\mathcal{B}
on a sample from
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, with probability at least

1-e^{-\Omega(n)}-O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot m^{2}\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right)\geq 1-O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot m^{2}\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right),

\mathcal{B}
will compute
D\geq t+1
.
As we assumed that
\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot m^{2}\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}=O(\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot m^{2}\cdot s}{2^{t/2}})\leq o(1)
, this means that
\mathcal{B}
computes
D\geq t+1
with probability at least
1/2
over
\mathcal{D}_{2}
. By a simple averaging argument, this means that with probability at least
1/4
over the sampling of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1}
,
\mathcal{B}
computes
D\geq t+1
when given access to this set of superspaces with probability at least
1/4
; we refer to such sets of superspaces as “good” sets. But when
D\geq t+1
, this means that
\mathcal{B}
has computed at least
t+1
linearly independent vectors in
S_{1}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
despite the fact that
T_{1}^{\perp}
has dimension
t
, and thus
\mathcal{B}
produces at least one vector in
S_{1}^{\perp}\setminus T_{1}^{\perp}
. Since
\mathcal{B}
uses
m
i.i.d executions of
\mathcal{A}
, for every
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
which is “good”, when we prepare an oracle access to
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
and execute
\mathcal{A}
, we will get
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}}\in S_{1}^{\perp}\setminus T_{1}^{\perp}
with probability at least
\frac{1}{4m}
.

Thus, over random
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
, we have that

\Pr[\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}}\in S_{1}^{\perp}\setminus T_{1}^{\perp}:(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}]\geq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{4m}=\frac{\varepsilon}{16\cdot n\cdot(t+1)},

as desired.
∎

We now consider a cryptographic version of Lemma
D.3
.

Lemma D.6
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
, and let
S_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\ell+1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be subspaces where
S_{j}
has dimension
r+j-1
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{s}
be the distribution over subspaces
T_{j}
defined as follows: pick
s
random linearly independent vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that
\spn\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}\cap S_{\ell+1}=\{0\}
and let
T_{j}:=\spn\{S_{j},v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}
for all
j
. For any subspace
S^{\prime}\subseteq Z_{2}^{n}
let
C_{S^{\prime}}
some canonical classical circuit that checks membership in
S^{\prime}
, say by Gaussian elimination. Let
\mathsf{iO}
be an indistinguishability obfuscation scheme that is
(f(\lambda),\varepsilon(\lambda))
-secure, and assume that
(f(\lambda),\varepsilon(\lambda))
-secure injective one-way functions exist, where
f
and
1/\varepsilon
are sub-exponential functions.

Then, for every security parameter
\lambda
such that
\lambda\leq n-r-s-\ell
and sufficiently large
p:=p(\lambda)
polynomial in the security parameter, we have the following indistinguishability:

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{O}_{S_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{S_{\ell+1}}:\mathsf{O}_{S_{j}}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{S_{j}})\}\approx_{(f(\lambda)-\ell\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\lambda),\mathsf{poly}(n)\cdot\varepsilon(\lambda))}

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}:(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s},\mathsf{O}_{T_{j}}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{T_{j}})\}.

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
D.6
is a simple extension of the proof of Lemma
D.3
to the cryptographic setting using the same proof outline as in
[
Zha21b
]
. We will use an induction argument, first considering the case where
s=1
.

Consider the following series of hybrids:

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: In this hybrid,
\mathcal{A}
receives
\mathsf{O}_{S_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{S_{\ell}}
. All programs are appropriately padded before obfuscating so that all the programs received by
\mathcal{A}
in the following hybrids have the same length.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: Since
S_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\ell+1}
, we also have that
S_{\ell+1}^{\perp}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{1}^{\perp}
. Fix any full column-rank matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)}
such that
S_{j}^{\perp}=\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}^{[j:n-r]})
; such a matrix can be computed by Gaussian elimination. Sample a random matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times\ell}
and compute
\mathbf{C}:=\mathbf{A}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-\ell}\end{bmatrix}
. Let
\hat{P}
be an obfuscation under
\mathsf{iO}
of the program
Z
that always outputs
0
on inputs in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r-\ell}
. Then
\mathsf{O}_{S_{j}}
is the obfuscation under
\mathsf{iO}
of the function

\displaystyle Q_{j}(\mathbf{x}):=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}\\
1&\text{if }\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:\ell]}=0^{\ell-j+1}\text{ and }\hat{P}\left(\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}\right)=1\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

As shown in the proof of Lemma
D.1
, the condition
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}=0^{n-r-j+1}
is equivalent to checking that
\mathbf{x}\in S_{j}
for all matrices
\mathbf{B}
. Since
\hat{P}
always outputs
0
,
Q_{j}
is a membership program for
S_{j}
and thus is functionally equivalent to
C_{S_{j}}
. Thus
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
are
(f(\lambda),(\ell+1)\cdot\varepsilon(\lambda))
-computationally indistinguishable by the security of the
\mathsf{iO}
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: Let
\hat{P}
now be the obfuscation under
\mathsf{iO}
of the function

\displaystyle P_{y}(\mathbf{z}):=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathsf{OWF}(\mathbf{z})=y\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
\mathsf{OWF}
is an injective one-way function and
y=\mathsf{OWF}(\mathbf{z}^{*})
for a random
\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r-\ell}
. Since
n-r-\ell\geq\lambda
, we can invoke the security of
\mathsf{OWF}
. Specifically, the only point on which
Z
and
P_{y}
differ is
\mathbf{z}^{*}
, and finding
\mathbf{z}^{*}
requires inverting
\mathsf{OWF}
; thus,
\mathsf{iO}
is also a differing-inputs obfuscator for these circuits
[
BCP14
]
. Since the reduction will need to embed either an obfuscation of
P_{y}
or
Z
in
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{\ell+1}
before running
\mathcal{A}
, it has overhead
\ell\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
. Therefore,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
are
(f(\lambda)-\ell\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\lambda),O(\ell\cdot\varepsilon(\lambda)))
-computationally indistinguishable by the security of
\mathsf{OWF}
and
\mathsf{iO}
. Note that
Q_{j}(\mathbf{x})
decides membership in the subspace
S^{\prime}_{j}
of vectors
\mathbf{x}
such that
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{C}^{[j:n-r]}\in\spn\{0^{\ell-j+1}||\mathbf{z}^{*}\}
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
: In this hybrid, a random
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{z}^{*}
are chosen which fixes
S^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,S^{\prime}_{\ell+1}
; the canonical membership programs
C_{S^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,C_{S^{\prime}_{\ell+1}}
are then obfuscated. Since all programs in both hybrids still decide membership in
S^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,S^{\prime}_{\ell+1}
, the programs being obfuscated are functionally equivalent. Thus,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
are
(f(\lambda),(\ell+1)\cdot\varepsilon(\lambda))
-computationally indistinguishable by the security of the
\mathsf{iO}
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
: Now
\mathbf{z}^{*}
is chosen at random, but restricted to being nonzero. Since
\lambda\leq n-r-\ell
,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
are
O\left(\frac{1}{2^{\lambda}}\right)
-statistically close. Observe that by Lemma
D.1
,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
now contains precisely the obfuscations of
C_{T_{j}}
.

The proof follows by iterating over
s
and observing that
\lambda\leq n-r-\ell-s
and
\ell,s\leq n
.
∎

We remark that the lemma also trivially extends (with the same base security parameter
\lambda
) to the setting where we choose stronger parameters
\kappa\geq n-r-s-\ell,\mathsf{poly}(\kappa)\geq p(n-r-s-\ell)
to obfuscate the circuits.

We will also need cryptographic versions of Corollary
D.4
and Lemma
D.5
.

Lemma D.7
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
, and let
S_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\ell+1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be the same subspaces as in Lemma
D.6
. Define the distribution
\mathcal{S}_{s}
and membership circuits
C_{S^{\prime}}
for subspaces
S^{\prime}
similarly. Let
\mathsf{iO}
an indistinguishability obfuscation scheme that is
(f(\lambda),\varepsilon(\lambda))
-secure, and assume that
(f(\lambda),\varepsilon(\lambda))
-secure injective one-way functions exist, where
f
and
1/\varepsilon
are sub-exponential functions.

Then, for every security parameter
\lambda
such that
\lambda\leq n-r-s-\ell
and sufficiently large
p:=p(\lambda)
polynomial in the security parameter, we have the following indistinguishability:

\displaystyle\{(\mathsf{O}_{S_{1}}^{1},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{S_{\ell+1}}^{1}),\ldots,(\mathsf{O}_{S_{1}}^{m},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{S_{\ell+1}}^{m}):

\displaystyle\forall i\in[m],\mathsf{O}_{S_{j}}^{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{S_{j}})\}

\displaystyle\approx

(f(λ)-m⋅n⋅poly(λ),m⋅poly(n)⋅ε(λ))

\displaystyle\{(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}}^{1},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}^{1}),\ldots,(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}}^{m},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}^{m}):

\displaystyle(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s},

\displaystyle\forall i\in[m],\mathsf{O}_{T_{j}}^{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{T_{j}})\}.

Proof.

The proof is nearly identical to
[
SZ25
]
, but we include one here for completeness.

As long as
p
is sufficiently large, then it is indistinguishable to tell whether a given circuit is obfuscated under one or two layers of obfuscation. Thus, for a sufficiently large parameter
p
, the distribution

\{(\mathsf{O}_{S_{1}}^{1},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{S_{\ell+1}}^{1}),\ldots,(\mathsf{O}_{S_{1}}^{m},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{S_{\ell+1}}^{m}):\forall i\in[m],\mathsf{O}_{S_{j}}^{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{S_{j}})\},

is
(f(\lambda),O(\ell\cdot m)\cdot\varepsilon(\lambda))
-computationally indistinguishable from a distribution where
C_{S_{j}}
are swapped with obfuscations of
C_{S_{j}}
(denote this modified distribution by
\mathcal{D}_{S}
). Similarly, the distribution

\displaystyle\{(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}}^{1},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}^{1}),\ldots,(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}}^{m},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}^{m}):

\displaystyle(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s},

\displaystyle\forall i\in[m],\mathsf{O}_{T_{j}}^{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{T_{j}})\},

is
(f(\lambda),O(\ell\cdot m)\cdot\varepsilon(\lambda))
-computationally indistinguishable from a distribution where
C_{T_{j}}
are swapped with obfuscations of
C_{T_{j}}
(denote this modified distribution by
\mathcal{D}_{T}
).

The indistinguishability between
\mathcal{D}_{S}
and
\mathcal{D}_{T}
follows from Lemma
D.6
: consider the reduction
\mathcal{B}
that gets a sample
(\mathsf{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{\ell+1})
which is either from
\mathcal{D}_{0}:=\{(\mathsf{O}_{S_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{S_{\ell+1}}):\mathsf{O}_{S_{j}}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{S_{j}})\}
or from
\mathcal{D}_{1}:=\{(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}):(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s},\mathsf{O}_{T_{j}}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{T_{j}})\}
for random superspaces
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
of
(S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell+1})
.
\mathcal{B}
then generates
m
i.i.d sets of obfuscations

\{(\mathsf{O}_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{\ell+1}^{(1)}),\ldots,(\mathsf{O}_{1}^{(m)},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{\ell+1}^{(m)})\}

of
(\mathsf{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{\ell+1})
and executes
\mathcal{A}
on the
m\cdot(\ell+1)
obfuscations. When
(\mathsf{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{\ell+1})
comes from
\mathcal{D}_{0}
then the output sample of the reduction comes from the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{S}
, and when
(\mathsf{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{\ell+1})
comes from
\mathcal{D}_{1}
then the output sample of the reduction comes from the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{T}
. Since the reduction executes in complexity
\ell\cdot m\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)\leq m\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
, this means that

\mathcal{D}_{S}\approx_{(f(\lambda)-m\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\lambda),\mathsf{poly}(n)\cdot\varepsilon(\lambda))}\mathcal{D}_{T}.

By transitivity of computational indistinguishability, the two distributions in the statement of the lemma are
(f(\lambda)-m\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\lambda),m\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)\cdot\varepsilon(\lambda))
-computationally indistinguishable.
∎

Corollary D.8
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
, and let
S_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\ell+1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be the same subspaces as in Lemma
D.6
. Define the distribution
\mathcal{S}_{s}
and membership circuits
C_{S^{\prime}}
for subspaces
S^{\prime}
similarly. Let
\mathsf{iO}
an indistinguishability obfuscation scheme that is
(f(\lambda),\varepsilon(\lambda))
-secure, and assume that
(f(\lambda),\varepsilon(\lambda))
-secure injective one-way functions exist, where
f
and
1/\varepsilon
are sub-exponential functions.

Then, for every security parameter
\lambda
such that
\lambda\leq n-r-s-\ell
and sufficiently large
p:=p(\lambda)
polynomial in the security parameter, we have the following indistinguishability:

\displaystyle\{

\displaystyle(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}}^{1},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}^{1}),\ldots,(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}}^{m},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}^{m}):

\displaystyle(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s},\forall i\in[m],\mathsf{O}_{T_{j}}^{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{T_{j}})\}

\displaystyle\approx_{(f(\lambda)-m\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\lambda),m\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)\cdot\varepsilon(\lambda))}

\displaystyle\{

\displaystyle(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1,1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{1,\ell+1}}),\ldots,(\mathsf{O}_{T_{m,1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{n,\ell+1}}):

\displaystyle\forall i\in[m],(T_{i,1},\ldots,T_{i,\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s},\mathsf{O}_{T_{i,j}}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{T_{i,j}})\}.

Lemma D.9
.

Let
n,r,s,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
r+s+\ell\leq n
, and let
S_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\ell+1}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be the same subspaces as in Lemma
D.6
. Define the distribution
\mathcal{S}_{s}
and membership circuits
C_{S^{\prime}}
for subspaces
S^{\prime}
similarly. Let
\mathsf{iO}
an indistinguishability obfuscation scheme that is
(f(\cdot),\frac{1}{f(\cdot)})
-secure, and assume that
(f(\cdot),\frac{1}{f(\cdot)})
-secure injective one-way functions exist, where
f
is a sub-exponential function.

Let
\lambda
be any security parameter such that
\lambda\leq n-r-s-\ell
and
p:=p(\lambda)
a sufficiently large polynomial in the security parameter. Denote by
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda,p,s}
the distribution over obfuscated circuits that samples
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}
and then
O_{T_{j}}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\lambda},1^{p},C_{T_{j}})
.

Assume there is a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
of complexity
T_{\mathcal{A}}
such that,

\Pr[\mathcal{A}(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}})\in T_{1}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}:(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}})\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{\lambda,p,s}]\geq\varepsilon.

Also, denote
t:=n-r-s
,
m:=\frac{n(t+1)}{\varepsilon}
, and assume (1)
\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\cdot\left(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell}\right)\leq o(1)
and (2)
\frac{m\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)\cdot T_{\mathcal{A}}}{f(\lambda)}\leq o(1)
.
Then,

\Pr[\mathcal{A}(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}})\in\left(S_{1}^{\perp}\setminus T_{1}^{\perp}\right):(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}})\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{\lambda,p,s}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{16\cdot n\cdot(t+1)}.

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
D.9
is nearly identical to that of Lemma
D.5
; we will point out where the main differences lie.

As before, we consider a reduction
\mathcal{B}
which uses
\mathcal{A}
as a subroutine.
\mathcal{B}
has access to
m:=n(t+1)/\varepsilon
samples of obfuscations

((\mathsf{O}^{(1,1)},\ldots,\mathsf{O}^{(1,\ell+1)}),\ldots,(\mathsf{O}^{(m,1)},\ldots,\mathsf{O}^{(m,\ell+1)})),

for
t:=n-r-s
.
\mathcal{B}
executes
\mathcal{A}(\mathsf{O}^{(i,1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(i,\ell+1)})
for every
i\in[m]
and obtains
m
vectors
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\}
, before taking only the vectors
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{j}\}
that are inside
S_{1}^{\perp}
. Finally, it computes
D:=\dim(\mathsf{span}\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{j}\})
.

The running time of
\mathcal{B}
is
O(m\cdot T_{\mathcal{A}}+m\cdot n^{3})
, where the first term arises from running
m
executions of
\mathcal{A}
and the second term comes from running Gaussian elimination to check whether the
i
’th vector
u_{i}
produced by
\mathcal{A}
is in
S_{1}^{\perp}
and is outside of the span of the previous vectors (produced by
\mathcal{A}
) that lie in
S_{1}^{\perp}
(recall that
S_{1}
is known to
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
and hence checking
S_{1}^{\perp}
also takes at most
O(n^{3})
time).

Consider the following distribution
\mathcal{D}_{1}
: sample
(T_{i,1},\ldots,T_{i,\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}
i.i.d.
m
times and for each set of superspaces, give
\mathcal{B}
an obfuscation of all membership programs
((\mathsf{O}_{T_{1,1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{1,\ell+1}}),\ldots,(\mathsf{O}_{T_{m,1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{m,\ell+1}}))
. By an identical argument as in Lemma
D.5
, we have that
D\geq t+1
with probability at least
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
assuming
\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\cdot\left(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell}\right)\leq o(1)
.

We now execute
\mathcal{B}
on a different distribution
\mathcal{D}_{2}
: sample
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}
once, then sample
m
i.i.d. obfuscations of the same circuits
(C_{T_{1}},\ldots,C_{T_{\ell+1}})
, denoted by
((\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}^{(1)}),\ldots,(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}}^{(m)},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}}^{(m)}))
. By Corollary
D.8
,

\mathcal{D}_{1}\approx_{f(\lambda)-m\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\lambda),\frac{m\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)}{f(\lambda)}}\mathcal{D}_{2}.

By assumption, we have that
\mathcal{B}
runs in time
\leq f(\lambda)-m\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
. Thus, whenever we execute
\mathcal{B}
on a sample from
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, with probability at least

1-e^{-\Omega(n)}-\frac{m\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)}{f(\lambda)}\geq 1-\frac{m\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)}{f(\lambda)}\geq\frac{1}{2},

\mathcal{B}
will compute
D\geq t+1
. By an averaging argument, this means that with probability at least
1/4
over the sampling of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1}
,
\mathcal{B}
will compute
D\geq t+1
on the appropriate input with probability at least
1/4
; we refer to such sets of superspaces as “good” sets. But when
D\geq t+1
, this means that
\mathcal{B}
has at least
t+1
linearly independent vectors in
S_{1}^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
; since
T_{1}^{\perp}
has dimension
n-r-s=t
,
\mathcal{B}
produces at least one vector in
S_{1}^{\perp}\setminus T_{1}^{\perp}
. As
\mathcal{B}
uses
m
i.i.d. executions of
\mathcal{A}
, for every set of superspaces
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
which is “good”, when we prepare obfuscations of
T_{i}
,
\mathcal{A}(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}})\in S_{1}^{\perp}\setminus T_{1}^{\perp}
with probability at least
\frac{1}{4m}
.

Thus, over random
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell+1})
,

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathcal{A}(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}})\in S_{1}^{\perp}\setminus T_{1}^{\perp}:(\mathsf{O}_{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{O}_{T_{\ell+1}})\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{\lambda,p,s}]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{4m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{16n\cdot(t+1)},

as desired.
∎

Appendix E
Missing Proofs from Section
4
and
5

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

We consider a series of hybrids, and show that the success probability in each pair of hybrids is statistically close.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: The original execution of
\mathcal{A}
.
By assumption,
\mathcal{A}
succeeds with probability
\varepsilon
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: Using small-range distribution.
Consider the random function
F
which samples the randomness used to compute the cosets
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
for every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r}
; these cosets are then used in
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{D}
. We now swap
F
with a small-range version
F^{\prime}
, which is sampled as follows: let
R:=(300\cdot q^{3})\cdot\frac{2^{7}\cdot n^{2}}{\varepsilon}
. For every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r}
, sample
\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}\leftarrow[R]
; for every
\mathsf{idx}\in[R]
, sample a coset
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathsf{idx}})
. Define
F^{\prime}(\mathbf{y}):=(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}})
.

By Theorem A.6 of
[
AGQY22
]
, every quantum algorithm making at most
q
queries to
F
or
F^{\prime}
has distinguishing advantage at most
\frac{300\cdot q^{3}}{R}<\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
, so the outputs of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
have statistical distance at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
, and thus
\mathcal{A}
succeeds in this hybrid succeeds with probability at least
\frac{7\varepsilon}{8}
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: Relaxing the dual oracle.
In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, on input
(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
, the oracle
\mathcal{D}
accepted if and only if
\mathbf{v}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}}^{[j:n-r]})^{\perp}
. Consider the following modification. For each
\mathsf{idx}\in[R]
, sample a random
(r+s+\ell)
-dimensional superspace
T_{\mathsf{idx},\ell+1}^{\perp}
of
\ColSpan\left(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}\right)^{\perp}
by sampling
s
random linearly independent vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}
such that

\spn\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}\cap\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}}^{[\ell+1:n-r]})^{\perp}=\{0\}

and define

T_{\mathsf{idx},k}^{\perp}:=\spn\{\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}}^{[k:n-r]})^{\perp},v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}

for
k\in[\ell]
. Now, on input
(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
, the oracle
\mathcal{D}
will instead accept if and only if
\mathbf{v}\in T_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}},j}^{\perp}
.

By Lemma
D.3
, for each
\mathsf{idx}\in[R]
, changing
\mathcal{D}
is
O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right)
-indistinguishable. Since we use the above indistinguishability
R
times, we get
R\cdot O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right)
-indistinguishability. Thus, if we denote
\mathcal{A}
’s success probability in
\mathsf{Hyb_{2}}
by
\varepsilon_{2}
, we have that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{7\varepsilon}{8}-R\cdot O\left(\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right)\geq\frac{7\varepsilon}{8}-O\left(\frac{q^{4}\cdot\ell\cdot s\cdot n^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{7\varepsilon}{8}-O\left(\frac{q^{4}\cdot n^{4}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right)\geq\frac{3\varepsilon}{4},

by assumption (2).

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
: Changing the success predicate.
Recall that in
\mathsf{Hyb_{2}}
, we say
\mathcal{A}
is successful if it finds a collision, i.e. it produces a pair
x_{0}\neq x_{1}
such that
H(x_{0})=H(x_{1})
. We now say that
\mathcal{A}
is successful only if
H(x_{0})=H(x_{1})

and

\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1}\notin T_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}},1}
. For every
\mathsf{idx}\in[R]
, let
\varepsilon_{2}^{(\mathsf{idx})}
be the probability that
\mathcal{A}
finds
x_{0}\neq x_{1}
such that
y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y}
and
\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathsf{idx}
; by definition,
\sum_{\mathsf{idx}}\varepsilon_{2}^{(\mathsf{idx})}=\varepsilon_{2}
. Observe that in this case,
\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}})\setminus\{0\}
. Let
\delta^{(\mathsf{idx})}
be the probability that
\mathcal{A}
finds
x_{0},x_{1}
such that
y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y}
,
\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathsf{idx}
, and
\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1}\in T_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}},1}\setminus\{0\}
.

Define
\varepsilon_{3}^{(\mathsf{idx})}
to be the probability that
\mathcal{A}
finds
x_{0},x_{1}
such that
y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y}
,
\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathsf{idx}
, and
\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1}\notin T_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}},1}
. Let
L
be the subset of indices
\mathsf{idx}\in[R]
such that
\varepsilon_{2}^{(\mathsf{idx})}\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{2R}
; this means that
\sum_{\mathsf{idx}\in L}\varepsilon_{2}^{(\mathsf{idx})}\geq\varepsilon_{2}/2
. Our goal is to show that for every
\mathsf{idx}\in L
,
\varepsilon_{3}^{(\mathsf{idx})}\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{2}^{(\mathsf{idx})}}{16n^{2}}
. By definition, we have that
\varepsilon_{3}^{(\mathsf{idx})}\geq\varepsilon_{2}^{(\mathsf{idx})}-\delta^{(\mathsf{idx})}
, so if
\delta^{(\mathsf{idx})}\leq\varepsilon_{2}^{(\mathsf{idx})}/2
, then we are done. Thus, we assume that

\delta^{(\mathsf{idx})}>\frac{\varepsilon_{2}^{(\mathsf{idx})}}{2}\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{4R}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{q^{3}\cdot n^{2}}\right).

Let
t:=n-r-s\leq n-1
and
m:=\frac{n(t+1)}{\delta^{(\mathsf{idx})}}\leq O\left(\frac{q^{3}\cdot n^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
. By assumptions (1) and (2), we have that

\frac{t}{\delta^{(\mathsf{idx})}}\cdot\left(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell}\right)=O\left(\frac{q^{3}\cdot n^{3}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\cdot\left(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell}\right)\leq o(1),

and

\frac{q\cdot\ell\cdot m^{2}\cdot s}{2^{t/2}}=O\left(\frac{q^{7}\cdot n^{10}/\varepsilon^{4}}{2^{(t-1)/2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{q^{7}\cdot n^{10}/\varepsilon^{4}}{\sqrt{2^{n-r-s-\ell}}}\right)\leq o(1),

so the conditions of Lemma
D.5
are fulfilled.
8
8

8

Strictly speaking,
\mathcal{A}
only has access to one oracle which, when restricted to inputs
(k,\cdot,\cdot)
, behaves as the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{T_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}},k}}
, but this is an even weaker model of access and so Lemma
D.5
readily applies.
Thus, we have that for
\mathsf{idx}\in L
,
\mathcal{A}
finds
x_{0},x_{1}
such that
y_{0}=y_{1}:=y
and
\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}})\setminus T_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}},1}
, and thus
\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1}\notin T_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}},1}
, with probability at least
\frac{\delta^{(\mathsf{idx})}}{16n(t+1)}\geq\frac{\delta^{(\mathsf{idx})}}{16n^{2}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{2}^{(\mathsf{idx})}}{32n^{2}}
.

Therefore, the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in this hybrid satisfies

\varepsilon_{3}=\sum_{\mathsf{idx}}\varepsilon_{3}^{(\mathsf{idx})}\geq\sum_{\mathsf{idx}\in L}\varepsilon_{3}^{(\mathsf{idx})}\geq\sum_{\mathsf{idx}\in L}\frac{\varepsilon_{2}^{(j)}}{32n^{2}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{64n^{2}}\geq\frac{3\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.1}
: Using the randomness of
\Pi
.

Recall that for every
\mathsf{idx}\in[R]
, the process of sampling
T_{\mathsf{idx},k}^{\perp}
involves sampling
s
random linearly independent vectors
\mathbf{v}_{\mathsf{idx},1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{\mathsf{idx},s}
and adding them to
S_{\mathsf{idx},k}^{\perp}
. By Lemma
D.2
, we can equivalently sample
T_{\mathsf{idx},k}^{\perp}
by sampling a random full column-rank matrix
\mathbf{M}_{\mathsf{idx}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times(n-r-\ell)}
and a random matrix
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{idx}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times\ell}
for each
\mathsf{idx}\in[R]
, computing
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{idx}}:=\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{idx}}&\mathbf{M}_{\mathsf{idx}}\end{bmatrix}
and setting

T_{\mathsf{idx},k}^{\perp}=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[k:\ell]}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{\perp}.

Since this new sampling process is statistically equivalent, the change is indistinguishable.

Define the permutation
\Gamma
over
\{0,1\}^{n}
as follows: for an input
\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, take the left
r
bits denoted
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r}
, compute
\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}\in[R]
, and then apply matrix multiplication by
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}}&\mathbf{M}_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}}\end{bmatrix}
to the remaining right
n-r
bits. Since
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}}
is full-rank for all
\mathsf{idx}_{\mathbf{y}}
,
\Gamma
is indeed a permutation. We now apply
\Gamma
to the output of
\Pi
inside the execution of a query to
\mathcal{P}
, and apply
\Gamma^{-1}
to the input of
\Pi^{-1}
inside the execution of a query to
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
. Since
\Pi
is random and
\Gamma
is fixed, the composition produces a random permutation as well and thus the success probability in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.1}
is identical.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.2}
: Moving
\Gamma
into the coset computation.

In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.2}
, we stop applying
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{-1}
and instead multiply the output of
\Pi
by
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{idx}}
in
\mathcal{P}
and multiply the original input to
\Pi^{-1}
in
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
by
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{idx}}^{-1}
. By definition, the oracle distributions in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.2}
are identical, so the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.2}
is also
\varepsilon_{3}
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
: Using the randomness of
F^{\prime}
.

In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
, we stop multiplying by
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{idx}}
and
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{idx}}^{-1}
and directly define
T_{\mathsf{idx},k}
to be
\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}}^{[k:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)
. Observe that for any fixed choice of
\Pi
, the distribution of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{K}_{\mathsf{idx}}&\mathbf{L}_{\mathsf{idx}}\end{bmatrix}
has random matrices
\mathbf{K}_{\mathsf{idx}}
and random full column-rank matrices
\mathbf{L}_{\mathsf{idx}}
, so we have that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{idx}}:=\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{idx}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{K}_{\mathsf{idx}}&\mathbf{L}_{\mathsf{idx}}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{j}&\mathbf{M}_{\mathsf{idx}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{K}_{\mathsf{idx}}+\mathbf{L}_{\mathsf{idx}}\cdot\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{idx}}&\mathbf{L}_{\mathsf{idx}}\cdot\mathbf{M}_{\mathsf{idx}}\end{bmatrix},

which has the same distribution as
\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{idx}}
(as both
\mathbf{L}_{\mathsf{idx}}
and
\mathbf{M}_{\mathsf{idx}}
are random full column-rank matrices and
\mathbf{K}_{\mathsf{idx}}
is a random matrix). Thus, the success probability
\varepsilon_{4}
of
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
is identical to the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.2}
, which is simply
\varepsilon_{3}
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
: Rewinding small-range distributions.

We undo the process of sampling an
R
-small range distribution by swapping
F^{\prime}
back to a completely random function
F
. By an identical argument as before,
\mathcal{A}
succeeds in this hybrid with probability at least

\varepsilon_{4}-\frac{300q^{3}}{R}=\varepsilon_{3}-\frac{300q^{3}}{R}\geq\frac{3\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}-\frac{300q^{3}}{R}=\frac{3\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{128n^{2}}=\frac{\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}.

We then observe that the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
is exactly the probability we want to bound, which concludes the proof.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

Given oracle access to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}
which come from
(\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1},\overline{\mathcal{D}})
,
\mathcal{B}
acts as follows:

•

Sample a random function
F^{\prime}
that outputs sufficient (polynomial in
r
) randomness and a random
n
-bit permutation
\Gamma
. On input
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, use the randomness of
F^{\prime}(\mathbf{y})
to generate a random vector
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
, random matrix
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times\ell}
, and random full-rank matrix
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times(n-\ell)}
. Denote by
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}
the full-rank matrix
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times n}
. This defines the following oracles:

•

(\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r},\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})
:

–

(\overline{x}\in\{0,1\}^{r+s},\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r-s-\ell},x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ell})\leftarrow\Gamma(\mathbf{x})
.

–

(\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r},\overline{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r+s})\leftarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{x})
.

–

(\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times n},\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})\leftarrow F^{\prime}(\mathbf{y})
.

–

\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}x^{\prime}\\
\overline{\mathbf{u}}\\
\tilde{x}\end{pmatrix}+\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
.

•

(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\cup\{\bot\})\leftarrow\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r},\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})
:

–

(\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}})\leftarrow F^{\prime}(\mathbf{y})
.

–

\begin{pmatrix}x^{\prime}\\
\overline{\mathbf{u}}\\
\tilde{x}\end{pmatrix}\leftarrow\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}^{-1}\cdot(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}})
.

–

(\overline{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r+s}\cup\{\bot\})\leftarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}(\mathbf{y},\overline{\mathbf{u}})

–

Output
\mathbf{x}\leftarrow\Gamma^{-1}(\overline{x},\tilde{x},x^{\prime})
if
\overline{x}\neq\bot
, else output
\bot
.

•

\{0,1\}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(j\in[\ell+1],\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})
:

–

(\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}})\leftarrow F^{\prime}(\mathbf{y})
.

–

Output 1 iff
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}=0^{n-r-s-j+1}
.

Finally,
\mathcal{B}
runs
(x_{0},x_{1})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
,
(\overline{x}_{b},\tilde{x}_{b},x^{\prime}_{b})\leftarrow\Gamma(x_{b})
and outputs
(\overline{x}_{0},\overline{x}_{1})
.

Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-s-\ell}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-s-\ell}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-s-\ell}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)},

where
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(r+s)\times s}
is the matrix arising from
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}
. As
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}
and
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}
have full column-rank, so does
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}
; on the other hand,
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}
remains a random matrix; thus,
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
has full column-rank. Now, since
(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})\notin\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[1:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)
, this means that if we look at each of the two coordinates vectors
\mathbf{w}_{0}
,
\mathbf{w}_{1}
(which each have
n-r
bits) of
\mathbf{u}_{0}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}
, they differ somewhere between the
(\ell+1)
’th and
(\ell+s)
’th bit. However,
(\mathbf{w}_{b})_{[\ell+1:\ell+s]}
arises from applying the permutation
\overline{\Pi}
on
\{0,1\}^{r+s}
defined by the oracles
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1}
to
\overline{x}_{b}
and taking the last
s
bits of the output. As
x_{0},x_{1}
have the same
\mathbf{y}
’s and thus so do
\overline{x}_{0}
and
\overline{x}_{1}
, it must be the case that
\overline{x}_{0}\neq\overline{x}_{1}
in order for
\mathbf{w}_{0}
to differ from
\mathbf{w}_{1}
in the appropriate indices.

If we define
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{B}}
as the probability that
\mathcal{A}
outputs
x_{0},x_{1}
such that
y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y}
and
(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})\notin\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[1:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)
, we wish to lower bound the probability of
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{B}}
. We do this through a series of hybrids.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: The distribution
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathcal{B}^{\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{P}^{-1}}}
, as simulated by
\mathcal{B}
.

This is the original execution, and thus has success probability
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{B}}
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: Removing the inner permutation
\overline{\Pi}
by using
\Gamma
.

Instead of applying
\overline{\Pi}
and
\Pi^{-1}
inside
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}
, respectively, only use
\Gamma
. This hybrid is statistically identical to the previous hybrid since a concatenation of a permutation with a random permutation
\Gamma
is still a random permutation.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: Taking
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
to be the direct output of
F
by using
F^{\prime}
.

Instead of sampling
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}
and
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
before computing
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}:=\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-s}\end{bmatrix}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}:=\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\overline{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{y}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}+\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
, where
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}},\overline{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{y}}
are defined by the output of the inner random function, we sample
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
directly. We observe that by the randomness of
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
,
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}
,
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}
, and
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}
, the joint distributions of matrices and offset vectors in both hybrids are identical. Therefore, the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in this hybrid is also
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Remark E.1
.

Note that

\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}-\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\overline{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{y}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\overline{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{y}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{y}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}

has only zeros in the first
\ell
coordinates. Thus, if
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
is distributed randomly, then
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
is distributed randomly; if
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
is uniformly random with the
\ell
’th coordinate set to 1, then so is
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
.

Since the distribution generated in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
is precisely
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n,r,\ell,s}
, this means that
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{B}}=\varepsilon
, as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

The perfect correctness follows from the perfect correctness of Construction
2
and observing that the verification algorithm
\mathsf{Ver}
in Construction
2
does not actually need the full capability of
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
and that having access to
\mathcal{D}_{0}
suffices.

The oracle
\mathcal{D}_{0}
can be implemented using
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
. Thus, it suffices to show the strong signature security of Construction
3
with respect to the oracles
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{D}
(where we will abuse notation and also refer to this distribution of oracles by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{n,r,\ell}
).

As with Construction
2
, we can “bloat” the dual oracle. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{n,r,\ell,s}
denote the following distribution over oracles
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. The oracles
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
are defined identically to the oracles in
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{n,r,\ell}
. However, our new dual oracle
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
will be a relaxed version of the original dual oracle
\mathcal{D}
:

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+1+s:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}=0^{n-r-s-j+1}\text{ and }j\in[\ell+1],\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The analog to Lemma
4.4
remains true for these sets of oracle distributions:

Lemma E.2

(Analogous Bloating Lemma)
.

Suppose there is an oracle-aided
q
-query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
such that

\Pr[(y_{0}=y_{1})\land(x_{0}\neq x_{1}):\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D})&\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{n,r,\ell}\\
(x_{0},x_{1})&\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}}\\
(y_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})&\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(x_{b})\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon.

Then we have that

\Pr[\begin{array}[]{l}{\scriptstyle(y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y})\hskip 5.0pt\land}\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})\notin\ColSpan}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[1:\ell]}}&{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}}\end{bmatrix}\right)\end{array}:\begin{aligned} {\scriptstyle(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{n,r,\ell,s}}\\
{\scriptstyle(x_{0},x_{1})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}}\\
{\scriptstyle(y_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(x_{b})}\end{aligned}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}.

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
E.2
is identical to the proof of Lemma
4.4
, since the oracle distribution of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{n(\lambda),r(\lambda),\ell(\lambda)}
is the same as the distribution of
\mathcal{O}_{n(\lambda+1),r(\lambda+1),\ell(\lambda+1)}
except for the coset shift vectors
\{\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}\}_{\mathbf{y}}
whose distribution is not used in the proof.
∎

Just like with Construction
2
, we can entirely simulate access to a bloated dual given only access to the primal oracles.

Lemma E.3

(Analogous Dual Simulation Lemma)
.

Suppose that there is an oracle aided
q
-query algorithm
\mathcal{A}
such that

\Pr[\begin{array}[]{l}{\scriptstyle(y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y})\hskip 5.0pt\land}\\
{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})\notin\ColSpan}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[1:\ell]}}&{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}}\end{bmatrix}\right)\end{array}:\begin{aligned} {\scriptstyle(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{n,r,\ell,s}}\\
{\scriptstyle(x_{0},x_{1})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}}\\
{\scriptstyle(y_{b},\mathbf{u}_{b})}&{\scriptstyle\leftarrow\mathcal{P}(x_{b})}\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon.

Then there is an oracle aided
q
-query algorithm
\mathcal{B}
such that

\Pr[(\overline{y}_{0}=\overline{y}_{1})\land(\overline{x}_{0}\neq\overline{x}_{1}):\begin{aligned} (\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1},\overline{\mathcal{D}})&\leftarrow\mathcal{O}_{r+s,r,0}\\
(\overline{x}_{0},\overline{x}_{1})&\leftarrow\mathcal{B}^{\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}}\\
(\overline{y}_{b},\overline{\mathbf{u}}_{b})&\leftarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{x}_{b})\end{aligned}]\geq\varepsilon,

where
s:=16\cdot(\lambda+1)
and
r:=s\cdot\lambda
.

Note that the new distribution
\mathcal{O}_{r+s,r,0}
is the same dual-free oracle distribution as in Section
4
, which uses fully random coset shift vectors.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Lemma
4.5
with one difference: instead of sampling the random function
F^{\prime}
such that on input
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
,
F^{\prime}(\mathbf{y})
provides randomness to generate a random vector
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
,
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
will be sampled to be a random vector whose
\ell=(\lambda+1)
’th coordinate is 1. It is not hard to verify that with this change, the rest of the proof goes through (see Remark
E.1
for more details).
∎

We can now apply Theorems
4.6
and
4.8
and conclude that Construction
3
has strong signature security (following the same outline as in Section
4
until Corollary
4.10
).
∎

Appendix F
Missing Proofs from Section
B

The proofs of the following lemmas will closely track the general outline of
[
SZ25
]
.

Proof of Lemma
B.2
.

We include a proof of Lemma
B.2
, which very closely follows the proof of Lemma 54 in
[
SZ25
]
, for completeness.
We will consider a series of hybrids, and show that the success probability in each pair of hybrids is computationally close.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: The original execution of
\mathcal{A}
.

By definition,
\mathcal{A}
succeeds with probability
\varepsilon
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: Preparing to switch to a small-range distribution.

Let
(\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F})
be a
(f(\kappa),\frac{1}{f(\kappa)})
-secure lossy function with output length
m
and lossiness parameter
w
. We would like to apply Lemma
A.1
, so we will describe two PRFs and one ensemble of distributions (which will serve as both
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
). The first PRF takes as input
\{0,1\}^{d}
and applies
\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}},\cdot)
to get randomness
r
. The second PRF takes as input
\{0,1\}^{d}
, samples
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}}\leftarrow\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\kappa},0,1^{w})
, applies
\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}},\cdot)
to the input (padded with
\kappa-d
zeros), and then applies
\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\prime},\cdot)
to get randomness
r^{\prime}
.

We note that
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}}
produces an associated injective function with probability
\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\kappa)=1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
, so we assume this is the case in the following analysis.

The first function is a puncturable PRF by definition. It is not hard to see that the second function is indeed a puncturable PRF: the key is simply
(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}},k_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\prime})
, and evaluation can be done by padding with zeros and successively applying
\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}},\cdot)
and
\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\prime},\cdot)
. Puncturing is also relatively simple: for a set
S
, one evaluates the set
S
on
\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}},\cdot)
to get a set of images
S^{\prime}
before puncturing
k_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\prime}\{S^{\prime}\}\leftarrow\mathsf{Punc}(k_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\prime},S^{\prime})
. Correctness follows from the injectivity of
\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}
and the correctness of
\mathsf{F}
, while puncturing security follows from the injectivity of
\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}
and the puncturing security of
\mathsf{F}
on inputs of length
m
.

If we now define distributions
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{0}=\mathcal{D}_{1}
which take as input
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
and randomness
r
and construct a coset
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
, it is easy to see that in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
, the circuit
E_{0}(\mathbf{y}):=\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{y};\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}},\mathbf{y}))
is used black-box by
P
,
P^{-1}
, and
D
(and all three circuits are obfuscated).

In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, we will instead use the circuit
E_{1}(\mathbf{y}):=\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{y};\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\prime},\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}},\mathbf{y})))
, where
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}}
and
k_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\prime}
are freshly sampled. It follows by Lemma
A.1
that the output of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
are
(f(\kappa)-\mathsf{poly}(\kappa),O(|\mathcal{X}|/f(\kappa)))
-computationally indistinguishable, where
\mathcal{X}
is the set of all possible values of
\mathbf{y}
, which has size
2^{r}
. We incur some loss due to assuming
\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}
is inejctive, and thus it follows by Assumptions (1) and (3) that the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in this hybrid is

\varepsilon_{1}\geq\varepsilon-O\left(\frac{2^{r}}{f(\kappa)}\right)-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\geq\frac{31\varepsilon}{32}.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: Switching to a small number of cosets.

E_{1}
now samples a lossy key
\mathsf{pk}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{LF}}\leftarrow\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\kappa},1,1^{w})
instead of an injective key
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}}\leftarrow\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\kappa},0,1^{w})
. By the security of the lossy function, the output of this hybrid is
(f(\kappa),\frac{1}{f(\kappa)})
-computationally indistinguishable from the previous hybrid. Thus, Assumption (1) implies that the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in this hybrid is

\varepsilon_{2}\geq\varepsilon_{1}-\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}\geq\frac{15\varepsilon}{16}.

We will refer to the image of the lossy function
\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{LF}},\cdot)
by
\mathcal{X}
. Note that in this hybrid, the coset
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}
is also an efficiently computable function of
\mathbf{x}=\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{LF}},\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{X}
(and is the same for all
\mathbf{y}
’s which map to the same
\mathbf{x}
), so we will refer to these cosets by
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{x}}
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
: Using obfuscated circuits inside
D
.

Recall that in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
,
D(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
computes
\mathbf{x}
before applying
\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}},\mathbf{x})
to get pseudorandomness
r_{\mathbf{x}}
which is used to generate a coset
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{x}})
;
D(j,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
then checks membership in the dual of
\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}}^{[j:n-r]}):=S_{\mathbf{x},j}
.

In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
, we additionally sample another puncturable PRF key
k_{S}
. After computing
\mathbf{x}
, we apply
\mathsf{F}(k_{S},\mathbf{x})
to obtain additional pseudorandomness
r^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}
; using
r^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}
, we generate obfuscations
\mathsf{O}_{S_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},C_{S_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}})
of the membership circuits for
S_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}
.
D
now uses
\mathsf{O}_{S_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}}
instead of
C_{S_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}}
to decide dual membership. By the correctness of the inner obfuscation scheme,
D
remains functionally equivalent, so the two hybrids are
(f(\kappa),\frac{\ell}{f(\kappa)})
-computationally close. Thus, by Assumption (1), the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in this hybrid is

\varepsilon_{3}\geq\varepsilon_{2}-\frac{\ell}{f(\kappa)}\geq\frac{29\varepsilon}{32}.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
: Relaxing dual verification inside
D
.

Once again, we would like to apply Lemma
A.1
, so we now describe the two ensembles of distributions we want to use; we will use one puncturable PRF
\mathsf{F}
. The first distribution
\mathcal{D}_{S,k_{\mathsf{lin}}}
takes as input
\mathbf{x}
and randomness
r
and computes
S_{\mathbf{x}}
from
\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}},\mathbf{x})
before using
r
to generate obfuscations
\mathsf{O}_{S_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},C_{S_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}})
. The second distribution
\mathcal{D}_{T,k_{\mathsf{lin}}}
takes as input
\mathbf{x}
and randomness
r
and computes
S_{\mathbf{x}}
from
\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}},\mathbf{x})
before using
r
to sample superspaces
T_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}
(with
s
more dimensions) of
S_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}
and generate obfuscations
\mathsf{O}_{T_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},C_{T_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}})
.

Specifically, the first part of
r
will be used to generate a random full column-rank matrix
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times(n-r-\ell)}
and a random matrix
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r-\ell)\times\ell}
. This defines matrices
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{x}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}&\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-r)\times(n-r)}
and
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{x}}:=\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)}
. We can now define

T_{\mathbf{x},j}^{\perp}:=\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{[j:\ell]}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{\perp}.

By Lemmas
D.2
and
D.6
, the distributions
\mathcal{D}_{S,k_{\mathsf{lin}}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{T,k_{\mathsf{lin}}}
are
(f(n-r-s-\ell)-\mathsf{poly}(n),\mathsf{poly}(n)/f(n-r-s-\ell))
-computationally indistinguishable (even though
S_{\mathbf{x},j}
are known).

Since
|\mathcal{X}|\leq 2^{w}
with probability
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
, in what follows, we assume this is the case. Consider the two circuits
E_{S,k_{\mathsf{lin}}}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathcal{D}_{S,k_{\mathsf{lin}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathsf{F}(k_{S},\mathbf{x}))
and
E_{T,k_{\mathsf{lin}}}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathcal{D}_{T,k_{\mathsf{lin}}}(\mathbf{x},\mathsf{F}(k_{T},\mathbf{x}))
for uniformly sampled keys
k_{S},k_{T}
. Observe that
E_{S,k_{\mathsf{lin}}}
is used black-box in
D
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
, and
D
will be obfuscated. Thus, if we switch to
E_{T,k_{\mathsf{lin}}}
, by Lemma
A.1
, this is
(f(n-r-s-\ell)-\mathsf{poly}(n),2^{w}\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)/f(n-r-s-\ell))
-computationally indistinguishable. We conclude that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
are
(f(n-r-s-\ell)-\mathsf{poly}(n),1/f(\kappa)+2^{w}\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)/f(n-r-s-\ell))
-computationally indistinguishable.

Therefore, after incurring some loss from the assumption that
|\mathcal{X}|\leq 2^{w}
, we have that by Assumptions (1) and (3), the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in this hybrid is

\varepsilon_{4}\geq\varepsilon_{3}-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)-\frac{2^{w}\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)}{f(n-r-s-\ell)}\geq\frac{7\varepsilon}{8}.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
: Changing the success predicate.

Recall that in
\mathsf{Hyb_{4}}
, we say
\mathcal{A}
is successful if it finds a collision, i.e. it produces a pair
x_{0}\neq x_{1}
such that
y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y}
. We now say that
\mathcal{A}
is successful only if
y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y}

and

\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1}\notin T_{\mathbf{x},1}
. As
|\mathcal{X}|\leq 2^{w}
with probability
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
, in what follows, we assume this is the case.

For every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, let
\varepsilon_{4}^{(\mathbf{x})}
be the probability that
\mathcal{A}
finds
x_{0}\neq x_{1}
such that
y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{x}=\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}},\mathbf{y})
; by definition,
\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\varepsilon_{4}^{(\mathbf{x})}=\varepsilon_{4}
. Observe that when a collision occurs, we have
\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\ColSpan(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}})\setminus\{0\}=S_{\mathbf{x},1}\setminus\{0\}
. Let
\delta^{(\mathbf{x})}
be the probability that
\mathcal{A}
finds
x_{0},x_{1}
such that
y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y}
,
\mathbf{x}=\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}},\mathbf{y})
, and
\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1}\in T_{\mathbf{x},1}\setminus\{0\}
.

Define
\varepsilon_{5}^{(\mathbf{x})}
to be the probability that
\mathcal{A}
finds
x_{0},x_{1}
such that
y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y}
,
\mathbf{x}=\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}},\mathbf{y})
, and
\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1}\notin T_{\mathbf{x},1}
. Let
L
be the subset of
\mathcal{X}
such that
\varepsilon_{4}^{(\mathbf{x})}\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{4}}{2|\mathcal{X}|}
; this means that
\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in L}\varepsilon_{4}^{(\mathbf{x})}\geq\varepsilon_{4}/2
. Our goal is to show that for every
\mathbf{x}\in L
,
\varepsilon_{5}^{(\mathbf{x})}\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{4}^{(\mathbf{x})}}{16n^{2}}
. By definition, we have that
\varepsilon_{5}^{(\mathbf{x})}\geq\varepsilon_{4}^{(\mathbf{x})}-\delta^{(\mathbf{x})}
, so if
\delta^{(\mathbf{x})}\leq\varepsilon_{4}^{(\mathbf{x})}/2
, then we are done. Thus, we assume that

\delta^{(\mathbf{x})}>\frac{\varepsilon_{4}^{(\mathbf{x})}}{2}\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{4}}{4|\mathcal{X}|}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{w}}\right).

Let
t:=n-r-s\leq n-1
and
m:=\frac{n(t+1)}{\delta^{(\mathbf{x})}}\leq O\left(\frac{n^{2}\cdot 2^{w}}{\varepsilon}\right)
. By Assumption (2), we have that

\frac{t}{\delta^{(\mathbf{x})}}\cdot\left(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell}\right)=O\left(\frac{2^{w}\cdot t}{\varepsilon}\right)\cdot\left(2^{t-s}+2^{-t+\ell}\right)\leq o(1),

and by Assumption (3),

\frac{m\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)\cdot T_{\mathcal{A}}}{f(n-r-s-\ell)}=\frac{2^{w}\cdot\mathsf{poly}(n)\cdot T_{\mathcal{A}}/\varepsilon}{f(n-r-s-\ell)}\leq o(1),

so the conditions of Lemma
D.9
are fulfilled. Thus, it follows from Lemma
D.9
and the security of the iO and puncturable PRF that for
\mathbf{x}\in L
,

\varepsilon_{5}^{(\mathbf{x})}\geq\frac{\delta^{(\mathbf{x})}}{16n(t+1)}-O\left(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}\right)\geq\frac{\delta^{(\mathbf{x})}}{16n^{2}}-O\left(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}\right)\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{4}^{(\mathbf{x})}}{32n^{2}}-O\left(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}\right).

Therefore, after incurring some loss from the assumption that
|\mathcal{X}|\leq 2^{w}
, we have that by Assumption (3), the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in this hybrid is

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{5}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\varepsilon_{5}^{(\mathbf{x})}-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\geq\left(\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in L}\varepsilon_{5}^{(\mathbf{x})}\right)-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in L}\left[\frac{\varepsilon_{4}^{(\mathbf{x})}}{32n^{2}}-O\left(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}\right)\right]-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{4}/2}{32n^{2}}-O\left(\frac{|\mathcal{X}|}{f(\kappa)}\right)-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{7\varepsilon}{512n^{2}}-O\left(\frac{2^{w}}{f(\kappa)}\right)-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\geq\frac{3\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
: Using the security of the P-PRP.

Sample all components except
k_{\mathsf{in}}
for the initial permutation, thus defining subspaces
S_{\mathbf{x},\ell+1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{\mathbf{x},1}
and
T_{\mathbf{x},\ell+1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq T_{\mathbf{x},1}
.

Now consider the following permutation
\Gamma
over
\{0,1\}^{n}
. Denote by
h_{0}
the first
r
bits and
h_{1}
the last
n-r
bits of the input to
\Gamma
. Recall that in
P
,
P^{-1}
, and
D
, the value of
\mathbf{x}
(and more specifically the matrices
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}
which are used to compute
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{x}}
) can be written as an efficient function of
H(x)\in\{0,1\}^{r}
. Thus, we define
\Gamma
as the (efficient) permutation which takes
h_{0}
, computes
\mathbf{M}_{h_{0}}
,
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{h_{0}}
, and
\mathbf{C}_{h_{0}}
, interprets
h_{1}
as a vector in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r}
, and multiplies
h_{1}
by
\mathbf{C}_{h_{0}}
. Note that this means we will now use the PRF key
k_{T}
in
P
and
P^{-1}
as well, because it is needed to compute
\mathbf{M}_{h_{0}}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{h_{0}}
and hence to compute
\Gamma
.

For every value
h_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, multiplying by
\mathbf{C}_{h_{0}}
is an affine transformation, which is
(2^{n-r}\cdot(n-r)^{2},\mathsf{poly}(n-r))
-decomposable by Lemma
A.3
. Thus,
\Gamma
applies a controlled decomposable permutation and is itself a
(2^{r}\cdot 2^{n-r}\cdot(n-r)^{2},\mathsf{poly}(n-r)+\mathsf{poly}\log(2^n))=(2^{n}\cdot n^{2},\mathsf{poly}(n))=(2^{\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)},\mathsf{poly}(\lambda))=(2^{\mathsf{poly}(\kappa)},\mathsf{poly}(\kappa))
-decomposable permutation by Lemma
A.3
.

Thus, if we switch to applying
\Gamma
to the output of
\Pi
(and
\Gamma^{-1}
to the input of
\Pi^{-1}
), since the circuits
P
and
P^{-1}
which use
\Pi
and
\Pi^{-1}
are obfuscated, this change is
(f(\kappa),O(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}))
-computationally indistinguishable by Lemma
A.5
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
: Using the security of outside iO.

For every
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r}
, we make the following change to
P
,
P^{-1}
, and
D
. Instead of composing the permutation
\Gamma
to the output of
\Pi
(by using
k_{\mathsf{in}}^{\Gamma,1}
), we switch to using just
k_{\mathsf{in}}
which applies
\Pi
as-is. However, after generating
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{x}})
, we will compute
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{x}}
,
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}
, and
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{x}}
to get
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{x}}:=\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{x}}
.
P
and
P^{-1}
will now use
(\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{x}})
instead of
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{x}})
. Additionally, in
D
, instead of obfuscating the membership check circuits for
T_{\mathbf{x},i}^{\perp}
, we go back to using
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{x}}
to directly check for membership.

Since
P
,
P^{-1}
, and
D
remain functionally equivalent, by the security of the outer iO scheme that obfuscates
P
,
P^{-1}
, and
D
, this change is
(f(\kappa),O(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}))
-computationally indistinguishable from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
. Thus, by Assumption (1), the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in this hybrid is

\varepsilon_{7}\geq\varepsilon_{6}-O\left(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}\right)\geq\varepsilon_{5}-O\left(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}\right)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{128n^{2}}.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
: Using the security of the puncturable PRF.

Recall the process in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
for generating our cosets in all three circuits
P
,
P^{-1}
, and
D
: given
\mathbf{y}
we compute
\mathbf{x}
with the lossy function, and then apply a puncturable PRF with two i.i.d keys,
k_{\mathsf{lin}}
and
k_{T}
to obtain
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{x}})
and the matrix
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{x}}
. We then compute
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{x}}:=\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{x}}
and use
(\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{x}})
in all circuits. Our goal is to apply Lemma
A.1
, so we will describe two distributions and two puncturable PRFs.

The first distribution
\mathcal{D}_{2}
takes as input
\mathbf{x}
and randomness
(r_{1},r_{2})
, uses
r_{1}
to generate
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{x}})
and
r_{2}
to generate
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{x}}
, computes
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{x}}:=\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{x}}
, and outputs
(\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{x}})
. The second distribution
\mathcal{D}_{3}
takes as input
\mathbf{x}
and randomness
r_{1}
and uses
r_{1}
to generate
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{x}})
. By an identical argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.4
, it is easy to see that
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}_{3}
are statistically equivalent.

Consider the function
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}
which takes as input
\mathbf{x}
and outputs
(r_{1},r_{2}):=(\mathsf{F}(k_{1},\mathbf{x}),\mathsf{F}(k_{2},\mathbf{x}))
for randomly sampled keys
k_{1},k_{2}
. We claim that
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}
is a puncturable PRF whose keys are simply a pair of keys for
\mathsf{F}
. Puncturing
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}
is simple:
\mathsf{Punc}^{\prime}(k=(k_{1},k_{2}),S)=(\mathsf{Punc}(k_{1},S),\mathsf{Punc}(k_{2},S))
. The correctness of
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}
follows directly from the correctness of
\mathsf{F}
, and it is not hard to see that
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}
has
(f(\kappa)-\mathsf{poly}(\kappa),O(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}))
-puncturing security by the security of
\mathsf{F}
.

As
|\mathcal{X}|\leq 2^{w}
with probability
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
, in what follows, we assume this is the case. We now define the circuits
E_{2}(\mathbf{x})=\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathbf{x};\mathsf{F}^{\prime}((k_{\mathsf{lin}},k_{T}),\mathbf{x}))
and
E_{3}(\mathbf{x})=\mathcal{D}_{3}(\mathbf{x};\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}))
for freshly sampled keys
k_{\mathsf{lin}},k_{T},k_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\prime}
. Observing that all three circuits in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
use
E_{2}
black-box, we see that Lemma
A.1
implies that switching to
E_{3}
in all three circuits is
(f(\kappa)-\mathsf{poly}(\kappa),O(\frac{2^{w}}{f(\kappa)}))
-computationally indistinguishable.

Therefore, after incurring some loss from the assumption that
|\mathcal{X}|\leq 2^{w}
, we have that by Assumptions (1) and (3), the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
is

\varepsilon_{8}\geq\varepsilon_{7}-O\left(\frac{2^{w}}{f(\kappa)}\right)-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{128n^{2}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{512n^{2}}=\frac{3\varepsilon}{512n^{2}}.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{9}
: Switching back to injective mode of the lossy function.

We make the same change from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, but in the other direction. Recall that in the previous hybrid
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
, on input
\mathbf{y}
, we compute
\mathbf{x}\leftarrow\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}},\mathbf{y})
and use
\mathbf{x}
to compute all other components. We now sample an injective key
\mathsf{pk}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{LF}}\leftarrow\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\kappa},0,1^{w})
instead of the lossy key
\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{LF}}
. By the security of the lossy function, the output of this hybrid is
(f(\kappa),\frac{1}{f(\kappa)})
-computationally indistinguishable from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
. Thus, Assumption (1) implies that the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in this hybrid is

\varepsilon_{9}\geq\varepsilon_{8}-\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{256n^{2}}.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{10}
: Stopping use of the lossy function.

We make the same change from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, but in the other direction. Instead of applying
\mathsf{LF}.\mathsf{F}
to
\mathbf{y}||0^{\kappa-d}
to get
\mathbf{x}
and then applying
\mathsf{F}
to
\mathbf{x}
to get randomness, we directly apply
\mathsf{F}
(with a different key) to
\mathbf{y}
to get our randomness. By an identical argument as before, this hybrid is
(f(\kappa)-\mathsf{poly}(\kappa),O(2^{r}/f(\kappa)))
-computationally indistinguishable from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{9}
, so by Assumptions (1) and (3), the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in this hybrid is

\varepsilon_{10}\geq\varepsilon_{9}-O\left(\frac{2^{r}}{f(\kappa)}\right)-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{512n^{2}}.

We conclude by noting that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{10}
is exactly the distribution
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda},n,r,\ell,s)
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

Given
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}
from
(\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1},\overline{\mathcal{D}})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{Setup}}(1^{\lambda},r+s,r,0,0)
and the code of
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
acts as follows:

•

Sample a puncturable PRF key
k_{\mathsf{F}}
that outputs a sufficient (polynomial in
\kappa
) number of bits and a P-PRP key
k_{\Gamma}
for a P-PRP on
\{0,1\}^{n}
. On input
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
, use the pseudorandomness of
\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{F}},\mathbf{y})
to generate a vector
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
, matrix
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times\ell}
, and full-rank matrix
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell)\times(n-\ell)}
. Denote by
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}
the full-rank matrix
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times n}
; we write as shorthand
(\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}})\leftarrow\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{F}},\mathbf{y})
to refer to this process. This defines the following circuits:

•

(\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{d},\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})\leftarrow P(x\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})
:

–

(\overline{x}\in\{0,1\}^{r+s},\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-r-s-\ell},x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ell})\leftarrow\Pi(k_{\Gamma},x)
.

–

(\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{d},\overline{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r+s})\leftarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{x})
.

–

(\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times n},\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})\leftarrow\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{F}},\mathbf{y})
.

–

\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}x^{\prime}\\
\overline{\mathbf{u}}\\
\tilde{x}\end{pmatrix}+\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
.

•

(x\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}\cup\{\bot\})\leftarrow P^{-1}(\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{d},\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})
:

–

(\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}})\leftarrow\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{F}},\mathbf{y})
.

–

\begin{pmatrix}x^{\prime}\\
\overline{\mathbf{u}}\\
\tilde{x}\end{pmatrix}\leftarrow\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}^{-1}\cdot(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}})
.

–

(\overline{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{r+s}\cup\{\bot\})\leftarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}(\mathbf{y},\overline{\mathbf{u}})

–

Output
x\leftarrow\Pi^{-1}(k_{\Gamma},(\overline{x},\tilde{x},x^{\prime}))
if
\overline{x}\neq\bot
, else output
\bot
.

•

\{0,1\}\leftarrow D^{\prime}(j\in[\ell+1],\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{d},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})
:

–

(\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}})\leftarrow\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{F}},\mathbf{y})
.

–

Output 1 iff
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[j:\ell]}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}=0^{n-r-s-j+1}
.

•

Generate obfuscations
\mathcal{P}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},P)
,
\mathcal{P}^{-1}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},P^{-1})
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{iO}(1^{\kappa},1^{p},D^{\prime})
.

Finally,
\mathcal{B}
runs
(x_{0},x_{1})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
,
(\overline{x}_{b},\tilde{x}_{b},x^{\prime}_{b})\leftarrow\Pi(k_{\Gamma},x_{b})
and outputs
(\overline{x}_{0},\overline{x}_{1})
.

Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-s-\ell}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-s-\ell}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\ell}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}&\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-s-\ell}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times(n-r)},

where
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(r+s)\times s}
is the matrix arising from
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}
. As
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},2}
and
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}
have full column-rank, so does
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+1:n-r]}
; on the other hand,
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y},1}
remains a random matrix; thus,
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
has full column-rank. Now, since
(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})\notin\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[1:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)
, this means that if we look at each of the two coordinates vectors
\mathbf{w}_{0}
,
\mathbf{w}_{1}
(which each have
n-r
bits) of
\mathbf{u}_{0}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}
, they differ somewhere between the
(\ell+1)
’th and
(\ell+s)
’th bit. However,
(\mathbf{w}_{b})_{[\ell+1:\ell+s]}
arises from applying the permutation
\overline{\Pi}
on
\{0,1\}^{r+s}
(defined by
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1}
to
\overline{x}_{b}
) and taking the last
s
bits of the output. As
x_{0},x_{1}
have the same
\mathbf{y}
’s and thus so do
\overline{x}_{0}
and
\overline{x}_{1}
, it must be the case that
\overline{x}_{0}\neq\overline{x}_{1}
in order for
\mathbf{w}_{0}
to differ from
\mathbf{w}_{1}
in the appropriate indices.

If we define
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{B}}
as the probability that
\mathcal{A}
outputs
x_{0},x_{1}
such that
y_{0}=y_{1}:=\mathbf{y}
and
(\mathbf{u}_{0}-\mathbf{u}_{1})\notin\ColSpan\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[1:\ell]}&\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}^{[\ell+s+1:n-r]}\end{bmatrix}\right)
, we wish to lower bound the probability of
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{B}}
. We do this through a series of hybrids.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: The distribution
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathcal{B}(\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{P}^{-1}})
, as simulated by
\mathcal{B}
.

This is the original execution, and thus has success probability
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{B}}
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: Removing the inner obfuscation.

In this hybrid, we remove the obfuscations from
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}
and use the underlying plain circuits
\overline{P}
and
\overline{P}^{-1}
. By the correctness of the inner iO scheme, we have not changed the functionality of the final circuits
P
,
P^{-1}
,
D
, so the security of the outer iO scheme guarantees that this change is
(f(\kappa),O(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}))
-computationally indistinguishable.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: Removing the inner permutation
\overline{\Pi}_{\mathsf{in}}
by using the security of the P-PRP.

Let
\overline{\Pi}_{\mathsf{in}}
be the first P-PRP inside
\overline{P}
. In the previous hybrid, we apply the
n
-bit P-PRP
\Pi(k_{\Gamma},\cdot)
to
x
before applying the inner permutation
\overline{\Pi}_{\mathsf{in}}(\overline{k}_{\mathsf{in}},\cdot)
to the first
r+s
output bits of
\Pi(k_{\Gamma},x)
. The inner permutation can also be thought of as a permutation
\Gamma^{\prime}
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
which acts as the identity on the last
n-r-s
bits and applies
\overline{\Pi}_{\mathsf{in}}(\overline{k}_{\mathsf{in}},\cdot)
to the first
r+s
bits.

By Theorem
A.4
, the inner P-PRP
\overline{\Pi}_{\mathsf{in}}(\overline{k}_{\mathsf{in}},\cdot)
is
(2^{\mathsf{poly}(\kappa)},\mathsf{poly}(\kappa))
-decomposable. This implies that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is also
(2^{\mathsf{poly}(\kappa)},\mathsf{poly}(\kappa))
-decomposable.

Since the circuits
P
and
P^{-1}
which apply
\Pi
and
\Pi^{-1}
are obfuscated by the outer iO scheme, if we stop applying
\Gamma^{\prime}
to the output of
\Pi
(and
(\Gamma^{\prime})^{-1}
to the input of
\Pi
), this change is
(f(\kappa),O(\frac{1}{f(\kappa)}))
-computationally indistinguishable by Lemma
A.5
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
: Taking
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}
to be the direct output of the PRF
\mathsf{F}
.

In the previous hybrid, we computed
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}:=\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-s}\end{bmatrix}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}:=\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\overline{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{y}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}+\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
, where
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}},\overline{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{y}}
are defined by the output of the inner PRF
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{k_{\mathsf{lin}}},\mathbf{y})
and
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
are computed from the output of
\mathsf{F}(k_{\mathsf{F}},\mathbf{y})
.

Our goal will be to apply Lemma
A.1
, so we begin by describing two distributions and two puncturable PRFs.

The first distribution
\mathcal{D}_{0}
takes as input
\mathbf{y}
and randomness
(r_{1},r_{2})
, uses
r_{1}
to generate
(\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}},\overline{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{y}})
and
r_{2}
to generate
(\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}})
, computes
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}}:=\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{y}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{n-r-s}\end{bmatrix}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}:=\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{y}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\overline{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathbf{y}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}+\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{y}}
, and outputs
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
. The second distribution
\mathcal{D}_{1}
takes as input
\mathbf{y}
and randomness
r_{1}
and uses
r_{1}
to generate
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{y}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}})
.
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}_{3}
